Sm: As requested in your letter of the 5th instant, I transmit herewith the information compiled under the
supervision of Mr. R . .A.. Bayley, of this office, concerning the old loans of the government and other matters

JJon. WM. WINDOM,
Secretary of the TreasunJ.

Sm: I have the honor to transmit herewith, for such disposition as you may deem proper, the manuscript notes
prepared by me relative to the national loans of the United States from July 4, 1776, to June 30, 1880, showing
the issues and redemptions of the several loans for each year, together with a brief historical resume of the causes
which led to their negotiation. I take great pleasure in acknowledging my indebtedness to Hon. J, K. Upton,
assistant secretary of the treasury, Ron. James Gilfillan, treasurer of the United States, J. T. Power, esg., chief
clerk, Mr. W. F. MacLennan, chief of the division of warrants, estimates, and appropriations, and James R. Saville,
esq., formerly chief clerk of the Treasury Department, for encouragement and valnable assistance. The late
David S. Green was m.y fellow-worker in the laborious searchings through documents, old and new, bringing to.
the work great intelligence, zeal, and industry! I trust that the information furnished in these sheets may be
promotive of a more general knowledge of our :fiscal history, and more especially of the early :financial struggleSof our government, and that, as a work of reference, the compilation may be useful to many persons in official life.
The fact that this work ends with the census year 1880, explains the absence of any reference to the :financiaill
operations of the government for the current year. These include a notable reduction of the annual interest chargeon about six hundred millions of the bonded debt; a reduction effected practically without expense to the
government or :financial disturbance of any kind. The annual saving thus brought about is but one of the great
results effected, and the transaction, taken as a whole; renders memorable the present administration of the
Treasury Department.
· The responsibility for any errors that may exist in this work must rest entirely upon me, but I think it proper
to sa.y that the statements have been most carefully verified, and it is believed that strict reliance may be placed
upon them.
Very respectfully, your obedient servant,
RAFAEL .A.. BAYLEY.

THE NATIONAL I.JOANS OF THE UNITED STATES FROM
JULY 4, 1776, TO JUNE 30, 1880.
SECTION !.-HISTORICAL.
FOREIGN SUBSIDIES.
The opening of the Revolutionary War excited deep ~nterest in Europe, and especially in France which power
'Once the possessor of two-thirds of North America, had been humbled by the seven years' warfure that closed with
the treaty of Fontainebleau, uncler which France had been forcecl to surrender to Great Britain all her American
.possessions except a few unimportant islands. The French watched with interest the course of events that
threatened in turn to strip their hereditary enemy of both her old and new possessions in America, and to bui1d up
·on this side of the Atlantic a new power. The contest claimed the particular attention of the Oornte de Vergennes,
the French minister of exterior relations, who, though unwilling at first to conclude an armed alliance with the
·colonies, determined to assist them witll money and munitions of war. The treaty followed, but the military
-snpplies aud money furnished early in the contest were of the utmost importance. These supplies were not
furnished openly, because France was not in a position to commence war with Great Britain. Accordingly the
·celebrated Oaron de Beaumarchais was employed as a secret agent. He was a br:i:lliant French writer and courtier,
'<'1 man of great vivacity and energy, but apparently with limited knowledge of mercantile affairs. As much
:sym11athy has been expended on the memory of Beaumarchais; and his fate has been referred to as an illustration
'()f the ingratitude of republics, an attempt will be made to bring to light, from the documents on record and from
•the works of his biographer and contemporary authorities, the facts in the case, with a view of showing the justice
•or injustice of the settlements between Beaumarchais and the United States. This question once divided Oongress,
aml was the cause of much bitter feeling. It can now, however, be discussed, by the aid of documents then
inaccessible, without prejudice. The charge made against the United Stn,tes was a serious one, and involved the
\receiving of millions of dollars worth of supplies under a regular contract during the darkest hours of the Revolution,
:arnl then allowing the person furnishing these supplies to pass his last days in prison for the non-payment of the
debt thus incurred.
Beaumarchais, at the commencement of the Revolution, had taken up the cause of the Americans with all the
ardOl· of his nature, and rendered most important services in bringing the hesitating French ministry to adopt a
decisive conri,e of action. His services in this particufar were greater, probably, than those of all our agents iu
Europe at the time. .A.s early as September, 1775, he addressed a memorial to the king of France, in which he
plead the cause Of America with all the vigor of his pen. He followed this up with many others, addressed either
to the king· or the Oomte de Vergennes. The gist of his arguments was that it would be impossible for Great Britain
to subdue the colonies if they were assisted in their struggle; that if not assisted they might succumb, join the
English, and turn their arms against France. He says: "We are not yet in a fit state for making war; we must
prepare ourselves, keep up the contest, and with that view send secret assistance, in a prudent manner, to the
Americans."· These memorials seem to have decided the Oomte de Vergennes to give the assistance, and to
employ, as his agent, their author. Beaumarchais was sent to London in the spring of 1776 to make the necessary
arrangements, there being then no American agent in France. In London he was introduced to Arthur Lee, who
was at the time studying law in the Temple, but who bore some kind of commission from the secret committee of
Oongress. Beaumarchais informed him that the French government wished to send 200,000 lonis-d'0r (equal to
$816,750), in arms, ammunition, and specie, for the assistance of tb.e Americans, but in a secret manner, and that
all they wanted was to know through what source it was best to make the remittances. He requested Mr. Lee to
give the earliest intelligence of' this to Oongress, and to request that a small qtrnntity of tobacco or some other
jprouuction of America might be returned, to give it the air of a mercantile transaction.*
On Beaumarchais' return to Paris he made the acquaintance of Silas Deane, who arrived there about the first
of July, 1776, with full powers as political and commercial agent for the United States in France. His instruct:i.ons

I find Beaumarchais, as I before hinted, possesses the entire confidence of the ministry; he i? a man of wit and geni.ns, and a;.

The whole tenor of Deane's letter t shows that while he supposed the supplies were to be paid for as well as.
received through Beaumarchais, yet that, if a debt, it was a debt due to the government of France. On the part of:
Beaumarchais, he was not only aware that the French ministry had determined to assist the Americans, but when
he signed the contract he had in his possession a million of liYres drawn from the French treasury for the i)lll'poseof furnishing this assistance, and a few days afterward he received from the Spanish treasury, through the Comte
de 'Vergennes, another million for the same purp'ose. As shipments went on and remittances came but slowly from
.America, he received a third million from the king of France. As the whole controversy between the United
States and Beaumarchais turns upon the fact of these advances for the benefit of the colonies through him, it is
necessary to show the evidence on this point.·
On the 2d of May, 1776, the Comte de 'Vergennes submitted the subject to Louis XVI in the following note:+·
I have the honor of submitting to your majesty the paper which is to authorize me to fu1·nish a million of lfores for tho English colonies,.
if yon should deign to ratify it with ypur signature. I add, too, sire, the· draft of the reply which I mean to malrn to M. Bemuun.rclmie.
if your majesty shall approve it; I beg it may be returned to me without delay. It shall not go forth in my lin.ndw1•iti11g 11or in t;hat oJl"
any of my clerks or secretaries. I will employ that of my son,.whi.ch cannot be known, a.nd 1 although he is in his :fifteenth year, l eMll
answer positively for his discretion. .M it is of importance that this operation should "!:lot be deteotoa, or n.t least not imamted to tho
government, I propose, if your majesty consents, to call hither the Sieur Montn.udin; the ostensible motive will be to ask an account of hie
correspondence with the Americans, and the real one to charge him with the transmission of the f1mils, which your majesty is plen.aetl to
grant them, directing at the same time all the precautions to be taken as if he advanced thefunils on Ms own aocaunt,
On thiB head also I take the liberty of requesting the orders of your majesty. That being done, I will write to tbe Marq11is Grimald
(secretary of foreign affairs in Spain). I will inform him of our operations and propose to him [ile la doub!e1'] to do the same.

The king immediately gave his minister the authority he askecl for, and M. de Rarvelay, keeper of the royal
treasury, was directed to hold the million of livres subject to the particular order of 'Vergennes. ':rlw Sieur
Montaudin was, however, not intrusted with the transmission of the funds, Beaumarchais being employed for that
purpose, and the million was paid over to him. For it he gave the receipt of which the following is a trauslati6D
of the original in the archives of France :§
I have received from Monsieur Duvergier, n.greeably to the orders of the Comte de Vergennes dated the 5th instant which I Imel

that a million ~as advanced on the 10th of June, 1776, before the .Americans ha~l any authorized agent in France~

SlR: D~~ng the last war France furnished several sums of money to the United States of America, both under the head of lonns and

If more conclusive proof iti needed that this million of livres was not only advancecl to the United Colonies
tbrough Beaumarchais, but that it was intended as a gratuity to the colonies, it is to be found in the letter of M. Dnrival,
one of the French ministry, and Ohef dii biweau des fonds des affaires etrangeres. Benjamin Franklin, minister of
the Unitecl States to France in 1785, discovered that a million of livres had been advanced from the royal treasury,
which did not appear in the accounts of M. Grand, the banker of the United States in Paris. He determined to
have the matter explained if possible, so that, as he says, "it may stand clear before I die, lest some enemy should
afterward accuse me of having received a million not accounted for." After his return to .America in 1786 he
opened a correspondence with the secretary of Congress on the subject, and finally wrote to M. Grand requesting
him to make inquiry into the matter at the French treasury. The result of his inquiries was the following 1etter:t
M. Durival to M. Grand.
[Translation.]

VERSAILLES, .August 30, 1786.

Sm: I have received tho letter which you dicl me the honor to write on the 28th of this month touching the advance of a million,
which you say was made by the farmers-general to the United Stat!ls of America, the 3d of June, 1777. I have no knowledge of that
11dvance. Who.t I have verified is, that the king, by the contract of the 25th of February, 1783, has confirmed the gratuitous gift which
bis majesty h11d previously made of the three millions hereafter mentioned, viz: one million delivered by the royal treasuriJ the 10th, of June,
1776 ancl two other millions advanced also by the royal treasury in 17771 on four receipts of the deputies of Congress, of the 17th of
1
January, 3d of April, 10th of June, and 15th of Octob!lr1 of the same year. 'This explanation will, sir, I hope, resolve your doubt touching
the advanee of the 3d of June, 1777. I further recommend to you, sir, to confer on this subject with M. Gojard, who ought to be better
informed than we, who had no knowledge of any aclvanees but those made by the royal treasury.
I have the honor to be, &c. 1
DURIVAL.

This would appear to settle the question as to the advance of the first million. Against all this evidence there
can be adduced only a resolve of Congress passed in 1779, denying that any present of supplies previous to the
treaty of alliance had been receivecl from France, and the denial of M. cle,Talleyrand in 1816, when pressing the
claim of the heirs of Beaumarchais. The resolution of Congress was passed under the pressure of a demand
made by Gerard the French embassador, after the publication of a pamphlet by Thomas Paine, secretary of the,
congressional c~mmittee on foreign affairs, in which, from official documents in his possession, he had, withou1l
authority, made such extracts as went far to reveal this dangerous state secret.

express declarations of Vergennes, Durival, and others, the actors in this affair.

No evidence as to this second million was accessible to the United States authorities when they settled Beaumarchais' accounts. The versatile author of the "Barber of Seville" was now set. up as a merchant, an entirely new
walk in life for him, with a capital of two millions of livres (about $363,000), advanced from the royal treasuries of·
France and Spain to supply the United Colonies with the means of carrying on the contest. It is probable thu,t he,
had rece1ved orders from the French ministry to invest it in material supplies, instead of handing over the specie.
The cannon, ammunition and muskets were taken directly from the l!'rench arsenals. It is said that he wn.s torestore these in kind, antl this may be the fact. The only evidence on the point is a letter from the French minister
of war notifying him that when he returns the powder it will have to be subjected to the nsual tests. '£his might
apply either to the powder sent to the Americans or to that required for the private navy which Beaumarcllais fitted'
out, as he says, "to cruise across the ocean, to convoy, attack, burn, or take private yessels." One of his vessels,.
the Fier Roderigue, a three-decker, carrying sixty guns, while convoying ten merchant vessels, was stopped a.nd
orclered into line of battle by .Admiral de Estaing, who was then lying off the island of Grenada in commm1c1 of' the·
French fleet, and preparing to fight the English fleet commanded by Admiral Byron. The Fier Roclerigue came
out of the action covered with glory, but the captain and a large number of the crew were killed ancl the vessel
riddled with balls. Byron was forced to retreat.
The cloth for uniforms was purchased in market, as it is hardly possible that the French government hall in·
store clothing for soldiers to suit the fantastic ideas of Beaumarchais, and if it had possessed it, to clothe Americitll"
soldiers in French uniforms would have been equivalent to a declaration of war with Great Britain. The secret
committee of Congress had ordered that the uniforms to be purchased should be blue with fn,cings of di:lforent
colors for the different arms of the service, but Booumarchais very coolly changell all this. .As a Wl'iter of operas,
he had perhaps derived his ideas of martial equipments from those of the mimic soldiers of the stage. He wrQte to·
the committee, December 1, 1776, that he should ship uniforms for six divisions of five thousantl men each, one
division to be blue, the others respectively brown, green, red, gra~r, and light blue.
History has not recorded the color of the uniform in which onr Revolutionary forefathers won 01· lost tholr
battles, but as Beaumarchais actually shipp~d a large part of the clothing, it is probable that some divisions of the
army really were attired in this parti-colored fashion. In these shipments his native energy came out in all its.
force. Despite the scarcity of vessels and seamen, the fact that the ocean swarmed with British cruisers rea<ly to
intercept all supplies for America, the strict blockade of the American coast, and the energetic remonstrances 0£ ·
Lord Stormont, the British embassador, he succeeded in loading and dispatching a large amount of supplies, cannon,"
ammunition, and arms, most of which reached their destination.
Lomenie, the author of the entertaining work" Beaumarchais and his Times", says he had forty ships at sea, and
that the first cargo of supplies ·sent to the .Americans was valued at three million livres, but in this there is mtlch...
exaggeration. If he had forty ships at sea they were not engaged fa supplying the United Colonies. The tota.I.
nntnber of vessels sent was eight, the Amphitrite, Mercure, Mere Bobie, Flamand, Mary Oatberine Seine Amelia
a~d. Therese. The first shipment was by the Amphitrite, and her cargo was valued (as shown b; Beau:narcbais; ·
ongmal account-current) at 979,493 livres, 8 sols, 3 deniers. Four of these vessels arrived safely at Portsmouth New
Hampshire, two reached Martinique, where their cargoes were received by Mr. Bingham ao-ent for the Oonti~ental 1
Oo~gress, and dispatch.ed in small, swift-sailing vessels to Edenton, North Carolina, and tV:.o, the Amelia and Th6rese,.
arnved ~t cape Fran901s, where they were received by M. Carabosse, agent for Beaumarchais. The fate of these last·
cargoes IS uncertain; a portion at least was dispatched to the continent.
Lomenie in his wo~k asserts th~t hardly any return was made in produce for these shipments, mentioning only
three ~.all lots as having been received, and even for these, he says, Beaumarchais had a contest with the American-.
comnuas10nertl, Franklin, ~eane, and Lee, who wished to sell the cargoes and appr?priate the proceeds to other,
purposes. Now, the fact !s, as shown by the Beaumarchais manuscript, that ten vessels were dispatched with•

The shipments began October 1, 1777, when the Amphitrite was dispatched with a return cargo, and continued
until July, 1781, by the following vessels: Therese, Fier Uoderigue (two cargoes), Perouse, Deux Helenes, Bonhomme Richard, Polly, Amelia, and Mercure. These vessels were not all fully loaded, but their cargoes, after
deducting one-half for freight and large sums for commissions, netted 713,996 livres. It is a noticeable fact that
among the American produce sent was a large amount of indigo, then extensively cultivated at the South, but nolonger produced jn the United States, though the soil proved well adapted to it, and its quality wal'I excellent.
The returns from America were, however, very slowly made-too slowly to enable Beaumarchais to meet his
engagements. For this reason he applied in the year 1777 to Louis XVI, and received by installments another
million of livres. The proof of this is to be found in Lomenie's work referred to above. Lomenie often exaggeratesr
but he advocates most warmly the side of Beaumarchais, and wastes a great deal of special pleading to prove that
.the United States defrauded him. For this very reason he is a :first-cla.ss witness when stating facts that bear against
his client. He held in his possession Beaumarchais' papers and had access to the French archives, and the proof
was so incontestable th.at he could not avoid the conclusions. He says (vol. 3, page 224):
I have been obliged, nevertheless, contrary to tho very sincere opinion of tho heirs of Beaumarchais and to the declarations of the
different ministers since 17781 all based upon the first official declaration of M. cle Vergennes-I have been obliged to re-establish the truth
as to the fact of the celebratecl million which was incontestably given by tho government, not for a sec-retpolitioal service, 1mc0Mwotei110itk
tho Amerioan B11:1J,Plie.s, but for the supplfos tlwnlBelves. Finding, also, in tho archives of foreign affairs, the material proof that Beaumarchais,.
in<lepenclontly of tho first million, given June 10, 1776, roceivecl a seconrl from the court of Spain, August 11, 17761 and a third paid by
installments in tho course of 1777, I have been obliged to mention all these facts because they are true, and because the first duty of lt
writer, who respects himself, is not to conceal the truth.

Beaumarchais' letters written while shipping the supplies are both unique and interesting. He lectures the
Oontinental Congress with the freedom of a general officer speaking to a council of western Indians, advises the·
appointment of a dictator, urges the declaration of war against Portugal, as if war with Great Britain was not
enough, and wishes ex11editions fitted out against "the defenseless English factories in Africa", and the "galleons.
heavy with gol~l on the coast of Brazil". He says he has two thousand military officers ready to start for America,
and in a subsequent letter says he has sent thirty-four of them over. To these foreign officers he made large advances:
in specie. Some of them did good service in the armies of the Revolution; others, who came over with great
:pretensions, provecl worthless. Besides the shipments of produce, Congress paid in specie to M. de Francy, an agent
sent over by Beaumarchais in 1777, as admitted in his account-current, 55,000 livres; in June, 1780, by bills on Dr.
Franklin, 144,000 livres; in June, 1781, 144,000 more; and May 18, 1782, bills of exchange for 2,400,000 livres at three·
years from date, with interest added. These bills were all cashed at maturity.
Under the act of April 18, 1806 (2 Stat., 380), $78,886 26, equal to about 434,635 livres, was paid to Beaumarchais~
heirs; and under the convention with the king of the French of July 4, 1831, 800,000 francs, equal to 810,000 livres,
wer~ reserved and paid.*
An account fairly stated between the Unitecl States and Beaumarchais and his heirs, allowing t-0 Beaumarchais all.
his charges for shipments, freight, insurance, and advances to foreign officers, and to the United States the subsidies
and the payments admitted to have been made, will stand as follows. The account is stated in livres Tournois, as
nearly all the transactions were carried on in that currency. Fractions. of li'11:'es are thrown out. Five and a half
livres are nearly equal in value to one dolla.r.

out except on matters in which it is confirmed by others. The charges of interest by Beaumarchais have not been
adi.:iitted. If any interest was due it was to the United States, the three millions of subsidy having b.een advanced

Beaumarchais was financially ruined, it is true, but not by his dealings with the United Oolonies. Lomenic1
wl10was in possession of his private papers when he wrote, says that from October 1, 1776, until September 301 1783,
his accounts show that he received 21 1092,515 livres and disbursed 21,044,191 1 showing an excess of receipts over
disbursements of 48,324 livres. • But these seven years cover precisely the time of the American Revolution,
during which the shipments were made. The balance on the right side of the profit-and-loss account is smalli but
it does not look like financial ruin. The fact is that his embarrassments arose solely from his engaging, in the year
1779, in the republication of the works of Voltaire, one of the most extensive and most unfortunate literary uuuertakings on record. Voltaire's wor:Irs were prohibited in France, and the influence of the clergy was sufilcient to
prevent even Beaumarchais, though high in favor with the king, from republishing them there. He therefore
rented from the margrave of Baden, in Germany, an old castle at Kehl. Here he founded an enormous printing
-establishment, imported from England, notwithstanding the war, the best types that could be purchased, made the.
paper for the work, and carried the whole through, after years of labor, to its final completion.
Voltaire's works comprised seventy octavo volumes, and of these he printed 15,000 copies, or 1,0501000 volumes.
It was a most unfavorable time for a great literary enterprise. Half the world was in arms, and in France church
and state were tottering on the verge of an abyss. When the revolution broke out but 2,000 sets of Voltaire
had been disposed of, leaving on hand 910,000 volumes. So vaRt were the quantities of books that he was ol>lig·ed
to store in Paris, in the Faubourg St. .Antoine, that he was subjected to frequent domiciliary visits by the mol> ltllll
the revolutionary authorities, who were persuaded that his storehouses contained either grain or arms. t To sell
the books was impossible, and they probably saw the light only in the shape of gun cartridges.
So much space has been devoted to the affairs of Beaumarchais that not much more can be given to an account
.of the other subsidies received. Two million livres more were granted by France in the year 1777, and. in 1781 six:
millions, making a total of ten millions, of which Beaumarchais received two, and the rest was paicl through
Benjamin Franklin. The French authorities insisted that so much of this money as was not expended in Europe
should be drawn for by General Washington in bills on M. de Harvelay, garde di~ tresor royal. Irmnklin
protested that it was not the usage in the United States to allow the General of the army to draw for moneys
ill.tended to be paid into the treasury, but the French minister would hear no explanations 011 this point mul
.assured Franklin that it was his majesty's order. t It is supposed, therefore, t;hough the fact has not been reco~dell
that the bills went to Paris signed by General Washington.

obligations of the Unitecl States which they held, and claimed their payment-the property of the victims, held to
be public enemies, haviug esche~Lted to the state.
When the time came for settling this loan, the transactions of the American commissioners in France were
involved in so much myster;r, that it was supposed by the treasury authorities that this advance of 1,000,000 livres
was included in the French subsidies, and even Franklin, though engaged in the transaction, was unable to explain
it. '.l.'he king of Fritnce had declarecl that he had giYen 3,000,000 li\·res to the Unite<l Colonies in 1776 and 1777,
but only 2,000,000 had reached M. Grand, banker of the United States, in Paris. Wllat had become of the other
million, was unknown; and it was at one time supposed that the advance from the farmers-general was the third
million. The comptroller wrote, Febrlmry 8, 17M, to M. Bournonville, secretary of the foreign legation, as follows:
After a cn.rllfnl examination of all the foreign accounts of the United States, it is certain that 110 more than 3,0001000 liyres, inclucling

The mystery wi1s not cleared up until Gouverneur Morris obtained, from the French archives, a copy of the

FH.ENCII LOAN OF EIGHTEEN MILLION LIVRES.
It is extremely difficult to obtain information respecting this loan. It is probable that it was, in its inception,
not so much a loan as a subsidy, a l)ttyment of 750,000 livres every three months to the American commissioners
in France, to enabl<.1 the colonies to keep up the nneqmtl strnggle with Great Britain. The money was advanced
without an expectation of repayment, thoug'h with a stipulation that it should be repaid. In 1782 an account was
taken of former payments not included in the 10,000,000 livres expressly given as a gratuity, and a formal contract
for the repayment was drawn up. These payments amounted to 15,000,000 livres, ftnd a further sum of 3,ooo,ooo
livres was added ancl paid to the United States, making a total of 18,000,000, which it was agreed should be repaid.
The contract will be found in Journals of Congress, vol. iv, Appendix, p. 20, and is dated July 16, 1782. It
enumerates the different sums advanced by the king of Fnmce to the United States "uncler the title of a loan, in
the years 1778, 1779, 1780, 1781, antl 1782", and provilles that, although in the receipts for said payments it is
promised that the money should be repaid on the 1st of January, 1788, with interest at 5 per cent. per annum, yet,
as the payment of so large a sum at one period might greatly injure the finances of the United States, it should be
made in twelve annual payments of 1,i:;oo,000 livres each, to commence from the third year after the conclusion of
peace. Article 3 declares th<tt "although the receipts of the minister of the Congress of the United States specify
that the 18,000,000 of livres Tournois are to be paid at the royal treasury with interest at 5 per cent. per annum,
llis majesty, being willing to give the United Sttttes a new proof of his affection ancl friendship, has been pleased
to make a present of, and to forgive, the whole arrears of interest to this day, and from thence to the day of the
<late of the treaty of peace: a favor which the minister of the Congress of the United States acknowledged to flow
from the pure bounty of ~he king, and which he accepts in the name of the said United States with profound and
li\'ely acknowledgments''. Franklin, in transmitting this contract, wrote: t
A11 tho accounts against us for money Jent ancl stores, ai:ms, ammunition, ancl clothing furnished by government were brought in an cl
.examinecl, aml 11 balance recoivccl which made tho clebt amount to tho even sum of 18,000,000 livro.~, exclusive of the Holland loan for

LO.AN FROM SP.A.IN IN 1781.
The early financial transactions of the United States in Europe appear to have been, in the main, secret. To
this may be attributed the fact that the information respecting them is so scanty. If it exists, most of it is probably
buried in the archives of foreign governments, and the Spanish loan of 1781 is no exception to this rufo, even i;ho
Secret Journals of Congress containing but little information respecting it. The instructions to John .Tay, 1::1ent as
minister to Spain in 1779, show that he was directed to represent the distressed state of the financial concorus of
this country "to his Catholic majesty" and to solicit a loan of $5,000,000, but before asking for a loan he wa1::1 to
endeavor to obtain from his majesty a subsidy in consideration of a guarantee by the United Sta,tos of all rights
which Spain might acquire in Florida by conquest from Great Britain.* It is to be presumed, of course, tlmt Mr.
Jay obeyed his instructions, but he obtained neither the subsidy nor the five millions as a loan.
In making up a statement of the foreign debt, however, after the adoption of the Constitution, it l'LJ.lpeared that
a small sum was due Spain for advances of money in tile year 1781. The register of the treasury in a letter to
the Secretary, Alexander Hamilton, October 9, 1792, says:
I have the honor to inclose certified copies from tho treasury books of an account depending between his moS"b Catholic mn~jeHty 1t1ul
the United States, for moneys received cit loan. I cannot find that this loan has been recognized on the Journals of Co11gr(lSH iu Hirn
maHner with the French and Dutch loans. It is founded on a settlement made by the late commissionm: for settling tho foreign 1w11ounte,
entitled: "Loans from the coUrt of Spain." This money was paid to the Hon. James Gurlloquoi and has boo11 regnl1wly aeeo1mtotl fo1• hy
him, having been expended in the purchase of clothing and in the payment of 1Jills of exchange clrawn by orclor of Congress,

It appears by this statement that the Spanish debt amounted to $174,011 00. This was 1111 re!)a.id in tho ycnrs
1792 and 1793; in fact there was an overpayment of $6 13, caused by small variations in the rates of oxclumgt~.
FRENCH LO.AN OF TEN l\IILLION LIV RES.
The financial &ituation of the Continental Congress was at its worst in the ;rears 1770 and 1780.

Ovt>r

$200,000,000 in Continental currency had been is:rned, and this currency, at .first circulating readily at; its fact~

value, had depreciated as the amount issued increased, until it only passed at forty for one. lilvcn at tMs dhwount
it soon ceased to circulate at all, and in the year 1780 "it quietly expired in the hands of its possessorH ". '1'ho
Revolutionary army was reduced to extremity. On tlle 1st of February, 1778, nearly 4,000 men were retnmcHl as
unfit for duty for want of clothes. In January, 1780: General Washington wrote to the governo1· of Oonnocticmt,
that the army had been near three months on a short allowance of bread, within a fortnight almost perishing.
They had been sometimes without bread, sometimes without meat, and oftener without both. They had borne
this distress, in which the officers shared the common lot with the men, with as much fortitude as human mitm'o wnl'.l
capable of, but they had at last been brought to such a dreadful extremity that no authority or inflneuco of tho
officers, ~o virtue ?r patience. in the men, could any longer restrain them from obeying the dictates of their foc:lings.
The soldiers had m several mstances plundered the neighboring inhabitants even of their necessary snhRistoncc.
Without an im~ecliate remedy this evil would soon become intolerable, and, unhapily, l here was no l)l'Ospc~et of reliof
through t~e ordmacy channels. ~hey were reduced to this alternative: either to let the army disband, or to cu11 upon
the count1e~ of. that state to furnish. a supply of cattle and grain for the supply of their wants. If the magistrates
refused the~ md, they would be obliged to have recourse to a military impress. It was evident to the members of
Congress that reu:.ffrom new issues of paper money was hopeless, and it was determined to attempt to negotiu,to n

A commission was issued June 20, 1780, to John Adams, who had been appointetl minister plenipotentfot'Y to
negotiate a treaty of peace with Great Britain, authorizing him to contract for a loan in Holland, and imothcr was
issued to Francis Dana of the same purport, setting forth that Henry Laurens, who had been appointed on that
business, had been cletained by unavoidable accidents from proceeding on the business of his agency.
Shortly after these commissions were issued l\fr. Laurens sn,iled for Europe, but the vessel in which he took
passage was captured by a British frigate off the coast of Newfoundland. He threw his papers overboard, hut not
weighting them sufficiently, they floated and were recovere<l by a British sailor. ~'his incident produced a war
between Great Britain and Holland, for the papers contained the plan of a treaty with the United States drawn up
under the directions of Van Berckel, grand pensionary of Amsterdam. Sir Joseph York, English minister at the
Hague, was instructccl to demand a disavowal of this plan by the states general and "the exemplary punishment
of the pensionary and his accomplices as disturbers of the public peace and violators of the rights of nations"·
"Satisfaction for the supposed offense not being made by the states general, the British minister was orderell to
withdraw from Holland, and this was soon followed by a declaration of hostilities against that country by the court
of London." t
Mr. Laurens was taken to London, examined before the privy council, and committed a close prisoner to the
Tower on the charge of high treason, but the negotiations for a loan went on under the commission issued to Mr.
.A.dams, aided by the exertions of Col. John Laurens, who had been dispatchecl to Europe on the same mission in
December, 1780, his father, Henry Laurens, being still a prisoner in the Tower of London. When Colonel J;aurcn:-;
arrived in Paris, he found that very fair promises of assistance had been made by the French court., but nothing had
been done.
The delay in fulfilling these promises illy accorded with the high and ardent feelings of the young Amedcau
envoy. Knowing the pressing wants of his country and the necessity of immediate aid, if afforded at all, n,fter a delay
of more than two mouths he determined at the next levee day to present, in person, a memorial to the ldng, though
directly contrary to the forms of court. In conversation with the Comte de Vergennes, on the morning of the day
on which he intended to present his memorial to the king, Mr. Laurens expostt1latecl with him, on delaying tbe
promised aid, in such warm and bold language that the minister replied:
"Colonol Lanrens, you are so recently from the headquarters of the American army, th:tt you forget yon are no longer clelivering the

Ile presented his memorial to the king on the same day. It was graciously received and, no doubt, was the

Applications for loans in Holland had hitherto been unsuccessful. The Hollanders either distrusted the secnl'ity,
or were unwilling to incur the resentment of Great Britain by lending the Americans money to enable them to carey
ou the war.
The king of France had, through his minister at the Hague, offered his assistance to the Americans in
procuring loans in that country, but without eftect. He now engaged to become, himself, responsible for the
sums which might be furnished. In consequence of this and the exertions of l\fr. Adams, a loan of ten mi1lions of
livres was obtained in Ilolfand. t
The money thus borrowed, although intended solely for the United States, ha.ving been obtained on the credit
of France, became a debt due to that country, and was provided for in the :financial contract drawn up July 16, 1782,
a.nd signed by the Comte de Vergennes and Benjamin Franklin.

FRENCH LOAN OF SIX MILLION LIVRES.
The loan of 10,000,000 livres obtained from France iu 1781 was soon exhausted. Very little of it in cash ever
reached .America, one-half of it being immediately paid into the French treasury for supplies previously furnished
1Jy the government, and the balance mostly expended in taking up bills of exchange drMvn long before, under the
authority of the Continental Congress. So utterly exhausted was the American treasury that iu 1782, when, peaco
busing become a certainty, it was determined to reduce the army, the utmost difficulty was oxperienc<.'d in obfaining
a small sum to pay the discharged soldiers enough to take them to the places of their enlistment. Now lottus were
necessary, and it was resolved to make new efforts both in France and Holland.
On the 14th of September, 1782, Congress resolved:
That a sum not exceeding $4,000,000, exclusive of the money which Mr. Adams may obtain by Lho loan now nogotiwtiug in Hollruul,

and those wl1ich during tho present year have 1.Jecn incurred, for carrying on the war.

The superintendent of finance and· secretary for foreign affairs were directed to take means for cmrrying this
resolution into effect, and to transmit it to the United States ministers plenipotentiary at Versailles aucl tho Ifogne.
The minister at the court of Versailles was instructed to communicate to his most Christian majesty tho
foregoing resolution, and to assure him of the high sense which the United Sta,tes, in Congress assembled, entertained of his friendship and geuerous exertions, and their reliance on a continuance of them; also, the necessi.t;y of
applying to his majesty on this present occasion. t
No information respecting the negotiations for the loan is accessible. By the contract dated li'ehrnar;1' 2u, 17tla,
it appears that "his majesty determined, notwithstanding the pressing necessities of his own service, to grant to
Oongre:,;s a new pecuniary assistance, which he fixed at the sum of 6,000,000 livres Tournois ($1,080,000), umler tho
title of a loan".f The money was to be paid to the United States, from the funds of the royal treasury, iu srnns of

annum, to be reckoned from. January 1, 1784, and to refund the principal in six equal llortions of 1,000,000 livros
each, the first to be paid January 1, 1785, and the payments to be made thereafter annually until the entire loan
was reimlmrsed. These repayments were not made as provided for by the contract. The debt rmnainml unpaid
until the year 1795, when the agent of the French government accepted a new 5i} per cent'. stock in lion of tho
money (see: 5~ per cent. stock of 1795).

HOLLAND LOAN OF 1782.
The negotiation of this loan was the commencement of a long series of financial transactions in Holland. If
the gold of France aided the United States through the war in which independence was obtained, it was frum
Holland that the money came which assisted the government through the difficult years ofpeace that followed. It
will appear by the remarks on the "French ·loan of ten million livres" that on the 21st of October, 1779, Remy
Laurens was appointed a commissioner to negotiate a loan in Holland, ancl that, as he was not able to leave for
Europe at the time, a commission was issued to John Adams, June 21, 1780, giving him authority to borrow in
Holland, on the credit of the United States, a sum not exceeding $10,000,000, at not exceeding Ci per cent. interest.
There was then no American minister in Holland, nor had .that country yet acknowledged the United States
as an independent nation. Mr. Adams, when he liegan his negotiations, held no commission authorizing him to
treat with the government, and this fact proved an insurmountable obstacle in the way of obtaining a loan in
Amsterdam. A commission as minister plenipotentiary to the court of the Hague hacl been issued: to Mr. Laurens,
and he hacl at last sailed for Europe, but his voyage had only resulted in landing him in. the Tower of London,
and his papers were in possession of the English.
Mr. Adams appeared in Amsterdam as the agent of thirteen states, unknown among nations, unrecognized
and not asking for recognition by the government, but seeking a loan of millions. The repayment of the loan
depended on the success of these states in a doubtful and disastrous war. It is not to be wondered at that the
security seemed very uncertain to the careful Hollanders. The court of the Hague was evidently well disposed
. town.rd the colonies, and had shown this disposition in many ways, which had been the occasion of earnest
remoustrances from the British minis.ter. To one of his letters, demancliug that American vessels be no longer
allowed a shelter in their harbors nor American rebels an asylum in their eountry, the states general returned the
haughty answer that "there were no gates to the Hague".
It does not appear why recognition and a treaty of amity and commerce had not been asked for earlier. No
money couhl be borrowed in Holland until recognition was obtained:i \:Villiam Lee, ·minister to Prussia, had
stopped in Amsterdam long enough to confer with Van Berckel, grand pensionary of that city, antl to draw up the
plan of a treaty; and this paper, captured with Mr. Laurens, finally involved the states geueml in war with Great
Britain.
Mr. Adams began the attempt to negotiate a loan in September, 1780, and soon found that there were great
difficulties in tho way. He wrote to the president of the Uontinental Oongress, Septem her 10 :*

HISTORY OF THE NATIONAL LOANS.

311

.After the recognition of the United States, however, and the opening of the negotiations for a treaty of amity and
commerce, the difficulties vanished. The surrender of Lord Cornwallis, October, 1781, hacl also a good effect on
American credit. It became evident in the spring of 1782 that Great Britain would be compelled to acknowledge
the independence of the United States. Mr. .Adams. applied, in May, 1782, to the firms of Wilhelm & Jan Willink,
N. & J. van Staphorst, and De la Lande & Fynje, who, after some preliminary negotiation, agreed to raise the
money. The Willinks and Van Staphorsts thus became, and long continued, the :financial agents of the United
States in Holland. ]for more than forty years they remained our European bankers, much to our advantage and
probably to their own. During all this long period, their financial honor remained unsullietl and their good faith
unbroken. Years afterwards they purchased from the state of New York a large body of land, which became
known as the "Holland land purchase", now a rich agricultural district in western New York.
The united firms offered a loan of 5,ooo,ooo guilders ($2,000,000), to run for teu years, at 5 per cent. interest,
tlien to be redeemed in five years, by paying each year a fifth part. As compensation for raising this money they
asked 4! per cent., to include all the expenses, except· a charge of 1 per cent. for paying out the annual interest
and a charg·e of 2- per cent. on the final redemption. To this last item Mr. Adams refused to accede. He
offered them 4i} per cent. to cover all charges except the 1 per cent. on the annual interest received and paid by
them. To this they agreed, and the contract was closed, varying in no other particular from their first proposition.
Five formal contracts for i,000,000 guilders each, numbered from 1 to 5, were drawn up, "as advised by the ablest
lawyers and most experienced notaries," setting forth these terms, with a great deal of verbiage, but which, as
Mr. Adams observes in one of his letters, "meant only that the money having been borrowed must be paid." The
contract was concluded June 11, and the five formal documents were confirmed by Oongress September 14, 1782.*
The placing of the loan went on meanwhile without waiting for the confirmation.
It appears that Mr. Adams 11ad small hopes of obtaining the money very soon. In one of his letters he says: t
Although I was obligocl to ongago with them to open tho loan for live mi11ions of guilder~, I clo not expect wo shall obtain that sum
If wo got a million and a half by Christmas it will be more than I expect.

for a long time.

The united firms, however, were more successful than he hoped. By the middle of August they were able to
advise the Continental Congress that 1,300,000 florins awaited their' order, besides reserving 200,000 guilders to
meet bills of exchange which had already been drawn. By the end of the year they had raised 1,S00,000 guil<lers,
notwithstanding the fact that money was very scarce, owing to the demand caused by the war in which ]'rrmce,
Spain, Holland, the United States, and England were engaged. During the year 1783 the smn of 1,400,000
guilders was paid into the hands of the bankers of the United States, although all the warring m1tions, and some
not engaged in the fight,. were striving to raise loans in the market of Holland.
Mr. Adams wrote to Secretary Livingston, July 28, 1783 ::j:
I havo groat pleasm·e in assuring yon that thorn is not ono foreign loan opon in this republic which is in as good credit ancl goes so
<1niok as mine. The empress of Russia oponed a loan ol' five millions about tho same time that I opened mine. She is far from having
oht.ained three mi'llions of it. Spain opened a loan with tho house of Uoii.o, at tho same timo, for two millions only, a-ml you may depeml
upon it it is vory far from being full. Not one-qua-rtor part of tho lonn of Franco, u11on lifo-rcnts, aclvantageous its it is to the lender,
is full. " • • You will see i1crsons ancl letters in America t.lm!; will affirm tl1at, tho SpaniBh loan is full, ancl that France ancl Spaiu'
can havo what money they please hero. Bcliovo mo, this is all stock-Jobbing gnsconacl<l. I have maclo very piirtioular inquiries, 1mcl flncl
tho foregoing account to be the truth. OJ' till tlte 80118 oj' nf'lln, I belie1io llw slouk-jobbvrs aro the greatest liars.

Congress appears, during the negotiations, to have made some shipments of produce to Holland, as the
united firms acknowledge, in December, 1783, the receipt and sale "of the cargo of tobacco of tlie ship Sally".
Notwithstanding these shipments and the large amount received on the loan, however, Congress was so pressed for
means, as to dra,w billR of exchange faster than money could be obtained to meet them, so that bills to the amount
of 1,250,000 guilders went to protest. In this emergency the Messrs. Willink and their co-partners advised Mr.
Adams that, owing to the great demand for money, and France having determined to open a new loan of one
hundred millions on better terms than those offered by the United States, it woulcl be necessary to either authorize
a new loan, at higher rates, or to ofter a higher interest on that which had already been placed on the market. It
was finally determined to authorize a new loan while the negotiations for money nuder the old contracts still went
ou. During the year 1784 the sum of 1,488,000 guilders was received under the contracts for five millions; in 1785,
1M,ooo; and in 1786, 118,000 guilders, making up a total of 5,ooo,ooo. This money was all repaid at the time
provided for in the five contracts.
HOLLAND LOAN OP 1784.

in Europe was seeking loans at the same time, the subscriptions to the 5,ooo,ooo loan, first n~gotfated by Mr.
Adams, came in but slowly. .At the same time there was extreme necessity for money to save the credit of the
United States, already endangered by the fact that drafts for nearly 1,300,000 guilders in excess of the amount
already raised in Holland had been drawn by Robert Morris, the superintendenb of finance, part of which drafts had

This contract was signed in Amsterdam, March O, 187'.f,, anu confirmed by Oongress February 1, 1785, " at the
city hall in New York." The United States by its terms were, for a principal of 2,000,000 guilders received, to
return 2,801,800 .
.Mr. Adams thought the contract involved an enormous sacrifice, and expresses the fear that he ''should
be blamed by numbers of versons "· He wrote to Benjitmin Franklin before concluding the bargain that he despaired
of obtaining the money "without agreeing to terms so disadvantageous as to be little better than the final protest
of the bills"· It is possible, in view of the distress he mm1ifestetl u,t the time, that he had not submitted tlrn terms
and his fears to the cn,lm logic of arithmetic. Oomputations show that if the United States cashed the obligations
distributed by lottery within six months after the drawing, as they had the privilege of doing, and, as they actually
did, the 4 per cent. interest on the loan, witlt bonus and gratifications added, amounted to less than Oil; per cent. annual
interest. He had expressed himself as willing and bad been authorized by Oongress to pay 6 per cent. interest; he
was therefore sacrificing· less than three-quarters of one per cent.
Benjamin Franklin, in commenting on the affair in one of his letters to l\fr. .Adams, says : "I hope these
. mischievous events will at length convince our people of the truth of what I long since wrote to them, that the
fonndation of crec7it must be laii.1. ctt home," a golden maxim for all financiers, public and private.
The united firms were to receive on this loan, as on the five million loan, a commission of 4j! per cent.
to cover all expenses. Tllo money was raised in time to save the bill$ of exchange from being returned to the
United States; in fact, more than I,000,000 of guilders were received as au advance before the contract was signed;
ancl the whole ·2,000,000 were obtained within the yea.r. It was all redeemed at the time and on the terms required
by the contract.
HOLLAND LO.AN OF 1787.
This loan appears to have been contracted in order to raise money with which to pay the .interest on the
previous loans in Holland, made in the years 1782 and 1784. The interest which hacl before fallen clue had been :rm\d,
partly by remittances from America and partly by some portions of the amonnt receiv~d on the original loans .
.Although the experiment of issuing vast amounts of paper money hacl proved so disastrous, yet the policy of the
government still ran in the same course, and a new emission of paper currency was afloat, which, on its face, w.as
redeemable' by the different States, individually, but inclorsed by. the United SbJites, and was, by an act of March
18, 1780, to be issued at a not greater rate than one dollar of tl1e new for each twenty of· the old. The idea of
replacing an inconvertible and valueless paper currency by another issue of a smaller amount, equally inconvertible
and almost as valuelesss, was not a new one in finance. It is one of the regular stages on the downward road of
paper money, as may yet be.forcibly shown, when a financial Gibbon shall arise to write the hi1;1tory of its decline·
and fall.
Such imposts and duties· as the Oontinental Oongress was able to command or persuade, from the thirteen
very independent' statesi were mostly paid in this new cl.urency, which, being wortbless abroad, rendered the

It was the financial difficulties experienced under the old system of government that, more than anything else,
brought about the calling of the convention which, in 1787, framed the present Constitution of the United. States.
The Continental Congress possessed sufficient authority to carry on war, to make peace, to conclucle treaties, autl
to carry on most of the functions of government; but to do all this required a revenue, and for this they were dependent
on the will of the states. It was evident that such a system could not last. At the time Mr. Aclams began the
negotiations for the loan, delegates to the constitutional convention had been chosen in most of the states, n.nd the
convention was in session before the loan was completed.

HISTORY OF THE NATIONAL LOANS.

The shipment of American procluce under the authority of Congress, to meet demands in Europe, appears to
have been a favorite idea among the American agents abroad, and hacl often been acted on, large ·amounts of
tobacco, &c., having been sent to Beaumarchais and others. It was continued by the new government after the
adoption of the Constitution of 1787, and extended to the purchase of cargoes of coffee and sugar. The contract
for this loan, as confirmed by Congress, October 11, 1787, will be found in Journals of Congress, vol. iv, Appendix,
p. 27. By its terms the loan was to run for ten years, at 5 per cent. per annum interest, then to be redeemable in
ft rn equal annual payments. It would appear from the letter of Willink aml others that the United States received
bm; 92 guilders for each 100 of obligations, as they state the amount of expenses at 8 per cent. It was all
redeemecl as provided for in the contract. The 'firm of De la Lande & Fynje who had been concerned in the
previous loans had nothing to do with this; before the negotiations commenced, they had failed, owing the United
States a considerable sum, only a part of which was recovered.
HOLLA.ND LOAN OF 1788.
This loan was negotiated to meet the expenses of the United States in Europe, and to afford a :financial basis
on which to start the new government at home. The new Constitution was to go into operation when ratified by
nine states. Some of the states voted to accept it in 1787, but the vote of the ninth state, New Hampshire, was not
secured until July, 1788. It was known in Europe, however, early in that year, that it would be ratified, and as the
Oontinental Congress was utterly without funds to turn over to its successors, money must be provided in some wa~T·
Thomas Jefferson vms then minister of the United States at the court of Versailles, and with Mr. Adams, then
about to return to America, condncted the negotiations. His account of the affair leaves little to be desired to make
the matter clear.
"Among the debilities of tho government of the Confederation, no one was more clistinguished or more distressing than the utter impossibility of obtaining, from the states, the moneys necessary for tho irn,yment of clebts, or oven for th() orclinary expenses of the government.
Some contributecl a little, some less, ancl some nothing; ancl the last furnishecl at length an excuse for the first to do nothing also. ifr.

was now elected Vice-president of the United St11tes, was soon to return to America., ancl had reforrecl our bankers to me for future counsel
on our affairs in their hands. But I had no powers, no instruct.ions, no means, ancl no familiarity with the subject. It had always been
exclusively,uncler his management, except as to occasional 11ncl partial deposits in tho hancls of Mr. Grauel, banker in Paris, for special a.nu
local purposes. Those last had been e:x:h11usted for some time, and I ha<l frequently prcssecl the treasury board to replenish this particular
deposit, as Mr. Grand now refused to make further advances. They answered cauclic1ly thn.t no funcls could be obtainecl until tho new
government should get into action and have time to make its arrangements. Mr. Adams had received his appointment to the court ot
J,ondon while ongagccl 11t Paris, with Dr. Frnnklin aml myself, in the negotiations under our joint commissions. Re hall repaired thence

wbile we coulcl yet avail ourselves of his 110WOrs, for when they would be gone WO shoulcl be 1Vit;l10ut resource. I was claily dunned by a.

"An act making provision for the debt of the United States" was approved .August 4, 1790 (1 Statutes, 138).
This act provided that the surplus of customs and tonnage duties above the sum of $600,000 should be applied to
the payment of the interest and the reduction of the principal of the foreign debt, and authorized the President to
borrow, on the credit of the United States,· a sum not exceeding $12,000,000, to be devotecl to paying interest,
arrears, and installments on the foreign debt, and to paying off the whole of it, if it could be effected on terms
·
advantageous to the United States.
".An act making provision for the reduction of the public debt" was ap1wovedA.ugust 12, J790 (1 Statutes, 186),
by which the President was authorized to borrow, on behalf of the United States, a sum not exceeding $2,000,000,
at an interest not exceeding 5. per cent., the money so borrowed to be applied to the purchase of the debt of the
United States. These purchases were directed to be made under the direction of the president of the Senate, the
Cliief Justice, the Secretary of State, the Secretary of the Treasnr;y, and the Attorney General, for the time being,
any tllree of whom were authorized to cause such purchases to be made.
Under these acts the Secretary of the Treasury, Alexander Hamilton, authoi:ized the houses of W. & J. Williuk,
N. & J .. van Staphorst, and Hubbard to open negotiations for a loan of three million florins or guilders ($1,200,000),
giving them authority to pledge the good faith of the United States for the pa,yment of the interest and the
rnpa.yment of the principal. The contl'act for the money has never been printed, but a translo,tion of the original
is to be found among the "Washington Papers" in the Department of State. It is dated November 12, 1790. It
provided that the loan should be reimbursable within fourteen years, in five annual payments of 600,000 guilders
each, the first pa;yment to be made February 1, 1800, and on that clu.y annually until paid.
Three thousand bonds or obligations of the Unitecl States, for one thousarnl gnil<lers each, were to be issued,
and in the December preceding each annua.l 1)ayment the numbers of six hundred of these were to be drawn by lot,
in the presence of a notary, the numbers so drawn to be reimbursed in the following February. Oou1)ons for the
annual h1terest at 5 per cent. per annum were to be attached to each bond. For commission and all expenses
connected with the loan the United States were to pay 42- per cent. on the principal.
ROLL.A.ND LO.A.N OF M.A.ROH, 1791.
This loan, like the previous one, was contracted under the authority given by the acts of August 4 and 12, 1700.
The Secretary of the Treasury, Septemller 1, 1790, by direction of the llresident, authorized Willia.m Short, then
charge d'affaires at· Paris, to proceed to Amsterdam and endeavor to effect a second loan under these acts. Mr.
Short's instructions will lle found in Hamilton's Works, vol. iv, p. 38. They were to the effect that he shoulcl borrow
for the United States, on the best terms practicable, such sums as would lle necessary to discharge the interest
and such installments of the foreign debt as became due during the year 1791; to employ the houses of Willinks
and tlie Van Stapllorsts unless doubt should lle entertained of their stability ancl influence in the money market1
and to endeavor to reduce the amount of commissions below that which the United States had paid on previous
loans. Mr. Short found no reason to discontinue the :financial relati011s of the United States with the :fil•ms
mentioned. Re met with much difficulty in his efforts to reduce the amount of commissions to be paid, the
bankers representing that but one power in the world, Austria, had obtained more favorable terms than the
"United States, and even in her case the differern;ie was but one-half of one pel' cent. .A.fter some negotiation,
however, the commission and brokerage were reduced to 4 per cent. It was determined to open a loan for two
and a half million of guilders ($1,000,000), at 5 per, cent. interest, the reimbursement to begin in ten years and to
be completecl in five, ~he United States to have the right to reimburse the whole at. an earlier period if deemed
prope:i;,
The brokers, however, when consulted about the time for opening the loan, thought it an unfavorable moment
for making loans in genera.J, and especially for the United States, because .their 5 per cent. loans were below i)ar ·
a1reaclS, owing to many people selling these loans in order to place _their funds in those made by the "liquidated
debt" of the United States, which bore a higher rate of interest. They therefore advised postponing the opening
of the loan until the following February, when it was thought there would be a better state of things in the money
market. This was acccordingly done. The loan was brought on the market February 15, 1791, and was all taken
up within two hours after being published on the exchange-a celerity, of which there had been no instance before
in the loans for any country. The credit of the United States evidently stoocl high in Holland .
.A translation of the original contract, as confirmed by the President, exists among the "Washington Papers" in
the Department of State. It provided that the loan should run for eleven years, at 5 per cent. interest, then to be
redeemable in th1.;, city of .A.msterclam, on the 1st day of March in each year until paid, at' the rate of 500,000 g'tlilders
11er annum, the Unitecl States to have the privilege of reimbursing tlle whole sum or any part thereof at any earlier
elate if they should wish so to do.
Two thousand five .hundred bonds of the United States, for 1,000 guilders each, were to be issued to the
subscribers to the loan, and it was to be determined by lot which of these should be redeemed in any one year.
Redemption of this loan began March 1, 1802, and was completed in 1805, one year sooner than the time fixed
in the contract, the United States having made a partial use of the option allowed.

Before the subscriptions were concluded it was found that money was obtainable at lower mtos, and a porifon

HOLL.A.ND LOAN OF DECEMBER, 1791.

The information respecting this loan is very scanty. It was negotiated in Holland under the authorizing acts of
August 4 and 12, 1790, by Mr. Short. .Finding that money could be obtained cheaper in Rolland, he withdrew 11
portion of the Antwerp loan from market and openecl a loan of 3,000,000 guilders ($1,200,000) in .Amsterdam. l1t 1
obtained the money at a lower rate of interest but a higher eommission. It appears to have been negotiatecl to run
. for twelve years, at 4 per cent. interest, then to he redeemable in five equal annual payments of 600,000. gnildon:l
each. The charges for commission, brokerage, &c. 1 were 51 per cent. W. & J. ·wminks, N. & J. van Staphon1t,
ancl Hubbard were the bankers emplo;yed in the transaction. The redemption commenced in 1803 and was complet1!1l
in 1~07.

HOLL.A.ND LOAN OF 1794.

It appears to have been determined, in tbe year 1793, to borrow an additional sum of five million guilders under
the acts of ~ugust ·:b aml 12, 1790, the power to borrow conferred on the President by those acts not having been
fully used, and money being needed to pay installments and interest on the foreign debt and to make purchases of
the domestic debt under the acts constituting a sinking fund. At the same time it was probable that the United
States might need large sums for military purposes, or at least·to make preparations for war. There waR a prospect
at home of general hostilities with the Indians, on whose territory the white population was beginning to encroach,
while abroad the French Revolutiou ho,d set Europe in a blaze. France had already declared war against England,
Rus·sia, and Hollancl. It was possible that this war wonlcl involve, as it did, every nation in Europe, while it was
doubtful if the United States would be able to preserve its neutrality. Hamilton submitted the question of a loan to
the President, who, before deciding, required a statement of the operations of the treasury under the authorizing
acts of 1700. Hamilton transmitted the statement June 24, 1793,* from which it appeared that there had been
borrowed, under these acts, $7,546,666 64, leaving, still unused, authority to borrow the sum of $6,453,333 36.
One of the purposes for which the money was wanted was the payment of a million guilders, due as an installment
on the• first loan contracted by the Oontinental Congress in Holland, but advices were soon recei>ed that this
installment had been reloaned to the United States for another ten yea.rs. This reduced the amount required to
four millions, which was subsequently cut down by Washington to three millions, as the rates of interest and
charges asked for money in Holland had increased, while too prospect of war hacl become less. Hamilton's
instructions to the ban.kers of the United States in Amsterdam are found in the following letter:t
Hamilton to W. go J. WillinTc, N. <]· J, Van Staph01·st, ai1d Hubbard, Ban7cers, A.mstm·dam.
'rREASURY DEPARTMENT, August 12, 1793.
GENTLEMEN: I have lately the i)leasure of your letters of the 22d of .A.pril and 1st of May.

The last was partienlarly acceptable, as
it removed all anxiety about the June payment in a mocle quite satisfactory to me.
The low prices at which our stocks have been for some time past, owing to the state of affairs in Europe (which has tended to lower
them in two ways, by lessening the foreign demand and by opening new channels for the more beneficial employment of our domestic
capital), indicate the expediency of extending the purchases of' stock on account of the government, which at present yields a very
decided advantage.
Upon this ground it is judged arlvisable to endeavor to obtain a loan in Holland for three millions of :florins, though better terms
should not be obtllinablo than 5 per cent. interest and 4 i)er cent. charges.
Not expecting that Mr. Short will be in Jlolland when this letter reaches you, and having full confidence in your pure zeal for the
interests of the United States, I have concluded to adclress an instruction for the above purpose immediately to you.
In the event, therefore, of Mr. Short's not being in Holland when this letter is received, you will take measures, of yourselves, to
obtain a loan for the abovo sum, within the limit above specified.
I count, nevertheless, upon your zealous exertions, if the state of the market should possibly aclmit of it, to obtain the loan at a lower
rate of interest. 'l'he success of your encleavors for that purpose will be particularly gratifying to me, and will be, in more than one way,
useful to our affairs. It is im110rtant, too, that I should receive the earliest advice of what you shall do.
The inclosed letter for Mr. Short ndvises him of the present aITangement, and desires him to co-opera.to iu whatever may be necessary
to give the business due form.
With great and real consideration and esteem, I am, &c.

Under these instructions the Messrs. Willink, Van Staphorst, and Hubbard opened the loan about December 1,
The general ·war in Europe had ra.ised the price of money,
though it had a favorable effect on the credit of the United States as a neutral power in the great contest.
The formal contract for the money cannot now be found. It seems to have borne the date of April IO, 1794,
and was for a loan of 3,ooo,ooo guilders ($1,200,000), to run for eleven years at 5 per cent. interest, then to be
redeemable in :fiv'3 equal annual payments of 600,000 ·guilders each. The charges for commission and brokerage
were 4 per cent. Its redemption commenced in the year 1805 and was completed in 1809. This closes. the
account of the Hollancl loans. They amounted to 32,5007000 guilders ($13,000,000), of which 9,ooo,ooo guilders
were borrowed under the authority of the Continental Congress and 23,500,000 guilders ($9,400,000) by the present
government of the United States, which began and completed the redemption oftbe whole.
.
A part of tbe redemption of these loans was effected in rather a singular way. Instead of buying bills of
exchange and remitting ·them the Secretary of the Treasury purchased cargoes of coffee and sugar, deliverable in
Rolland, paying the owners. i~ this country. These cargoes were consigned to the bankers of the United States ~n
.Amsterdam, sold by them, and the net proceeds applied in the redemption of the debt. These shipments began m
the year 1795 and continued until 1803 or 1804.
Of the $9 400 000 borrowed by the present government' over $3,000,000 was paid into the treasury of the United
States t1iml us~d i~ buying up the domestic debt, under the operations of the sinking fund; the balance was used
.in paying the debt due Spain, debts due to foreign officers who had served in the armies of the Revolution, and a
lai·ge portion in iiaying off a part of the de?t clue France. In connection with these paynients to France, an
1793, and obtained the money by August 31, 1794.

had been raised with difficulty, lJart of it by heavy sacrifices, ~nd to pay the mterest and installments on these

DEBT DUE FOREIGN OFFIOERS.
Tlierc were many foreign officers wlio fought in the armies of the H.evolution. Of these, some were sent to
this country by Ilf. de Beaumarchais in the same vessels that brought cargoes of military supplies; but this
statement does not apply to La Fayette, Steuben, Kosciusko, and others of high rank or of great military
experience in the wars of Europe. These, and a large number whose achievements are forgotten, and of whose
record nothing remains save what is found in the journals of the Continental Congress arnl the archives of the
Treasury Department, through which they received pay for their services, reached the American armies by their
own independent means. Several of these officers, however, hacl overestimated the reward they expected to receive,
while others followed with equally high expectations, ancl in many cases with snch extravagant l)romises made
theru as to cause serious trouble to Congress.
Native officers who had risen to high rank in the Revolutionary army were not inclined to be quietly
superseded by foreigners. Among those who were sent over by Beaumarchais was M. dn Oonclray, who emne
under a contract, by which he was to receive the rank of major general, with the command of the artillery. Bnt
General Knox was in command of the artillery, ancl was one of Washington's most trnstecl officers. To tnJrn the
command from him and give it to a foreigner ignorant of the langna.ge of the troops he was to command, wonld
have caused the immediate resignation of Knox, and would have demoralizecl the service; while to decline to
acknowledge a contract made by an American commissioner in Paris, would hn.ve causecl gren.t embitrrassmeut,
especially when the contracting party had come three thousand miles to claim its fulfillment. The question was
iwstponed in Congress, Dt1 Condray consenting to serve as a volunteer with a nominal rank until n, decision was
made. Before it was formally settled he was accidentally clrownecl while crossing the Schuylkill river. Mn.ny of
those who came over from Europe po seek commissions in the American army were men of high character, and
their services have added luster to our military history,
The Continental Congress was unable, when the war closed, to pay the army in full, mnch difficulty being found
in obtaining even enough money to send the soldiers to their places of enlistment. Tho foreign officers, so far as
pay was concerned, probably fared rather better than the American soldiers, a strong effort being made to pay tllem
as large a portion of the amount due them as possible; but to pay them in full, could not be done. An adjustment
of their accounts was made in 1782, and a part of their demands was paid in cash. For the balance, certificates of
indebtedness were given, bearing an interest of 6 per cent. These certificates, like all paper of the Continental
Congress, depreciated rapidly in value, and in January, 1784, under a resolution of Congress, they were called in
and new certijicates were issued, dated" y0 5th April, 1784", bearing interest at 6 per cent. from January 1, payable
annually at the house of M. Grand, banker, in Paris. No time for the' redemption of these certificates was named .
.The total amount of certificates or bonds issued was $186,988 78. Their redemption began in 1792 under instructions
from the Secretary of the Treasury, who directed that a part of the Holland loan of 2 950 000 guilders should be used
for that purpose, paying the holders of the bonds in gold or its equivalent, and not id the' clepreciatecl paper currency
of France. In 1803 the larger portion had been reimbursed, but a small amount remained outstanding, not lrnving
been presented for payment. It was not until the year 1828 that all were redeemed •
. The origin~l bonds, w~ich are all well preserved, were printed on parchment, and were the only bonds of the
Umted States issued on this material.

UNITED STATES, March 31, 1790.

SIX PER OENT., THREE PER CENT., AND DEFERRED SIX PER OENT. STOOKS OF 1700.
The mass of debt which devolved upon the United States, as an inheritance from the Oontiueutal Oong·ress
and the several colonies, engaged the attention of the national legislature soon aftCl' the adoption of the Oonstitntion. The debt was very large and depreciated, and was still depreciating in value; but it was the JH'ice of liberty,
and the payment of that portion of it contracted by national authority was macle obligatory by the new Constitution. It was necessary, therefore, to provide for it in some way, although the payment of even the interest seeme<l
impossible in the exhausted condition of the country at that time.
In reply to' a confidential letter from P.resident Washington, d11ted Mount Vernon, July 29, 1792, Hamilton
says:•
The general inducements to a provision for the public debt were:
1. To preserve the public faith and integrity by fulfilling, as far as was practicable, the public cnga"'eruonta.
2. To manifest a due respect for property, by satisfying the public obligations in the hands of the ;ublic credito1·a 1 and whioh wero
-is much their property as their houses or their lands, their hats or their coats.
3. To revive and establish public credit, the palladium of public safety.
4. To preserve the government itself by showing it worthy of the confidence which was plaoad in it; to procure to the community
the blessings which in innumerable ways attend confidence in the government, and to avoid the evils which ill as many ways attend the
want of confidence in it.

On September 21, 1789, the House of Representatives adopted the following resolutions:
Resoh•eii, That this Honse consider an adequate provision for the support of the public crodit as a matter of high importance to the
national honor and prosperity.
•
.Retolved'. That ·the Secretary of the Treasury be directed to prepare a plan for that purpose, antl to re1)orb J;ho samo to tbis Honse at
its next meetmg.
,

There were many difficulties in the way of adopting a plan which would be s~~tisfactory · in fact to do evenhanded justice was probably impossible. One great difficulty was in the many different ld~cls of aebt which it
was t;hought ~ould b.e paid ~y the United States. These various kinds of debt bad depreciated in different ways;
and on ma~y JUSt claims, which had been purchased of the original owners for a triile, a large amount of iuterest
was due, m ·some cases amounting to more than the purchase-money for the claims themselves. This del>t
was due on account of foreign loans obtained in France and Holh1nd; the obligations of the Continental Congress
