There were two kinds of debt i.n the adjustment of which there seems to have been no difficulty: One was
the undisputed foreign debt, where the lenders had paid for their bonds in gold, on the faith of the Continenta,l
Congress; the other was the paper money issued by Congress ancl the several states.
Authorities vary much as to the amount of paper money issuecl during the struggle for independence. Possibly
l\ir. J e:fferson's statement in his writings may be taken as approximate to the truth, and it affords, at the same time,
a striking exhibit of the effects of the inflation of our paper cm;rency: *
On the commencement of the late Revolution Congress had no money. The external commerce of tho states being suppressed, the
farmer coulcl not sell bis produce, and, of course, coulcl not pay a tax. Congress· had no resource then but in paper money. Not. being
11blo to ln.y a tax for its reclemption1 they could only promise that taxes should be laicl for that purpose, so as to redeem tho bills by a
certain. day. They clid 11ot foresee the long continuance of the war, the almost total suppression of their exports, and other events
which rendered the performance of their engagements impossible. The pa11er money continued for a twelvemonth equal to gold and
silver; but the quantities which they were obliged to emit, for the purpose of the war, exceeded what had been the usnaJ quantity of the
circulating medium.
·
It began, therefore, to become cheaper or, as we expressed it, it depreciated, as golcl and silver would ha.ve clone had they been
thrown into circulation in equal quantities. But not having, like them, an intrinsic value, its depreciation was more rapid ancl groatcn•
than conld ever h1we happened with them. In two years it had fallen to two dollars of paper money for one of silver; in three yenrs to
four for one; in nine months more it fell to ten for one; aud in the six months following, that is to say, by September, 17791 it hail fallen
to twenty for one.
Congress, alarmed at the consequences which were to be approhondecl should they lose this resource altogether, ·foonght it 11ecessary
to make a vigorous effort to stop its further depreciation, They therefore cletermiuec11 in the first place, that their emissions ahoultl not
exceed two hundred millions of dollars, to which sum they wore then nearly arrh•ed, and t.hough they knew that twenty dollars of what.
they were then issuing would buy no more for their army than cu1e.silver clollar would bny, yet they thought it woulcl be worth wllllo to
submit to the sacrifice of nineteen out of twenty dollars if they ooulcl thereby stop further dop1·oeiation. They therefore published an
aclclress to their constituents, in which they renewed their original declarations that this paper moneyshoulcl be recleemecl at dollar for
clollar. They proved the ability of ·tho states to do this, and that their liberty would be cheaply bought at this price. The cleolara.tion
was ineffectual. No man received the money at a bettor rate. On the contrary, in six months more, tha.t is, by llfo:rch, 1780, it bad fallen
to forty for one. Congr«i;iss tl1en tried au experiment of a different kind. Cousidel'ing their former offe1·s to recleem this money at l)ar as
relinquished, by the general refusal to take it but in progressive depreciation, they requirecl the whole to be brong·ht in, declarecl it should
be redeemecl at its present value of forty for one, and that they wou!cl'give to the holclors now bills, reducocl in their denomination to the
sum of gold or silver which was actually to be paid for them. This wonlcl reduce the nominal sum of the mass in circulation to tlrn
present worth of that mass, which was five millions, a sum not too great for the circulation of tho states, ancl which they therefore hopecl
would not depreciate further, as they continuecl Jlrm in their pnr1)ose of emitting no more. This effort was as unavailing as the former.
Very little of the money was brougl1t in.
·
H coutinuecl to circulate ancl to depreciate till the encl of 1780, when it had fallen to seventy-five for one; and the money circulated
from the French army being, by that time, sensible in all the states north of the Potomac, the paper oeasecl its circulation altogether hi
those states. In Virginia and North Carolina it co:µtinuecl a year longer, within which time it fell to one thousancl for oue, and then
expired, as it hacl done in the other states, without a single groan. Not a murmur was heard on this occasion among the people. On the
contrary, universal congratulations took place on their seeiug this gigantic mass, whose dissolution had threatened convulsions which
should shake ·their infl1ll°b Confederacy to its center, quietly interred in its grave.

Jefferson estimates the value of the two hundred millions of Oontinental currency at the time of its emission at
$36,367, 719 83 in specie, and says:*
If we estimo,to at the same value the like sum of $200,000,000 supposed to ho,ve been ei_nitted by the sto,tes'. ancl reckon the
fetleral debt, foreign and domestic, at about $43,000,000, and the state debts at $25,000,00D, it w1~l form an amount ?f $140,000~0.0~, t~e
total sum which the war cost the Unitecl States. It continued eight years from the battle of Lexmgton to the cessation of host1hties m
Americo,. The annual tJ:x:pense was therefore eqiml to about $17,500,000 in specie.

A portion of the· domestic debt had been audited by authority of the Continental Oong1:ess, and certificates
given therefor. This was called the "liquidated" debt; while the floating debt, not yet anchtecl, was called the
"nnliquidatecl."
.
The question of assuming the debts of the several states was one that gave rise to e~c1ted debate an(l much
angry ;f~eling. The states had contracted these debts, partly in defending themselves 11gams~ the common e?1e1ny,
partly in carrying on their governments or in undertakings with which the rest of the .contn~ent had notlnng to
do. Some of them were largely indebted to the general government, while others agam clauned la1'ge arnounts
dne them from the Confederation.
In relation to this subject, Ilamilton, in his letter to Washington, before referred to, says: t
The particular inducements to an assumption of the state debts were:
1. To consolidate the finances of the country, and give au assurance of pcnnanent order in them; avoicling the collision of thirteen

different and independent systems of finance under concurrent and coequal authorities, ancl tho scritmbliugs for revenue which wouhl
Jmve been incident to so many different systems.
.
2. To secure to the government of the Union, by avokling thoso entanglements, an effectual commaucl of the resources of tho Union
for present and future exigencies.
3. To equalize tlte conclition of the citizens of the several states in the important article of t,axatfon; rescuing 1~ irnrt of them from
lieiug oppressed with burdens beyond their strength, on account of extraordinary exertions in the war and througl1 the want of cort11iu
aclventitious resources which it was the goml fortune of others to possess.

On the 9th of January, 1790, Hamilton made his report to the House of Representatives, in obedience to
the resolution of September 21, 1789, before referred to, in which he gave many reasons for assuming the clebts
of the old government and of the several states, and furnished a plan for supporting the public credit. His
recommendations were finally (ldopted, and embodied in the act making provision for the payment of the debt
of the United States, approved August 4, 1790 (1 Statutes, 138).
Section 2 of this act authorized a loan of $12,000,000, to be applietl to the payrnent of the Jlrincipal and interesb
of the foreign debt: Provided, That no contract should be entered into which should preclude the Unitecl States
from reimbursing the loan within fifteen years.
Section 3 authorized a loan to the full amount of the domestic debt, payable in certificates issued for the said
debt, according to their specie vaJ.ue, and computing the iutcrest upon such as bore interest to December 31, 17!)1,
The character of these certificates is specified in the same section of the act.
Section 13 authorized a loan of $21,500,000, payable in the principal and interest of the certificates or notes
which prior to Januar;y 1, 1790, were issued by the respective states as evidences of indebtedness incurred by thenr
for the expenses of the late war.

The act of August 12, 1790 (1 Statutes, 18G), provides, that for the purpose of reclucing the amount of the public
debt, the surplus revenue derived from the duties collected on goods, wares, and merchandise imported, ancl the
tonnage of ships or vessels to the last day of December, 1790, shall be applied to the purchase of the clebt of the
United States, at its market price, if not exceeding i)ar, or true value thereof.
The sakl purchases to be made under the direction of the president of the Senate, Chief Justice, Secretary of
State, Secretary of the Treasury: and Attorney General for tl,ie time being; who, or any three of whom, with the
approbation of the President of the United States, sha.11 cause said purchases to be made in such manner and under
such regnlaLions as shall appear to them best calculated to fulfill thl"l intent of the act, said purchases to be made
openly and with due regard to the equal benefit of the several states.
Their accounts were to be settled as other public accounts, and a report of their i)roceedings, as commissioners
of the sinking fund, to be laid before Congress each session.
It also authorized a loan not exceeding $2,000,000, at an interest not exceeding 5 per cent., to be applied to
the purchase of the said debt under similar regulations and restrictions with the surplus aforesaid, and provided
tluit, out of the interest arising on the debt to be purchased, there should be approp1fated and applied a sum not
exceeding the rate of 8 per cent. per annum, on account both of princi1lal aud interest, toward the repayment of the
amount so to be borrowed.
Prior to the ymi,r 1836, the amount of money requisite for the redem1)tion of these secui.'ities had all been
advanced to the different loan officers in the several states, or to the Bank of the United States and its branches
acting as loan agents, and the nation was practically free from debt. Subsequently small sums were re1mid into
the treasury, not having been called for by parties holding the various outstandi11g obligations. Since that time
it portion of these have presented their claims for payment to the treasury, ancl received the amount due them.
The system in vogue in early years for the redemption of the public debt of the United States is thus explained
by Secretary Gallatin, in a letter to the Hon. John Randolph, chairman of the committee of ways and means,
November 28, 1803:
There was a commissioner of loans in each of the original thirteen states on the 4th of August, 1790, the
date of the "Act makir1g provision for the debt of the United States", and the whole of the domestic clebt was
credited to the stockholders for the sums to which they were respectivel;r entitled, on books kept either by one of
these commissioners or the register of the treasury.
The debt was transferable only on the books of the treasury, or of the commissioner upon which th~ credit for
tbe same existed at the time of transfer by the stockholder or his attorney; and the interest was paid and the
l'eimbursement of the principal effected, wheresoever the credit fo1· the stock existed at the time when such interest
became due, or such reimbursement was made.
The interest and reimbursement which might remain unclaimed for nine months was aftel'wards l)ayablo only
at the treasury. The proprietors might at any time have their stock transferred from the books of one commissioner
to those of another, or to the books of the treasury, and from the books of the treasury to those of a commissioner.
During the last fourteen days of every quarter all the above-mentioned books were closed, and no transfer
whatever coulcl be effected, that period being employed in calculating the amount of interest and refo1bursement
payable on the first day of the succeeding qt:tarter to each stockholder, and in transcribing the same on what were
called the" dividend books" for that quarter. The sum payable on the treasury books was the~, together with the
~Hamilton's Works, vol. iv, p. 251.

dividend books relative to it deposited in and paid at the office of the Ba.nk of the United States, at Washington,
to the proprietors, that part excepted which was payable to stockholders (principally foreigners) who had given
permanent powers of attorney to the Ba.nk of the United States, and which was remitted to that institution a.t
Philadelphia.
.
Some of the commissioners of loans at times, though not always, completecl their calculations, and communicated
the result to the treasury early enough to enable the Secretary to remit them the precise amount wanted before
the first day of the quarter. To every commissioner whose account was not received, a sum estimated sufficient to
discharge the amount payable was remitted in time to meet the clay of payment.
The commissioners of :Massachusetts, New York, Pennsylvania, South Carolina, and Georgia deposited the
money remitted to them, together with their dividend books, in the Bank of the United States and its offices,
respectively, where the dividends were paid.
The other commissioners, residing at places where there was no office of the Bank of the United States, kept the
specie and paid the dividends themselves. The amount of dividends on the books of the several commissionei s
which remained unclaimed for nine months was, from time to time, and as the same was ascertaine<l, paid by the
treasury to the office of the bank at the seat of government, and deducted from the estimate of advances to be
made to the respective commissioners. It will thus be seen that the duties of the commissioners of lmtns consisted
in entering on their books the transfers resulting either from sales or from transfers of stock from one office to
another; in issuing new certificatei:; in conformity with such transfers; in calculating and transcribing on proper
books, for every quarter, the dividends payable on the stock then standing on their hooks; and, in those places where
there was no office of the Bank of the United States, in paying the dividends.
The records show that on June 30, 1803, there was standing upon the books of the treasury and the several
commissioners of loa.ns, in stock of the United States, $70;154,7Sl 48, which amonnt was held and owned by

HISTORY OF THE NATIONAL LOANS.

329-

SUBSCRIPTION LOAN OF 1791.
The first bank of the United States appears to have been proposed by Alexander Hamilton.
On the 14th of December, 1790, as shown by the journal of the House of Representatives of that date, the
Secretary of the Treasury transmittecl to the House a letter, accompanying his report No. 2, with a plan for the
institution of a national bank, which was read and referred to a committee of the whole.
On the 23d of December it was ordered: "That the clerk of the House do communicate to the Senate that this
Honse has received a report from the Secretary of the Treasury containing a plan for a national bank, and that he
carry an attested copy of the said report to the Senate."*
·
The bill to incorporate the subscribers to the Bank of the Unitell States passed the Senate January 20, li9lt
and was reported in the House of Representatives the following day, reacl twice aml referred.
On the 31st the Honse resolved itself into a committee of the whole, and the bill was read by paragra1Jhs; n<>
amendment being offered, the bill was reported and ordered to be read the third time on the succeeding day. The
debate on the passage of the bill began February 1. It took, immediately, a sectional form, .the bill being favored
by the members from the North and East, while it was opposed by most of the southern members.
The debate continued until February .S, when the bill passed the House by yeas 39, nays 20. An analysis of
the vote show1:i that but six members from the south of Masbn ltncl Dixon's line voted for the bill, while but 011e
member from the North voted against it.
The debates in tho Senate have not been preserved. James Madison, in the House, and James 1\fonroe, in the
Senate, each of whom afterward became President of the Unitecl States, voted against it.
On the 14th of February ib was presented to the President for his approval, received llis signaturr,, and became
a law Febrnnry 25, 1791 (1 Statutes, 191). In the interval he was occu1)ied in anxious and diligent inquiry into the
constitutionality of the bill, and asked the members of his cabinet for their advice on t_he subject. Edmund Randolph,
Attorney General, itrnl Thomas Jefferson, Secretary of State, gave their opinions, in writing, against it, while Hamilton
gave his written opinion on the other side. The opinions of the others do not appear.
The act provided in subst~nce that a bank should be established, with a capital stock of not exceeding
$10,000,000, divided into 25 1000 shares at $400 each. Subscriptions were to be payable one-fourth in gold or sil\•er
and three-fourths in that part of the public debt which bore 6 per cent. interest. The total amount of debts which
the corporation might 11t any time owe in any way, except for moneys actually deposited in the bank for safo keeping,
was never to exceed $10,000,000, and if' this limit was exceedecl the directors under whose administration it mig·ht
lHl})pen were to be personally liable for the excess.
,
The corporation was allowed to sell tlie evidences of the public debt subscribed to its stock, but was not to
purchase any public debt whatever. Notes were allowed to be issued, payable to ~tny person or persons, assigmtble
and negotiable, or to bearer assignable by delivery. The directors were to establish offices, for discount and deposit
only, wherever they should think fit in the Unitecl States. A report oft.be c01H1ition of the bank was to be furnishecl
whenever the Secretary of the Treasury required it, but not oftener than once a week. The President of the United
States was authorized to subscribe to the stock of the corporation $2,000,000, borrowing an equal sum of the bauk,
the money to be paid otit of that which should be obtained under previous acts of Congi:ess relating to the 1mblic
debt. The charter was to expire March 4, 1811.
A supplementary act, approved March 2, 1791 (1 Statutes, 196), extendecl the time for receiving subscriptions in
the stock of the United States, and limited the number of shares that might be subscribed by any person in any 01.ie
day to thirty.
The subscription of $2,000,000 to the stock of the bank by the President of the United States, under the
authorit.y of the act, was made, giving to the bank, J nne 25 and J nly 16, 1792, bills of exchange on Hollan cl,
equivalent to gold, and borrowing from the bank $2,000,000 for ten years at 6 per cent. interest.
The bank must have gone into operation Yery soon after the charter was .obtained, and before the subscription
of $2,0001000 was made by the Unitecl States, as a dividend of 4 per cent. was declared in July, 1792. So far as
the evidence goes, it appears that the bank was well managed, ancl was of great benefit to the government and the
people at large, assistiug the government b:y: loans in cases of emergency, and forcing the "wild-cat" banks of' the
country to keep their issues somewhere within reasonable bounds. More than $100,000,000 of government money
was received and disbursed by it without the loss of a single dollar. It made semi-annual dividends, averaging
about 8:} per cent., and its stock rose to a bigh i1ricc. The $2,000,000 of stock belonging to the Unitecl States was
sold out at different times at a profit, 2,220·shares sold in J802 bringing an advance of 45 per cent. The government
subscription, with ten years' interest, amounted to $3,200 1000, while there was received in dividends ancl for stock
sold $3,773,1380, a profit of $573,580, or nearly 28.7 per cent. As this transaotion was subsequently made the
subject of investigation by a committee of the Honse of Representatives, a full explanation of the method devisecl
to carry out the provisions of the act 11Utborizing a subscription loan, taken from official reports, is made. a part of
this paper.

TEMPORARY LOAN OF 1793.
The condition of the treasury at the close of the year 1702 was unsatisfactory. The increased expenditures,
on ncco1mt of Indian troubles, and the large amount requisite for the payment of the interest on the public debt,
added to the growing demands for current service, seemed likely to exhaust the revenues, and it was evillent
that some additional resources must be provided to meet the emergency. The House of Hepresentatives, by
resolutions of November 21ancl22, 1792, referred to the Secretary of the Treaslll'y the subject of providing a. l>hm
for the redemption of the public debt according to the rights of the government then existing, ancl also for the
reimbursement of the subscription loan made of the Bank of the United States, according to the provisions of the
law authorizing it.
In reply thereto the Secretary, in his report of the 30th of November, submitted such reeommenclatious as, in
his judgment, would result in the accomplishment of the object desired.
For the first, temporary loans from time to time, equal to the sums anmmlly redee111al1le, secured by a pledge
of the same revenues then appro1)l'iatecl for the payment of interest and the establishment of additional revenues
by taxation, were the only resources rnmaining. For the second, that power be given by htw to borl'ow the sum
due, to be applied to the reimbursement of the loan, as required by the eleYenth section of the act of incorporation,
and that so much of the dividend, on the stock of the government in the bank, us was necessm'y be appro11ril1ted
for the payment of the interest on the sum to be borrowed, as by this operation a saving to the government would
result equal to the difference between the interest on the new loan and thn.t payable on the sum due the bank at
that time.* In conformity with these recommendations, section 3 of the act approved February 28, 1703 (1 Statutes,
328), "making appropriation for the support of the government fo~· 1703," g-ranted the President authority to
borrow the sum of $800,000, at an interest not exceeding 5 per cent., r0imbursable at the pleasure of the United
States, to be applied for the purposes specified in the act; and also in·ovided for the redem1)tion of the loan made
of the Bank of t.he United States, as authorized by section 11 of tho act by which it was incorporated, in sums of
$50,000, from time to time, as, in tho opinion of the Secretary, the state of the, treasnry woul(l admit.
The contract for this loan wa::i made May 31, 1703, the mori0y to be advancocl in four eqnal monthly iustallrnents
from .June 1, ancl to bea,r interest at 5 per cent. from the date of ::inch adyance.

TEl\IPOH.A.RY LOAN OF MA.ROH, 1794.
On the 5th of February, 1794, the Secretary of the Treasury submitted a report to the Honse of Representatives, from which it appeared that the probable demands upon the treasury to the 1st of April wonl<l amount to
$1,360,812 07, while the available resources for the same time would probably fall short of this amount $621,29418.t
The report was referred to a committee, with imitructions to submit to the House whether any, and what, stun
would be necessary to be loaned for the purpose of carrying on the public service fo;e the year 1794. In tbe debate
which ensued upon the report of the committee, which was, that, in their opinion, it was expedient that the
President be authorized to borrow, on the credit of the United States, a sum not exceeding $1,000,000, if, in his
opinion, the public service shoukl require it, it was stated: "That, however inadequate the actual receipts of the
revenue might be to discharge all the demands made on the government, there was, in fact, no deficiency in the
funds appropriated to the (Uscharge of those demands; the deficit was owing merely to the credit, which, for the
convenience and benefit of trade, was allowed to the merchants; and that, in fact, the bonded duties were more
than equal to meet all the demands on the treasury. This credit it woulcl be remembered extended from four
months to two years''; * * * "while it was true that all the demands on the treasury might not be made at the
precise moment they became due," * * * '~and that the estimated deficiency might not be justified by events, yet
it was the duty of the legislatlU'e to put tlie preservation of the public credit almost beyond the reach of the
possibility of being injured."+ The report of the select committee was agreed to, and a bill in conformity thereto
having been reported to the House March 3, and, passing both houses, it was approved by the President March
20, 1794 (1 Statutes, 345). The act authorizeli the President to borrow, if in his opinion the public service should
require it, a sum not exceeding $1,000,000, at an interest not exceeding 5 per cent., reimbursable at the pleasure
of the United States, to be applied to such public purposes as were authorized by law, to be repaid out of the
duties on imports ancl tonnage to the encl of the year 1794. This loan was obtained from the Bank of the Unitccl
States.
TEMPORARY LO.AN OF JUNE, 1794.
lhe report of the committee recommending a naval armament for the protection of American commerce against
the .Algerines, was, after some moclification, finally adopted, and, having passed both houses, was approved, and
became a law March 27, 1794 (1 Statutes, 350). Although it authorized the President "to provide, by purchase or
otherwise, equip, and employ four ships to carry forty-four guns each, and two ships to carry thirty-six guns each",
yet there was no appropriation to pay for the same contained in the act; and in order to remedy this defect, ancl
guard a~ainst any deficiency in the revenues, the President was authorized by section 2 of the act of June o, 1794
(1 Statutes, 395), to borrow the sum of $1,000,000. Under this authority, a loan of this amount was obtained from
the Bank of the United States, at 5 per cent. interest. The money was received August 27, 1794, and was re1mid
April 1, 1795.
TEMPORARY LOAN OF DEOEMBER, 1794.
Such were the exigencies of the public service at the commencement of the last session of the Thircl Congress
that means .had to be provided in anticipation of the revenues of the ensuing year, and on the 8th of December,
1794, a committee was appointed by the House of Representatives to prepare and bring in a bill to authorize the
President to procure a loan not exceeding $2,000,000. The hill was reported the next day, and passed the House
on the 10th, apparently without debate. It became a law December 18, 1794 (1 Statutes, 404), ancl under its
provisions the P1·esident was authorized to borrow any sum not exceeding $2,000,000, at 5 per cent. interest,
reimbursable at the pleasure of the United States, to be applied to such public purposes as were authorized by law,
and repaid out of the duties on imports and tonnage for the year 1795. A loan of $2,000,000 was obtained from
the Bank of the United States under this act, $1,000,000 of which became payable January 1, and the remainder
April 1, 1796.

TEMPORARY LOAN OF FEBRUARY, 1795.
On the 4th of February, 1795, the President transmitted to Congress a special message, with a letter from the
Secretary of State, relative to an additional loan for the maintenance of the intercourse of the United States with
foreign nations, the object of this communication being for the purpose of' securing aclditional authority for the
negotiation of a loan, requisite at the earliest possible date, in order to procure the release of American citizens held
·as prisoners in .Algiers. The authority asked for was granted, and by an fict approved February 21, 1795 (1 Statutes
418), the Bank of the United States was authorizecl to lend to the government the whole, or any part, of th~
sum of $800,000 (remaining unappliecl), in pursuance of the authority granted by the act of March 20, 1794 (1
Statutes, 345). Under this authority, a loan of $800,000 was procured from the Bank of the United States, at 6
per cent. interest, payable quarterly, the principal to be refunded in four annual installments of $200,000 each,
payable December 31, 1796, 1797, 1798, and 1799. This lon,n was received from the bank in G per cent. stock of
the United States, which was transmitted to Messrs .. Baring & Oo., of London, to be sold on account of the
government, and the proceeds held by them subject to the order of the minister of the United States at Lisbon.
With regard to this operation, Secretary Wolcott says : *
A conviction of the urgency of tho case, and a disposition to accommodate tho government, alone induce the bank to consent to tho
loan, as the stock was salable in large quantities at par, including interest. Bills of exchange were not readily obtainable, and the sudden
exportation of so considerable a sum of specie woul(l have been attended with inconvenient effects. Indeed, no alternative o:fl'erecl but to
renounce the negotiation, or to remit stock ns a fund. Various causes operated to produce a depression of the prices of all kinds of public
stock, soon after the remittances, harl been made.

TEMPORARY LO.AN OF MARCH, 1795, A.
On the 26th of December, 1795, the Secretary of the Treasury reported to the commissioners of the smkrng

at pleasure, after giving one month's notice to the bank of•its intention to do so.t This loan was made under the
authority granted by the act of March 3, 1795 (1 Statutes, 433).
TEMPORARY LO.AN OF MARCH, 1795, B.
Under the provisions of s1::ction 6 of the act of March 3, 1795 (1 Statutes, 439), the Secretary of the Treasury
. was authorized by the President to negotiate a temporary loan of $500,000, in anticipation of the revenues for that

FOUR AND A HALF AND FIVE AND A HALF PER OENT. STOOKS OF 17!:15.
The Secretary of the Treasury, in his report embodying a plan "on the basis of the actual revenues for the fnrt.lwr
support of public credit", communicated to the Senate January 20, 1795, estimated the total debt of the United

In accordance with. these suggestions, a bill "making further provision for the su11port or public credit" was
introduced in the House of Representatives, February 14, 1795. The bill was under discussion for several clays,
but the debate was gcnerall;y confined to the sections respecting the domestic debt. A motion was made to strike
out the section proposing an additional half per cent. to the holders of the foreign debt, if they subscribed the same
to the domestic debt, on the ground that nothing would be saved, as the half per cent. would amount to more than
the amount lost in exchange on remittances .to Europe. It was said that a single concern in Holland were the
directors of the chief' of our loans in Europe, receiving, as interest alone, between five and six hundred thousand
dollars annually, aml that the command by one firm of such eno1mous sums of mOIJ<'Y to be drawn, as the bill
proposed, quarterly from the treasury, wonk! be dangerous to the trade of the country, forcing tile banks to narrow
their discounts, and causing stringency in the money market. After debttte, the motion was withdrawn, ~ind the
bill passed both houses, and was approved March 3, 1795 (1 Sta,tutes, 433).
The section relating to the foreign debt provided that a loan be openell at the treasnr:v to the full amount or
the foreign debt, and that subscriptions to said loan should be payable in equal sums of the l.1rincipal of the foreign '
debt, stock to be issued to bear an interest of one-half per cent. greater than was then paJTable on the stock
subscribed. The principal of the stock issued was to be reimb;usable at the pleasure of the United States.
It was found impossible to effect the conversion of the Holland loans. Oliver Wolcott, jr., Secretary of the
Treasury, in his report, December 14, 1795, says iu reference to this subject:*
Measures have been taken for ascertaining whether tho residue of the foreign debt, due inAmst'erdam and Antwerp, could be reloanetl
with the consent of tho c.reditors. Tho experiment cannot yet bo considered as fully made, but tho prospect of success is not encouraging.
The objections against a mutation of the contracts in the manner proposed, which have been statecl, are, generally:
1. That the proposed new stock will bo redeemable at the pleasure of the Unitecl States, by which condition the government will
possess the power 'of recluciug the rate of. interest 01• rcfuncliug the c11pital at periocls when the reimbursement may be difficult or
disadvantageous to the creditors.
2. That the proposed aclclition of .one-half per centum per annum to the rates of interest, docs not afford an equivalent for t;he
expenses of the agency, loss upon the oxcliange, and the risk of remittances from America.
3. That the facilities which attencl tho negotiation of bonds iiayable to bearer, over fnncls which can only bo transferred at tlm
treasury or loan offices in tho United States, render the q.bligations of the foreign debt, in their present form, part.icnlarly eligible fol' foreign
crecli tors. • • •
Considering the actual state of Holland and the restrictions upon our intercourse with that country, it was deemed for the interest of
the United States to propose a post11onemont of the installment which fell due in the present year. It has, however, been conclusively
ascertained that no adclitional loans can be obtained in Holland; and of course the United States must be i1rovided to make the
reimbnrsements sti1mlated in their contracts.
The measures acloptecl by this department to effect a reimbursement were seasonable, and such as promised to be effectual, but, owing
solely to the war, and the stagnation of credit occasioned by the Revolution, the installment which fell due on the 1st of June had not
been paicl, at the date of the last advices in August. The causes of the clelay were, however, well understood by the creditors, ancl as
they neither originated with, nor could be controllecl by, the Unitecl States, and as the interest has been paid, the public credit has yet
sufi'ered no blemish.

The "actual state of Holland", referred to by the Secretary, was the fact that, after a disastrous war with
the republic of France, the country had been conquered by the enemy. The numerous ri.vers and canals of Holland
render the country easily defensible, and have al ways made its invasion a difficult task, but in the winter of 1794-'95t
during intensely cold weather, the watery defenses were frozen, and the French army, under comman~l of General
Pichegru, crossing on the ice in overpowering strength, were soon in possession of the kingdom.
··
The foreign debt not payable in Holland was due to the gover:nment of France, and the revolutionary
authorities of that conntry proved easier to deal with in the conversion than the private holders of the Holland
loans. The terms of conversion provided for in the act of March 3, 1795, were accepted by .James Swan, the
authorized agent of France, and the full amount of the debt due that country, consisting of balances due on
·various loan accounts, was subscribed and paid for in the new stock. The a.mount so subscribed was $2,024,900,
which, by the original contracts, bore interest, part at 4 and part. at 5 per cent. Certificates of stock were
therefore issued as provided for in the act, bearing interest at one-half per cent. more than had been previously
paid on the amount subscribed. Of these certificates, $176,000 bore interest at 4~, and $1,848,900 at 5~ per cent.
The issue of these certificates closed the accounts of the French debt, and it became merged in the domestic
debt of the United States. A small amount of this stock was received for lands in 1797. Redemption of the
remainder began in thQ ,year 1807, and was completed in 1815.

SIX PER CENT. STOCK OF 1796.

Under the act of May 31 1796 an abortive attempt was made by the government to borrow $5,0001000 for
the purpose of paying a debt clue th~Bank of the United States. The bank, since.its charter in 1791, had made many
temporary loans to the government in cases of emergency, and in anticipation of the revenues. In 1796 the United
States owed the bank for loans $6,000.000, and complaints were made by the president and directors that so large a
debt crippled tlle operations of the b~nk, caused them to refuse discounts to their private customers, and created n
.perpetual stringency in tbe money market. It was a further subject of complaint tllat ~be government paid but 5 and G
per cent. for these loans, while the current rate of interest was 8 or 0. A part of this money had been borrowed to
pav the expenses of suppressing the whisky insurrection in Pennsylvania, and another large sum had been used
to~buy a treaty with the iiirates of .Algiers. The government had yet to learn that iron ancl steel, backed by
gunpowder, were more efficient metals tban gold and silver in negotiating treatie.s with the barbarians of northern
.Africa.
The subject of making provision for tlle debts due the Bank of the United States was considered in committee
-Of the whole .April 12, 1'796, and was under discussion for many days, the debate covering the whole subject of the
national debt, tlle operations of the bank, and the propriety or the possibility of raising money by a new loan at Gper
cent. when the current rate of money was so much higher. A committee was appointed to confer with the officers
-0f the bank on the subject of continuing to a remote period the loans macle by the bank to the United States, and
this r,ommittee reported, May 3, the answer of the bank as follows:*
The board took into consideration the most essential points that bad relation to the present subject, viz: the great increase in the
price of all alienable property, which requires a corresponding addition of circulating medium to represent it; tbe necessity of placing
tb's institution in a more respectable situaHon, in point of available funds, which will enable it to promote more generally the interests of
-commerce and manufactures, and afford the means of facilitating the financial operations of the government by temporary loans, whenever
the fiscal administration may require such a resource; as well as the more immediate advantages of the stockholders and euat6roers of the
'bank, intimately connected with the active employment of a large specie capital: Whereupon,
Reaolved; That the United States be requested to extinguish the loans that are already due to the bank, as well as to make provision
for those which may become payable in the course of the present year.

This report occasioned another long debate, in which the proposition was made to sell the stock held in the
bank, and a provision was finally inserted to. allow its sale, or a portion of it, if deemed advantageous by the
.commissioners of the sinking fund.
The bill passed both houses, and was approved May 31, 1796 (1 Statutes, 488) .. It provided that it should be
Iawful for the commissioners of the sinking fund, with the approbation of the President of the United States, to
borrow any sum not exceeding $5,000,000, to be applied to the payment of the principal o.f any part of the debt
.of the United States due or to become due during the year 1796 to the·Bank of the United States or the Bank of New
York, and to pay any installment of the foreign debt. Certificates were to be issued for the money borrowed, to bear
.an interest of 6 ·per cent., the principal to remain fixed and irredeemable until the year 1819, then to be redeemable at
the pleasure of the United States. No certificate was to be issued for any less sum than $100. The commissioners
were to be ailowed t-0 sell these certificates to raise the money, but no more than one moiety of the stock was to be sold
under par. They might, if deemed advantageous, raise part of the money by selling the bank stock belonging t-0
the government.
·
Such portion of the public revenues as were heretofore pledged for the payment of the principal and interest of
certain debts, and which would be set free by their payment under this act, were pledged for the payment of the
interest and the reimbursement of the principal of the new stock.
The attempt t& borrow money under this act was almost a total failure. Only $80,000 were obtained, at a discount
of 12.Z per cent., yielding in cash. $70,000. It was evident that the market was already overloaded with United
States stocks, there being of the demestic debt afloat $63,216 1238 121 which was selling at a heavy discount. There
being no other immediate resource, United States Bank stock to the amount of $1 1304 260 was sold at a premium of 25
per cent., and with this a portion of the debt clue the bank was paid.

NAVY SIX PER CENT. STOOK.

In 1797 the United States possessed neither navy nor navy department. During the trollbles with the Barbary ·
Powers, in 1794, an attempt was made to create a small navy, by building six frigates four of forty-four guns ancl
two of thirty-six guns each, for the protection of our commerce in the Mediterranea~. These frigates were begun
nndel? many disadvantages-one each at the ports of Portsmouth, in New Hampshire Portsmouth in Viro'iniu Boston
New York, Philadelphia, and Baltimore. The t~mber from which they were to b~ con~tructed was :till ~tandin~
in the forest, the iron was still in its native ore, the seed for the flax and hemp was yet to be sown. t ·Copper could
not be procured in the United States. The government possessed neither navy-yards nor cannon foundries. It

was resolved to puild the ships of live-oak, and agents, with a force of lumbermen, were dispatched to Georgfa to

and other causes, that but slow progress was made. In January, 1796, a committee of the Rouse of Representatives

have a navy, and it was resolved to complete the frigates, to build galleys for the defense of the coast, and to purchase

manifested itself at different times to taking any steps whatever for the creation of a, navy.
The act supplementary to the act to provide an additional armament for the i)rotection of the trade of the
United States was approved June 30, 1798 (1 Statutes, 575). It authorized the ;J?resiclent to accept such vessels,

and issuing stock in payment therefor seems to have originated with Alexander Hamilton.*
EIGHT PER CENT. LOAN OF 1798.

In the years 1797 and 1798 the Unitecl States, though nominally at peace with all the world, was actually at
war with France, a war not formally declared, but carried on, upon the ocean, with very great virulence.
Oonstant captures were being made of American vessels on the ground of having the pro11erty of the enemy on
board. When captured, these vessels were carried into French 110rts and condemned on the slightest pretext. The
directory ordered that Americans found serving on ·board hostile armed vessels should be treated as J?irates, even
although they might plead compulsion in excuse. In other words, American citizens impressed by the British were
made liable to be hanged by the French.
a John Marshall, Elbridge Gerry, and Oharles 0. Pinckney were appointed envoys extraordinary to the French
ftlpublic. Their letters of credence and full powers cleclared them to have been appointed for terminating all
differences and restoring harmony, good understanding, and commercial and friendly intercourse between the two
republics. Their efforts, however, were vain.
Preparations were now made in the United States to resist a French invasion. The President was authorizecl
to call out eighty thousand militia in case of need, the regiments of regulars in service were ordered to be raised
to their full complement of seven hundred men each, ancl authority was given to raise twelve additional regiments
of infantry, with six troops of dragoons, to serve during the existing clifficulties with France. Bills were passed
for the purchase or lease of cannon foundries and the procuring of additional armecl vessels, with a,ppropriations
to the amount of $1, 750,000 for the purchase of ships, cannon, small-arms, and military stores. For the manage·
ment of naval affairs a separate naval department was now, for·the first time, created. Merchant vessels were
antllo1'ized to arm for their own defense, and commanclers of ships of war were orde1·ed to capture and bring into
port any French ship of war or privateer found depredating on American commerce.
It was evident that the ordinary revenues of the country would be inadequate for the increased ex1}encliture,
and that a loan would be required. On the 19th of June, 1798, a bill was introduced to enable the President to
i·aise a loan, which was read twice and referred to a committee. It came up for dis~ussion in the House on the 25th
of June. There appears to have been no dispute as to the necessity of a loan, but much objection was made to the
fact that the bill :fixed neither the amount to be raised, the interest to be paid, nor the time at which the loan
should be redeemaOle. It was said thait it would be impossible to spend a very large sum during the recess of
Oong.ress, and tho,t if a defin'.ite sum was fixed and more were needed, in consequence of a sudden invasion, Oongress
could be immediately called together. On the question of the amount of interest, Mr. Gallatin said that it would
be in· the power of money-lenders and the Bank of the United States to combine and compel the Sec1·etary of the
Treasury to pay a much higher interest than would be possible if the interest was fixed by law. Mr. Livingston,
of New York, said there was a probability, if the rate was not fixed, that we should have to pay a much higher
mte. He believed moneyed men might so combine as to extort 10 or even 20 per cent. for money.
On the other hand it was said that it was impossible to tell what sums might be wanted for the public service;
that the President and Secretary coulcl safely be trusted to b~rrow no more money th3(n was needed, and at a rate

.A curious circumstance took place in connection with this loan, being, it is believed, the only case in all the
financial history of the government of a successful attempt at a fraudulent issue of United States Atock. 'l'he
subscriptions to this loan were receiveu in cash at the different loan offices, the Bank of the United States, and the
treasury and scrip certificates issued for the money, which certificates were afterward surrendered at tho treasury,
and 8 pe~ cent. stock issued therefor. .After all the scrip certificates had been funded in this way, it was discovered,
in the vear 1805 that although only $5,000,000 in cash had been received, yet stock to tlle arnonni; of $5,001,000 had
,,
'
been issued, being an overissue of $1,000. .After a long investigation it seemed to be conclusively proved thu,t tt
certain Charles Tompkins, who had been a clerk in the register's office :f):oni 1700 to 1802, a.nd who had chargo
of the issue of the 8 per cent. stock, had fraudulently taken ten scrip certificates for $100 each from their phi.co
in the files of the register's office; had altered the abstract of certificates filed so as to conceal the theft; aud then,
presenting the certificates at the office in his own name, as agent for Clement Biddle, [li banker of very high stlmding
in Philat.lelphia, hacl obtained au order for tlle issue of the stock; had himself issned it under tllc order; nntl had
obtained from l\fr. Bidule $1,000, and 8uG as premium. It was suggestecl by Joseph Nourse, the register, that
Tompkins be arrested, but this was not done at the time, perhaps because the evidence, though conclusive to tw
accountant, hardly amounted to legal proof'. The United States redeemed the $1,000 of fraudulently issued stoek
as it did its genuine issues, and for a time the matter was at"rest.
In September, 1811, however, a new and more alarming feature of the case was developed. A certificate f<t .
$15,000 United States deferred 6 per cents, bearing the genuine signature of Joseph Nourse, register of tho
trea:mry, but of which tlle :filling np and marks of issue hacl evidently been forged, was fomvnrded to the treasury
b,y .Archibald Gracie & Sons, of New York. This was speedily followed by the inform11tiou that another for $30,000,
also forged, was in the possession of David. Parish, of Philadelphia, who had. imrchasecl it in Lorit.lou. '!'hose
certificates had both been sold in London by James Tompkins, a son of Charles Tompkins. On examination of tho
books at the treasury, it was found that out of a book of blank deferred 6 per cents, which llad been kept u.t the
treasury and had been in Oliarles Tompkins' care,. ten signed certificates were missing. It appears to have been iillo
custom of the register to sign the certificates of stock in blank, in quantities, and leave them in the cha,rgo of the
confidential clerk till wanted for issue. Information was soon received that James Tompkins had a coadjutor in the
fraud by the name of Thomas Joy, i:tlias Harrison." It was also ascertained that both James Tompkins and Joy 11ttcl
sailed from Liverpool for the United States, and might be expected early in November. There was an immediate
and determined effort made to capture them. on their arrival, before they could set foot· ou shore. It turned out
a~terwarcl that Joy and Tompkins were on the way over in the ship Justina, which James Tompkins had purchased
with part of the proceeds of the forged certificates, and had placed Joy in command. Ou the 9th of November the
Justina came intoNewYork harbor, under the guns of the Wasp, and Tompkins and Joy were immediately arrested.
Joy, on being examined, denielleverything, but Tompkins made a full confession. He said that he had received tho
ten blanks, ~ignec\by the register of the treasury, from. his father, Charles Tompkins; hacl himself filled up two of
them, oi;e with $!0,000 ~nd the other with $30,000; and had destroyed the rest, except one which he still retainecl.
He aclnutted havmg sol~ the forged certificates in London, receiving therefor £7,508 7s. 6il. sterling; hacl expenclod
about £4,000 on the J ustma; had loaned £3,000 in Liverpool on real estate security· had sent £300 to his mother and
expended a small amount in different ways. The father, Charles Tompkins, was 'now arrested and the throe 'were
fully com~ttecl. Th.e trial of the Tompkins', father and son, for forgery, came off in May, 1s12. James Tompkins
:vas convJCted, but his father was acquitted, in fact could not be convicted, of forgery, as he had not committe(l
it. Ile seems to have been careful through the whole tr;msaction to do but little that might bring him within the
t,rrasp of the l~w, The case of young Tompkins seems to have excited some commiseration. He was pardoned in
the course of a few months, ancl Joy was set at liberty, Mr. Dallas, who managed the cases for the government,

EIGHT PER CENT. LOAN OF 1800.
The committee of ways and means of t.he House of Representatives reported, February 28, 1800, that the
expenditure for the coming year was estimated at the sum of $13,793,034 11, while tlle estimated re\Tenue would be

aml it was therefore recommended that the President be authorized to borrow, for the service of the year, a sum not
exceeding $3,500,000. This deficit in the revenues was caused by the extensiye preparations for war with France,
during the years 1798 and 1799, in which the United States, possessing at the time neither army nor navy, hacl beeu
obliged to provide both, to meet a threatened invasion.
The bill to authorize the President to borrow $3,500,000 for the im1'1.ic service passed the House JYiarch 18 and

I10UISIANA SIX PER CENT. STOCK.
This loan was contracted to pay France for the province of Louisiana, ceded to the United States by that
power April 30, 1803. According to the construction of the United States, the cession of' France included all the
territor.v now coveretl by those portions of the states of Ahtbarna and Mississippi which lie south of the thirty.first
parallel; by the states of Louisiana, Arkansas, l\iissom:i, Iowa, Minnesota, Oregon, Nebraska, and Kansas; by the
territories of Du,kota, J\fontaua, Idaho, Washing ton, and the Indian territory ; i1nd by portions of Oolorado and
Wyoming. The Unitecl States had heavy demands on France for spoliations c<;munittecl on American commerce
dming the previous ten years. The amount of these claims was estimated at $5,000,000. The flrs.t proposition of
t.he French minister was that the Unite<~ Sttites should pay, for the province of Louisimm, 100,000,000 francs and
take upon themselyes the ptiymont of the claims for spoliations, but the amount was finally fixed at $15,000,ooo, of
which France was to receive $11,250,000 in United States bonds, payable in iifteen years, and bearing interest at
the rate of 0 per cent. The remainder, amonnting to $3,750,000, was to be devoted to reimbursing American citizens
for French <lepredations ou their commerce. The trmity was confirmed by the Senate of the United States, but was
the occasion of an extended deb~ite in the Rouse of Representatives.
The act to issue the stock in payment for the territory, which became known as the Louisiana 8tock, was
approved November 10, 1803 (2 Statutes, 245). It provided that, for the purpose of carrying into effect the
convention of April 30, 1803, the Secretary of the Treasury shouJcl issue, in favor of the French republic or its
assignees, certificates of stock for the sum of $11,250,000, bearing an interest of six per cent. per annum from the
time at which possession of Louisiana might be obtained in conformity with the treaty, the certificates to be delivered
by the President to the government of' France, or to such r)ersons as should be authorized to receive them, within
three mouths after Louisiana should be taken possession of in the name of' the government of the United States.
The faith of the United States was pledged for the payment of the interest ancl the reimbursement of the
principal in conformity with the provisions of the convention with France. The convention providecl that the
interest ~hould be payable half-yearly, in London, Amsterdam, or Paris, and that the stock should be.reimbursed
in annual payments of not less than three millions each 1 the first payment to commence fifteen years after the elate
of the exchange of ratifications. The act, however, provided that the Secretary of the Treasury might consent
to discharge the stock in. four equal annual installments, and also to shorten the time fixed by the conven~ion for
commencing reimbursement. The annual interest, payable in Europe, was to be paid at the rate of four sllillings
and sixpence sterling for each dollar payable in Lonclon, and of two and a half guilders for each dollar pa.yable in
.Amsterdam. Sufficient money to pay the interest for the first year was appropriated by the act. An annual
sum of $700,000 (in addition to the annuai sinking fund of $7,300,000), payable out of t~e duties on .merc~anclise
anc! tonnage, was appropriated, to continue so appropriated until the whole debt of the Umted States, mcludmg the
stock created by the aet, was paid.

EXCHANGED AND CONVERTED SIX PER CENT. STOCKS OE' 1807.
It appeared by the report of Albert Gallatin, Secretary of the Treasury, Jn,nuary 20, 1806, that ther? romained
outstanding: of the old 6 per cent. and deferred stocks about $31,800,000, and of the 3 per cents (nommal value)
about $19,050,000-total, $50,850,000. Of these stocks the G per cent. and defen:ed were redeemable _at the rate ot
2 per cent. per annum, each holder receiving, on e:ery $100 worth of stock, $6 for.mterest ~n:l $2 as ~ennbur~ement.
This mode of payment hatl in effect, tliougll not m terms, converted the stocks mto annuities, ter1mnable in from
twelve to eighteen years later. Under this arrangement these stocks sold in England, where a very large proportion
-of them was held, at about 95 per cent. on the unredeemed amount of each certificate, while tho 3 per cents, which
were not redeemable at any specific time, were worth in market about GO per cent. of their nomiual value.
J\Ir. GqJlatin proposed that, in exchange for these three stocks, a common 6 per cent. stock should be offered to
the public creditors; the old G pe1• cent. and deferred stocks to be received at the par value of the unredeemed
amount, and the 3 per cents at about 60 per cent.
.
.
. .
J\Ir. Gallatin thought the exchange woulcl be advantageous to the crechtors, because, 1st, mstead of rece1vmg,
.as they did, quarter-yearly, a small reimbursement of their capital, which was in effect a long n,nnuity, they would,
if tlley exchanged their stocks, receive the whole amount within a much shorter time; and, 2d, because an annuity
for a term of years is always worth .less in market than its intrinsic or arithmetical value, as exemplifiecl by the
market rate of every lease and every estate less than the absolute fee. For this reason the navy 6 })er cents, which
were redeemable at the pleasure of the United States, had always sold higher in market than the 6 per cent. and
.deferred stocks. He thought the exchange would also be advantageous to the United States, 1st, because the
government would thereby be enabled to reimburse the whole in less than nine ;years, instea,d of eighteen; and,
"2d, because if circumstances should render a resort to new loans necessary, the. terms on 'vhich these could be
-0btained would, in a considerable clegree, depend. on the price of existing stocks. It was therefore desirable that
.that species, the price of which had a tendency to regulate that of all others, should. be as valuable as circumstances
would atlmit, and, as has been stated, the 6 per cent. and deferred stocl~s, being annuities for a term of years, solcl
at less than their intrinsic value, the effect of which had already been felt in the operations connectetl. with the
purchase of Louisiana. A con1ersion of the 3 per cent. stocks, however, could not be so easily effected, they being
worth more in market than a 6 per cent. stock producing the same annual amount, and it would therefore be
:necessary for the United States in converting this stock to make some sacrifices.*
On the 20th of December, 1806, John Randolph, from the committee of ways and means of the House of
nepresentatives, presented a bill for the exchange and. conversion of the stocks, entitled "An act supplementary
to the act entitled an act making provision for the redemption of the whole of the public clebt of the United States".
()n tlie 15th of .January, 1807, the House resolved itself into a committee of the whole on the bill. The only part
of it on which a division was called was on filling the blank in the third section so as Ul entitle the holders of' tho 3
per cent. stock to an amount of the 6 per cent. stock equal to "65" per cent. of the nominal amount tlley held. On
this tho vote was yeas 52, nays 27. The bill was read the third time .January 16, ttncl was opposed by Mr. Alston,
who said he knew of no advantages that would accrue to the United States from the passage of the bill. vVero
the United States to be benefited by this modification of the present debt, or were they in the least interested in it~
It was clear they were not interested in it, for if they were the keen speculator would not come forward to subscribe.
~his was a measure giving to people who 'had already preyed on the interests of the country another advantage.
It held out a strong inducement to all citizens of the United States or of foreign countries who held 3 per cent.
stock to subscribe to the new loan, by giving them 9 per cent. more than the value of their stocks twelve or fifteen
months ago. He had evidence that at that period not more than 56 per cent. could have been obtained for it and
yet the bill proposed to give them 65.
·
'
:Mr. Randolph, who favored the bill, said that nothing seemed plainer than that where two individuals stood
in the relation of debtor and creditor, new arrangements of existing debts might be made to the advantage.of both.
At present the commissioners of the sinking fund were authorized to purchase the old G per cent. ancl deferred
stock at $100 in specie for every $100 of stock, and those stocks were every year partially retired at this ·rate. The
only good, therefore, that the provisions of the bill would do the holders of these stocks would be that the government would pay them at the same rate they were now paying them and at which purchases were now made. As

lf the expenditures for military and naval pmposes could not be reduced, a loan would be necessary to make
up tlrn deficiency. The Secretary stated that he had anticipated this state of things, ancl in his previous annual
report, December 10, 1808, had advised an increase of duties. This measure Congress had not thought proper to
adopt, ancl it was now too late, as the increasecl revenue arising from an increase fa the duties on imports (if made)
would hardly become available llnring the coming year. He stated that if the actual expenditures for military and
naval purposes during the year 1810 equaled those of the year 1809, there would be a deficiency of $3,000,000. He
therefore asked Congress to authorize a loan of $4,000,000, which he estimated would ·cover all expenditures and
leav(l $1,000,000 in the treasury.
It seems to have been thonght in Congress that it was impossible to effect any .important reductions in the
expenditures for the army and navy. Our foreign relations were in a very unsatisfactory state, and the war iu
which we soon after became engaged with Great Britain was already looming in the clistunce. In the discussions
which took place on antho:dzing the loan asked for by the Secretary, every gentleman who spoke assented to the
propriety of placing at the disposal of the government a sum of money fully adequate to meet the estimated
expenditures for the year. All those who spoke against the bill profess~cl to be willing, in a proper manner, to
authorize a loan of' any sum of money necessary to sustain the honor ~f the country, but they contended that the
bill was objectionable because the sum to be raised was not stated on the face of the bill; because tho bill bore a.
deceptive appearance of borrowing money to pay the public debt, when, in fact, it was to meet the ordinary expenses
of the government; because the bill authorized a loan of $5,160,000, being $1,160,000 more than the Secretary of the
Treasury had declared to be necessary; and because no loan ought to be authorized until bills then before the
House, which might effect a reduction in the annual expenditures, were decided on. In reply to these objections it
was urged that the amount autJ.iorizecl to be borrowed was as definitely expressed as though in figures; that there
could be no deception on the face of the bill, for if no debt heretofore contracted was to be paid off, there woulcl not
only be no occasion to borrow, but there woulcl be a large surplus iu the treasury; and that since the estimates
hacl been reported to the House various additional appropriations had been made, and it was impossible to say how
much money might be wanted, but no more would be borrowed than was actually required.
The bill to authorize the loan passecl the·House .April 25, yeas 77, nays 35, and it was ordered that the title be:
"A.n act authorizing a loan of money for a sum not exceeding the amount of' the principal of the public debt
reimbursable during the year 1810.":j: It became a law May 1, 1810 (2 Statutes, 610).

The act provided that tile President of the United States be authorized to borrow, on the credit of the United
States a sum not exceeding the amount of the public debt which would be reimbursed by law during the year 1810.
Tlic futerest was not to exceed 6 per cent., and the loan was to be reimbursable at the pleasure of the United States,
or l:llt such period as might be stipulated by contract, not exceeding six years from January 1, 1.811. The Bank of
the United States was to be allowed to Lend the whole or any part of the sum required, and the Secretary of the
Treasury was to be permitted to raise the money by selling certificates of stock, not under par. The Secretary was
authorized 1 with the approbation of the President, to give the preference, in the subscriptions to be made to the loan
under this act, to the holders of the exchanged 6 per cent. stock createcl by virtue of' the second section of the act
passed February 11, 1807, for an amount not exceeding for each stockholder the amount of the exchanged stock
held by him at the time of subscribing. The sum thus borrowed from the holders of the exchanged stock was to
be reimbursable at the pleasure of the United States. So much of the sinking fund of $8,000,000 as might be
needed for the purpose was pledged for the payment of the interest and the reimbursement of the principal, and
the faith of the United States was pledged to establish sufficient revenues to make up any deficiency in the fund.
Under this bill $2,750,000 was borrowed, reimbursable at the pleasure of the United States, 11nd it wa1:1 all l'eimbursed
during the year 1811.
SIX PER CENT. LOAN OF 1812.
The committee of ways and means of the Rouse of Representatives reported, February 17, 18121 that the
ordinary expenditures for the coming year were estimated at $9,400 1000, while the receipts for the year were
estimated at $8,200,000, leaving a deficit of $1,200,000, which, however, might be paid out oft.he balance remaining
in the treasurjr January 1, 1812, amounting to $3,502,305 80. This would leave a balance of about $2,302,305 80 on
band, which the committee thought it would not be prudent, under existing circumstances, further to exhaust.
'.But it was probable that more than the ordinary expenses for the year would have to be met. It was evident that
war with Great Britain must soon ensue; it was, in fact, declared within four months. Bills had been introduced
into Congress, and several of them had passed, to increase the regular army 25,000 men; to arm and drill the militia;
to purchase ordnance, ordnance stores, and camp equipage; to raise a force of mounted rangers;. to repair the rntvy
and purchase timber to build new ships; to erect additional fortifications; and to call for volunteers. The bills
which had passed or were likely to pass Congress to put the country in readiness for war called for an extraordinary
expenditure of abo11t $11,000,000, and this sum the committee proposed to raise by loan, ancl reported a bill for that
purpose. The bill was considered in the committee of the whole February 24, and seems to have caused but little
debate, passing to its third reading by yeas 92, nays 2(). It was slightly amended in tho Senato, but seems to
have passed that body without much debate. The Rouse concurred in the amendme.nts, ancl the bill passccl ilnally
March 11, and was approved March 14, 1812 (2 Statutes, 694). It authorized the President to borrow, on the
credit of' the United States, $11,000,000, at a rate of interest not exceeding 6 per cent., payn,ble quarter-yearly, the
monej' to be reimbursable, at the pleasure of the United States, at any time after the expiration of twelve years
from January 1, 1813.
Certificates of stock for the sums borrowe<l were to be issued, reimbursable as aforesaid, but no such certificates
were to be sold below par.
By a supplemental act, approved July 6, 1812 (2 Statutes, 784), the Secretary of the Tl·easury was authorized
to employ an agent or agents, for the purpose of' selling any part of this stock, and to allow such agent or agents a
commission on the amount sold not exceeding one-eighth of 1 per cent.
Under these acts the sum of' $1:\,134,700 was obtained, reimbursaJ:>le as provided for in the act, and 11lso a
temporary loan for $2,150,000, reimbursable as providecl for in special contracts with different banks. These loans
have been entirely repaid.

It was the first time since the. formation of the present government that the issue of treasury notes bad beer:

Tlrn bill passed the House June 17, by yeas 85, nays 41; passed the Senate June 26; and was approved June
30, 1812 (2 Statutes, 766). rt· authorized the President to issue treasury notes for such sums as he should deem
expedient, but not exceeding in all $5,000,000. These notes were to be reimbursed by the United States at srich
places, respectively, as. might be expressed on the. face of the notes, one year, respectively, after the day on which
the notes were issued. They were fo bear.interest from the day of issue, at the rate of 5f per cent. a year, payable
at the plac~s and _times respectively designated on the face of said notes for the payment of the principal. They
were to be issneLl m such portions as the President should deem expedient, in payment for supplies and for debts
due by the UniteLl States, to such public creditors as cllose to receive them at par; and the Secretary of the Treasuzy
was authorized to borrow from time to time, not under par, such sums as the President should deem expedient, on
the credit of the notes. They were to be receivable in pa;yment of all duties and taxes laicl by authority of th0
United States and for all public lands. In such payments interest was to be computed at the rate of 12- cent a day
on each $100 in treasnry notes, and each month was to be counted at thirty days.
The commissioners of the sinking fund w13re authorized and directed to cause to be reimbursM and paid the
principal and interest of the notes, at the severa1 times when they sllonld become reimbursable by the provisions
of the act, Rind were further authorized to purchase said notes, not above par, in the sam<i manner as they
pnrc11::1set1 other evidences of the public debt.
There was issued in these notes tlle full amoimt of $5,000,000 which the act authorized.

EXCHANGED SIX PER OENT. STOOK OF 1812.
The Secretary of the Treasury reported to the House of Representatives, June 24, 1812, through the committee
of ways and mealls, that of the loan of $11,000,000, authorized by the act of March 14, 1812, but $6,460,000 had
thus far been subscribed, including $200,000 offered on special contract but not yet accepted. He said: "The
result of the loan was more than doubtful. The old 6 per cent. and deferrecl stocks are 2 or 3 per cent. under
par, and any depression in the public funds would seriously affect the sales of the residue of tlle new loan. Nor
does it appear eligible, without an absolute necessity, to give a premium or additional interest in order to obtain
subscriptions for that residue. For as it would be just in that case to place the first subscribers on the same
footing·, the charge to the public would be more than double the premium actually wanted to obtain the four and
a half millions which are not yet subscribed." He therefore suggested a conversion of the old 6 per cent. and
deferred stocks into a new 6 per cent. stock not materially different from that created by virtue of the act author·
faing the loan of $11,000,000. This he thought would have a favora,ble effect on the price of those stocks, and
thereby facilitate the loan of this year aml prevent the necessity of applying, in this and subsequent years, the
large sums which must otherwise be expended in the reimbursement and purchase of the public debt.*
A bill fo:r· the purpose of e:tfocting a conversion of the old 6 per cent. and deferred stocks, as recommended by Mr.
Gallatin, was considered in committee of the whole July 3, 1812, and was reported witllout amendment. Hon. John
Randolph moved to amend it by striking out the enacting clause, which motion was rejected by a large majority.
Mr. Handolph then moved to amend it by inserting bi proYiso that nothing contained in it should be constrlied to
impair the obligation by which the commissioners of the sinking funcl were bound, under previous acts of Congress,
to apply $8,000,000 annually to the recluction of the public debt. This motion was also rejected. The bill was
. then ordered to be engrossed, and read the third time the same day. It i)assed, apparently without further debate,
by yeas 58, nays 26. It passed tlle Senate without amendment, and was approved July 6, 1812 (2 Statutes, 783).
The act provided that a subscription to the full ·amount of the old 6 per cent. and deferred stocks be proposed
to the proprietors thereof, for which purpose books should be 01)ened, at the treasury ancl the offices of the
commissioners of loans, on the 1st day of October next ensuing, to C'ontfnue open until March 17, 1813, the fourteen
last days of each q narter excepted. Fo; such part of the amount of the old 6 per cent. and deferred stocks subscribed
and surrendered as should remain unredeemed on the day of subscription, the subscribers were to be entitlecl to
receive certificates of United States stocks, bearing interest at 6 per cent. per annum, payable quarter-yearly, from
the first day of the quarter in which the subscription was made. The said certificates were to be reimbursable, at the
pleasure of the United States, n,t any time after December 31, 1824; but no reimbursement was to be made except
for the w'hole amount of stock standing at the time to tlle credit of any proprietor, nor until after at least six
months' public notice of the intended reimbursement. Nothing in the act was to be construed to alter, abridge: or
impair the riO'bts of those creditors of the United States who did not subscribe for the exchanged stock. Under
this act $2,9S4,746 72 were subscribed in old 6 per cent. and deferred stocks and exchanged for certificates of tho
new stock. This stock has all been redeemed.

SEVEN AND ONE-HALF MILLION LOAN.
The acts authorizing a loan of $16,000,000 and an issue of treasury notes for $5,000,000, in the early part of the
year 1813, were intendecl to provide ample means to meet the deficit of $19,000,000, which was expected to result
from the heavy military and naval operations of.the year. ';rhe acting Secretary of the Treasury, W. Jones, in his
report to the Senate, June 3, 1813, estimated the expenditures for the remainder of the year at about $29,230,000,
and the revenue from all somces, inclucli'.ng the loans and treasury notes authorized, at the same amouut. But he
seems to lrnve feared that so large an issue of treasury notes might result in their flepreciation, and he advised a
loan instead, reserving the power to issue treasury notes if the exigencies of the government req_uirecl it. He
says that, in the estimate of resources, "the whole sum of $5,000,000 authorized to be issued in treasury notes is
taken as a part of the resources of the llresent year. But as it is not deemed eligible to increase the amount of
treasury notes in circulation, and as three millions only of those authorized by the act of 1812 were issued in that
year, and are reimbursable in the course of the present year, it is respectfully suggested that, in lieu of issuing two
millions of the five millions authorized by the act of February, 1813, Congress should authorize an additional loan
for tlrn same amount."
In connection with this he recommended the imposition of new internal taxes.t A bill to authorize a loan of
- - - dollars, was reported in the House of Representatives from the committee of way.s and means July 22, and
considered in committee of the whole July 24. The blu.nk in the bill before the word "dollars" was fillecl with the
words "seven million five hundred thousand". Au attempt was made to amend the bill by inserting a provision
pledging the proceeds of the direct tax an(l the internal duties for the payment of the interest of the loan, but the
House voted it down by a large majorit,y. The debates, if any took place on the subject, have not been recorded.
The bill was slightly amended in the Senate, passed both houses July 30, and was approved August 2, 1813 (3
Statutes, 75). It authorized the Presiclent to borrow, on the credit of the Unitecl States, a sum not exceeding
$7,500,000, reimbnrsable at the pleasure of the United States at any time after the ~xpiration of twelve years from
January 1, 18H. It allowed the sale of certificates of stock, for the purpose of raismg the money, at not less than
$88 in money for $100 of stock. The interest was not limited. A commission of not exceeding one-quarter of 1 per
cent. on the amount sold or for which subscriptions were obtained, was to be allowed to agents. It contained the
usual provision directin~ t.he commissioners of the sinking fund to pay the interest and reimburse the principal
when due; also to purchase the stock, not above par, as they purchased other evidences of the.1mbli? debt. The
faith of the United States was pledged to establish sufficient revenues to make up any deficiency m the funds
provided for its reimbursement. Ban~s in the Di:strict of Columbia were to be allowed to lend any part of it,
notwithstandiqg any provision to the contrary in their charters. Under this act $7,500,000 was obtained in cash,
and stock to the ali10tmt of $8,498,581 95 was issue(l therefor, being a discount of 11.75 per cent.

SIX PER CENT. LOA.NS OF 1814.
A bill to authorize a loan of $25,000,000, to provide, together with the treasury notes of March 4, for the
esHmated deficiency of $29,350,000 in the revenues and receipts of the year, was reported from tl10 committee of
ways and means February 1, 1814. This was the largest loan that, up to that time, had ever been proposed in
Congress, and the idea of increasing the debt of the nation by so large au amount created much opposition and led to nn
exciting debate, which took a wide mnge and brought in incidentally the conduct of the war with Great Britain, its
justice, and its probable results. The bonds of 1813 had only been negotiated at a heavy discount, the treasury
receiving for $26,607,959 38 in bonds but $23 1500,000 in cash. This money had been mostly exhausted in disastrous
at.tempts on Canada, which had e:ffectecl nothing, and hacl only resulted in inflicting on both countries the miseries
of border warfare. The only brilliant successes of which the administration could boast were the naval victories.
The bill, after having been before the House for more than a month, finally passed by yeas 97, nays 55. It passed
the Senate without amendment by yeas 22, nays_ 13, March 19, and was approved March 24, 1814 (3 Statutes, lll).
