MISSISSIPPI STOOK.
At the close of the American Revolution Great Britain, by the treaty of September 3, 1783, surrendered all
-claims to sovereignty over the thirteen original colonies. The treaty declared that the Mississippi should be their
. western boundary, and article 1 relinquished to each of the states, naming them, and Georgia among them, "all
.claims to the government, proprietary, and territorial rights of the same." Under this treaty the state of Georgia,
.as the successor of the colony of that name, claimed to hold a perfect and imlefeasible title to all the lands (not
before granted to private parties) within her limits as far west as the Mif;sissippi river, including nearly all
the present states of Alabama and Mississippi. The only adverse claimants were the Imlian tribes, and over
all the soil occupied by them she held the right of pre-emption. In February, 1785, the legililature of Georgia
-0stablished the county of Bourbon, beginning at the mouth of the Yazoo river and running southward along the
Mississippi river to the thirty-first degree of north latitude. In February, 1788, the legislature authorized the
delegates of the state to cede to the United States all her rights of son, territory, and jurisdiction west of the river
Apalachicola; bnt Congress, by resolution July 15, 1788, refused to accept the cession.•
.
The fact of the possession by the state of Georgia of such an immense body of fertile land appears to have
-0arly a,ttmctecl the attention of speculators, and formed the basis of one of the greatest land speculations on record,
commenced (as the evidence seems to show) by bribery tmcl carried on by fraud ancl perjury.
On the 21.st of December, 1789, the legislature of Georgia passed an a.ct authorizing a grant of the whole country
from the Tombigbee to the Mississippi, comprising nearly the whole of the present state of Mississippi and a portion
.of Alabama, amounting to about 30,000,000 acres, to three companies, called the South Carolina Yazoo, the Virginia
Yazoo and the Tennessee companies, for the sum of $207,580, or rather less than one cent per acre. An inconsid. ancl the whole amount
€rable sum was pa,id, in the paper money of the state, by the first two mentioned compames,
of the purchase-money was tendered to the treasurer in the depreciated certificates or evidences of the public debt of
Georght, the acceptance of which was refused. It seems to have been considered that the grant had lapsed by non;p11yment of the purc1rnse-money, for, on the 7th of January, 1795, the legislature passed an act authorizing the sale
-0f nearly the entire country from the Alabama river to the Mississippi, estimatecl at from tbirty-:11ve to :fifty million
acres, to four companies, called the Georgia, the Georgia Mississippi, the Upper Mississippi, and the Tennessee,
for the sum of $500,000, which a.mount was paid into the treasury of the State. t

*Journals of Congress, vol. iv, pp. 834, 835.

VALUATION, TAXATION, AND PUBLIC IN~EBTEDNESS.

Sweeping charges of bribery were made, affecting with greater or less particularity almost every member of the
legislature. Nor is the history of the case confined to such charges involving members of the state senate and of
the house of representatives, with more or less of detail as to the acts and the expressions of lobby agents and of
members. State officials of peculiar and special responsibility in the care of the financial honor of the state were
the objects of like charge& of corruption and bribery.
It was not only the current report and the testimony of witnesi;;es that negroes and land were freely transferre<l
to influence votes, but darker crimes were suspected. A senator, who had borne witness to the corruption and who
was expected to confess complicity in the same, being murdered fa his own house, the murder was attribnterl to tho
motirn, "that he, together with his testimony, might be consigned to oblivion." The llistory of the tmnsaetion
iIJYolves the name of nearly every man at the time connected with the administration of the state government.
While tile popular verdict was that the corruption was almost universal, there were indivicluals who even in
the rumors of the hour kept a clean name before the public, and saved the state from the disgrace of unanimous
dereliction of its legislators and its officials to their trusts.
The alienation of so vast a body of fertile land, under such suspicious circumstances and for so insigniiicant a.
price, appears to bave produced a great excitement among the people of Georgia. A new legislature was chosenr
whose members proceeded, as far as in them lay, to undo the work of their predecessors, by passing, February 13,
17!.HJ, an act "declaring null and yoicl a certain usurped act imssecl by the last legislature of this state, at Augustat
on the 7th tlay of January, 1795, under the pretended title of: An act supplementary to an act entitled an act for
appropriating a part of the unlocatecl territory thereof for the protection of the frontier, and for other purposes".
This act, after declaring the act by which the territory was sold to be unconstitutional, imssecl by usurped nuthority,
fraud, and collusion, declared the whole void, and all grants obtained under it "annulled, renclered void, and of no
effect; and as the same was made without constitutional authority, and fraudulently obtained, it is hereby declared
of no binding force and efl:'ect on this state or the people thereof, but is an cl are to be considered, both law and grants,
as they ought to be, ipso .facto, of themselves void, and the territory therein-mentionecl is also hereby declared to be
the sole property of the state, subject only to the right of treaty of the United States to enable the state to purcluiso,
under its pre.emption right, the Indian title".
All documents and deeds connected with the purchase were ordered to be expunged from the books of record
of the state, and the act itself to be publicly burnt. The governor was directed to return, to those who had bona jlae
paid it, the money received into the treasury for the land.*
"Tllen," says Mr. Troup, of Georgia, in his speech of March 8, 1814, "such a scene of fraud, iniquity, aml
depravity was exhibited, that the governor, familiar as he had been with such scenes but a short time before,.
blushed, and shut the doors of the treasury against them. Men concerned in this transaction, but who had not
deposited one dollar, by perjury, forgery, or some other crime, drew thousands from the treasury".t
On the 24th day of April, 1802, Georgia ceded to the United States all her territory south of the state of
Tennessee and west of the Uhattahoochieriver. By the terms of the cession the United States was to pay Georgia,
out of the first net proceeds of the sales of the land thus ceded, $1,250,000, and to have the right to reserve t>,000,000.
acres, or the proceeds of their sale, for the purpose of satisfying any claims which might be made to any part of the
ceded territory.t

thmg, drsputed, the grantees under the repealed act of January 7, 1795, and purchasers under them, claimed ne11rly
the whole territory. It appeared, on investigation, that the following companies were the claimants and had paid
into the treasury of Georgia, as stated :

The debates on this subject took a very wide range in Congress, and the question was not settled until eleven
years afterward. On the one hand it was alleged that the claimants were entitled to the land, it having been
sold to them by Georgia, and that the attempt of the succeeding legislature to destroy the title was of no effect, as
being directly contrary to the provision of the Constitution which forbids the passage of any law to impair the
obligations of contracts.
On the other hand it was said that there had never been a real sale to these companies; that no contract
existed to be impaired, as fraud vitia.tes all contracts, and no contract existecl in a legal and equitable sense; that
the constitution of the state of Georgia gave her representatives no right to rob and plunder the people; that even
the claimants no longer asserted the validity of the law, but threw themselves on the mercy of Congress; and that
it was evident that, while we might vote a small sum as a gratuity or to quiet the shadow of title, we could not
pretend to purchase, as a reality, a title obtained, as was said, "where the corrnption in which the transaction was
engendered, was a corruption without example in history."
It seems to have been thought by the majority of Congress that the titles of the companies and those claiming
under them, though obtained by fraud, were of sufficient weight to interfere materially witli the settlement of the
Mississippi territory, and it was therefore best to extinguish them by a compromise.
On the 26th of March the bill to compromise with the claimants passed the House of Representatives by yeas
84, nays 76, and became a law March 31""1814 (3 Statutes, 116). It directed certificates of stock, not bearing
interest, payable out of the first money in the treasury of the United States arising from the sale of public lands in
tlle Mississippi territory, after the money clue to the state of Georgia and the expenses of surveying the lands llacl
been satisfied, to be issued to an amount not exceeding $5,00o,ooo, of which not exceeding $4, 750,000 was to go to
the representatives of the companies and $250,000 to the holclers of citizens' rights.
Under this act the Mississippi stock, as it was called, was issued to the amount of $4,282,036 92. This stock
was receivable in part payment for lands in the territory.

Congress had adjourned, in April, to meet the last Monday .in October, more than a month earlier than usual;
but the financial outlook was so alarming that the President summoned them to meet on the 19th of September.

an act to authorize another loan for three millions, which was approved November 15, 1814 (3 Statutes, 144).

STOOK OF 1815.
It will appear from the remarks on the direct tax loan that the estimated deficiency in the revenue of the
nation for the year 1815 was $40,90G,124 86. It was proposed to raise this amount partly by direct taxes on land,
inheritances, legal instruments, fl.our, dividends of banks, sales of stocks of incorporated companies, and incomes,
partly by loans, and partly by new issues of treasury notes.
A bill to authorize the issue of treasury notes for the service of the year was reported in the House of
Representatives Ja.nuary 30, 1815, and referred to a committee of the whole. The bill appears to have passed
through its several stages in both houses without discussion. As :first reported, it proposed an issue of $15,000,000
in notes, redeemable in :five annualinsjallments of $3,000,000 each, for which the land tax was pledged. Connected
with this plari was the intention to propose a loan of $25,000,000. The bill was amended in committee of the whole,
so as to provide for an issue of $251000,000 in treasury notes. This amendment, as reported, was connected with
a proposed loan of $15,000,000, thus reversing the proportion of loan and treasury notes. 'rhe bill passed finally,
with some further slight amendments, and was approved February 24, 1815 (3 Statutes, 213.) News of the signing

At the close of tho last session of Congress the demands on the treasury were interesting in their nature as well as great in their
amount. Exclusive of tho ordinary expenses of tho government, they consisted of demands for the payment of tho army 1 preparu,tory
to its reduction to the peace establishment, with other very heavy arrearages and disbursements i.n the \Var aucl Navy Depu,rtmenta; for

The efficiency of tho me~ns which were possessed for tho liquillation of these demanlls llepeudecl upon circumstances beyond the
-0ontrol of the government. '.l'he bn,lance of money in tho treasury consisted of bank creditB, lying chiefly in the southern aud western
11ections of the Union. 'l'he revenue proceeding from t.he pi:ovision maclo prior to the last session of Congress was, comparatively, of small
amount. The revenue proceecling from tho provision made during tha,t session conlcl not be available for n, great poi:tion of the preaent
year, ancl, in both instnnces, the revenue was 1>ayable in treasury notes, qr it assumecl the form of bank credits, at tho respective places of
-0ollection. The only remaining resources for immediate use were an aclditioual issue of treasury notes and a loan ; but the successful
-0mployment of these i:esources was rendered for some timo uoubtfL1l by the peculinr situation of the credit ancl currency of the nation.
The snspe11siou of s11ecie payments tl1roughout the greater portion of tho Unitecl States ancl the consequent cess11tion of tho interchange
of bnnk notes and bank ci:e11ifo between the institutions of the cliffercnt States, had deprivecl tho treasury of all the facilities of transferring
its funds from place to place; and a proposition which wns made, at an o!Lrly period, to the principal banks of tho commercial cities, on
t.ho line o~ tho Atlantic, with a view in some clct,"l'ee to restore those facilities, coulcl not be effectccl for the want of a concurrence in the
requisite number of banks. Hence it bns happenecl (and the duration of the evil is without nny posit.ive limitation) that, hewevei: aclequaw
t,he public revemrn may be, in its general product, to discharge tho public engagements, it becomes totally inadequate in the process of its
application, since the possession of public fun els in one i)ai:t no longer nfforcls the evidence of n fiscal cu,pacity to discharge a public debt
in any other part of the Union.
From tho suspension of specie imyments nnd from various other causes, real oi: imaginary, differences in the rate of exchange arose
between ·tlie several states an cl oven between tho several clistricts in tho same state; aud the embarrassments of tho treasury were more

the exchange in the tmnsactious of inclividuals, although tho keasnry coulcl only issue them at their par value. 'l'he publfo stock, created

Thv.s, notwithstaucling the ample revenue provided aud permanently pleclgecl for the payment of tho public credit.or, and
notwitbstancling the auspicious influence of peace upon the resources of the nation, tho market price of the trensui:y notes and of the
irnblic stock was everywl1ei:e fai: below its par or true value for a considerable perioc1 aftei: the adjournment of Congress, Tibrating,
however, with a change of place, from the rate of 75 to the rate of 90 per cent. Payments in bank paper were universally preferred during

The Secretary of the Treasury, in his annual report on the state of the finances, laid before the House December
In this.
report he said:*

1,' 1815, advised tlle creation of a United States bank .as a remedy for the evils of a depreciated curreney.

The cstablisbmeut of a natioual hank, authorized to issue notes which will be received ill- all payments to tho United Stu.tos, is regarded

The committee on a national currency having directed their chairman, Hon. John C. Calhoun, to request from
Mr. Dallas his views on certain points relating to the currency, the Secretary, in reply, December 24, 1815, sent ~.
plan for the establishment of a United States bank: with arguments in its favor.
Mr. Calhoun, as chairman of the special committee, reported to the House, January 8, 1816, .a bill to incorpor11tothe subscribers to the Bank of the United States. It was ilrst considered in committee of the whole Febrmtry 20,
and was under discussion for about two weeks. Tlle constitutional questions, which had occupied so much time in
discussing the bill for chartering the first Bank of the United States, received little attention. The debate turned:
principally on the advantages and disadvantages of a national bank, on the details of the bill, and on the question:
whether it was possible, through its aid, t<> colTect the evils of the miserably depreciated paper tha,t t,ben formed
almost the sole currency.
The bill passed the House Mai·ch 14, 1816, by a vote of 80 yeas to 71 nays.
An analysis of the vote shows that it was not as sectional as the vote to charter the first Bank of tbe UniiiedJ
States, and that the locality'of the opposition had changed. A considerable majority of southern members votecl
for the bill, while there was a small majority fro.n the North and East against it.
The debate in the Sen~te was principally confined to some of the details of the bill, and but littlo was s11kl on
the general question, or on its constitntional aspects. It passed the Senate, with some slight amendments, April 3.
The amendments were concurred in by the Honse, and the bill became a law April 10, 1816 (3 Statutes, 269).
It provided that a Bank of the United States should be established, with a capital of $35 1000 1000, di,vi<led into·
350,000 shares of ~!100 each. Seventy thousand of these shares, equivalent to $7,000,000, were to be subscribed
by the United States, payable in coin, or in stock of the United States bearing interest at 5 per cent. i1er anuurn,
redeemable at the ple~tsure of the government. This stock the bank was to have the right to sen for gold 11nd silvercoin or bullion, but not to sell more than $2,000,000 thereof in any one year. Subscriptions were to be opened
on the first Monday of July, 1816, at one place in each state, and at Washington. Subscriptions by indiviclun.ls,
corporations, or states were to be payable one-fourth in gold or silver coin, and three-fourths in the iilnded debt of·
the United States, or in coin, to be paid in three installments, 30 per cent. at the time of subscribing and tho·
balance in six: and twelve months.
For the management of the affairs of the corporation there were to be twenty-five directors, :five of whom (stockholders) were to be appointed by the President of the United States with the advice and consent of the Senate, noii
more than three of the five to be residents of any one state. No person, a director in the bank or its bra.nches .
should be a director of any other bank. Not more than three·fomths of the directors in office at the time of m~
annual election were to be eligible for re-election, nor was any director to hold his office for more than three yen.r::.1.
out of four, except the director who should be president at the time of an annual election who might always be
reappointed or re-elected. No one but a. stockholder, a. resident citizen of the United States could be a director

The corporation was not directly or indirectly to deal in anything except bills of exchange, gold or silver bullion,
goods pledged for money lent, or goods which should be the produce of its lands. It was not at liberty to purchase
any public debt whatever, nor to take more than 6 per cent. per annum for its loans or discounts. An office of
discount and deposit was to be established in the District of Columbia whenever a law of the United States should
require; also one in each state in which 2,000 shares of stock should be subscribed or might be held, whenever, at
the request of the legislature of the state, Oongress might by law require it, and the dii'ectors were to have the
right to establish such offi.c(ls of discount and deposit wheresoever they should think fit, iu the United States or the
territories thereof. The Secretary of the Treasury was to be furnished whenever he required it, but not oftener
than once a week, with statements of the condition of the bank.
The notes of the bauk, payable on demand, were to be received in all payments to the United States. No note
was to be issued of less than $5. The bank was not at any time to suspend or refuse payment, in gold or silver, of
any of its notes, bills, or obligations, nor of moneys received on deposit. The penalty for refusing to pay said ·
obligations or deposits in coin was to be 12 per cent. per annum until fully paid and satisfied. No other bank was
to be established by the authority of the United States during the continuance of the corporation except hanks in
the District of Columbia, with an aggregate capital in said District of not exceeding $6,000,000. In consideration
of tbe exclusive privileges conferred on the corporation by the act, it was to pay to tbe United States a bonus of
$1,500,000, in three equal payments, at the expiration of two, three, and four years.
The government subscription of $7,000,000 authorizell by the act was made in stock, bearing an interest of 5
per cent., redeemable at pleasure, while the remainder of tl,te stock of the bank was eagerly taken by private
parties, and the bank went int.o operation January 1, 1817.
In 1837 and 1838 the stock in the bank belonging to the nation was nearly all sold at a heavy premium, and
the proceeds paid into the United States treasury.
As a financial operation on the part of the government it proved a success, resulting as follows:

FIVE A.ND SIX PER CENT. LO.A.NS OF 1820.
The report of the committee of ways and means of the House of Representatives, April 14, 1820, showed that
there remained in the, treasury, in available funds, on the 1st of Januaryrireceding, only the sum of $245,665 35.
The total amount of expenditures for the year 1820 already authorized by law was $26,2991164 30, while the
estimated revenue was $22,280,000, leaving a deficit of' $4,019,164 30; or, deducting balance in the treasury of
$24.5,u65 35, the deficit for the year would be $3,773,498 95; which might be partially met by using, if thought
proper, the surplus of the sinking fund, amounting to $2,288,497 29, when the actual deficit would be $1,485,001 66.
They therefore reported a bill to authorize a loan of $2,000,000.*
1.'he committee said that, while they hesitated to recommend a, loan, they believed that powerful reasons existed
against a resort to internal duties and direct taxes in a period of profound tranquillity, the nation having been
accustome(l to a system of taxation (through the duties on imports) which, in its operation, had been paid without
being perceptibly felt. They thought that economy and retrenchment in the expenditures of the government had
'become imperatively necessary, especially under the state of things that then existed, when the extraordinary
depression of commerce, the stagnation of our navigation, the depreciation in the value of our exports and of
property of every description, and the serious embarrassments under which all branches of industry labored, had
compelle(l economy and i'etrenchment in the expenditures of every citizen, and had seriously affected the national
· finances. The excess of expenditures over revenue had, however, arisen principally from the heavy payments made
in redemption of the public debt, continued through a series of yeari;. The original sinking fund of $7 ,300,000 per
annnm, established in 1802, had been increased to $8,000,000 in 1803 aud to $10,000,000 per annum in 1817, with
an additional appropriation for that year of $9,000,000. From this fund $32,085, 750 of the public debt had been
redeemed since tlie 1st of January, 1817, and large outstanding claims, amounting to over $30,000,000, resulting
from ~he h1te :Wwr with Great Britain, had been paid.
T
, on the 4th of l\fay, resolved itself into a committee of the whole on the bill, when a debate arose

It was said on the other hand tlmt two millions wonhl be sufficient, and that there could be no violation of the

The bill passed the House in this form; but the Senate attempted to amend it by inserting "fl ve millions" iu
lieu of three. To this tlie House, after debate and the appointment of a committee of conference, refused to agree.
It finally passed both 110uses and was approved .May 15, 1820 (3 Statutes, 582). The bill provided that the President
of the United States be authorized to borrow $3,000,000, at a rate of interest not exceeding 5 per cent. per annum,

sales of the stock. 'Ibe surplus of the sinking fund was pledged for the payment of the interest and the
reimbursement of the principal.
Under this bHl $999,999 13 were borrowed, at 5 per cent. interest, reimbursable after January 1, 1832, and
$2,000,000 at 6 per cent., reimbursable at the pleasure of the United States. On the $2,000,000 a premium of 2 per
cent. was obtained. This stock bas all been redeemed.

FIVE PEH CENT. LOAN OF l 821.
The expenditures for the year 1821 were estimated by tlle Secretary of the Treasury, in his reporb of' December
21, 1820, at $21,208,483 03, and the revenue from all sources at $16,550,000, leaving a deficit of $4,G58,4K3 03 to Lo
provided for by taxation or new loaus.t This expected deficit arose from the fact that a portion of the imblic debt
would llecome clue in the year 1821, the principal and interest of which amounted to $5,477,776 7G.
The Secretary said that, in addition to this deficit, the sum of $1,000,000 should be provided for, so that there
might remain a clear balance of that amount in the treasury at the close of the year, in order to insure the prompt
discharge of all demands and place the public credit beyond the reach of accident. He therefore proposed that
provision should be made for raising tlle sum of $7,000,000 to meet all contingencies.
To raise money b;y additional taxation was almost impossible. The country could pay no more, for it was
still suffering from the effects of the great crisis of 1819. The report of a committee of the House of Representatives,
January rn, 1821, gives a vivid picture of the general distress: t

the markets of agriculture, the llllrsuits of manufactures drruinished ancl declining; commerce struggling, not to retain tho canyfng of
~he produce of other.nations, but our own. Thero is no national interest which is in ti healthful, thriving condition; tho nation at largo
is not so; the operat10ns of the go>ernment und individuals alike labor under difficulties which are folt by all. " " " The sea, tho
f~r~st, tlw earth yielll their abundance; the labor of man is rewardecl; pestilence, famine, or war commit no ravages; no calamity bas
v1s1tccl the people; peace smiles ou us; plenty blesses tho lanc1: "Whence, then, this burst of universal distress'/

EXCHANGED FIVE PER CENT. STOCK OF 1822.
This stock originated in an abortive attempt to exchange a stock bearing an interest of 5 per cent. for a part
@f the 6 and 7 per cents due in the years 1825 and 1820. The stocks due and redeemable in those years amounted
altogether to the sum of $39,819, 700, a sum far beyond the capacity of the sinking fund to meet, and the Secretary
of the Treasury therefore proposed, in his report of December 10, 1821, to offer, in exchange for $24,000,000 of these
stocks, a stock bearing a lower rate of interest and having a longer time to run. He said: t
A.s the current value of the 5 per cent. stock created during the last aml present years exceeds tliat of the 7 per cent, stock and of the
6 per cent. stocks of 1812 and 1813, it is presumed that tho holders of those stocks will be disposed to exchange them for an equal amount
o~ 5 per cent. stock, redeemable at such periods as to give full operation to the sinking fund as at present constituted. According to this view
of the suliject, $24,000,000 of the stocks, which will be redeemable in the years 1825 and lb'26, may be exchanged for 5 per cent. stock,
redeemable: one-third on the 1st of January, 1831, and one-thir,cl on the same days of 1832 and 1833. This exchange of 6 per cent. stock; if
effectcll on the 1st of January, 1823, will produce an annual reduction of the interest of tho public debt, from that time to the firstmentioned period, of $240,000, and au aggregate saving through the.whole period of $2,160,000. If the whole of the 7 per cent. stock
should be exchanged, tho saving will lie consideralily increased.
·
I

A bill to authorize an exchange of 5 per cent. stocks for those bearing an interest of 6 and 7 per cent. was
reported in the House of Representatives December 31, 1821, and considered in committee of the whole March
15, 1822. It was opposed on the ground that, if passed, it deprived the government of the option of redeeming the
G and 7 per cent. stocks when they became reimbursable, even if able. It was saicl that it was not probable that,
money would be any higher in 1825 than in 1822; that the proper way would be to borrow the money when it

became necessary to redeem the stocks; and that much of the stock to become clue could be redeemed, at the proper
time, by retrenching the expenses of the government and practicing strict economy.
Those who favored the bill said that there was no probability of the government being able to redeem the 6
aml 7 per cent. stocks without resorting to new loans; that the price of money was rising, owing to the revival of
trade and commerce; that by passing the bill a saving of $2,000,000 in interest might be effected; and that it was
necessary to do it at once, as the opportunity would soon pass away.
The bill passed the House early in April by a large majority, passed the Senate with very little debate, ancl
was approved April 20, 1822 (3 Statutes, 603). It ·authorizecl the opening of a subscription to the amount of
$12,000,000 of the 7 per cent. stock and of the 6 per cent. stock of 1812, and for $14,000,000 of 6 per cent. stocks of
the years 1813, 1814, and 1815. For the 6 per cent. stocks of the years 1812, 1813, 1814, and 1815 subscribed and
transferred to the United States, the subscribers were to receive certificates of United States stock, bearing an
interest of u per cent., payable quarterly, reimbursable as follows: one-third at any time after December 31, 1830,
one-third at any time after December 31, 1831, and t:\J.e remainder at any time after December 31, 1832.
For the sums subscribed in the 7 per cent. stock, certificates were to be issued, bearing an interest of 5 per
cent., payable quarterly, redeemable at the pleasure of the United States at any time after December 31, 1833.
The funds pledged by law for the payment of the interest and principal of the stocks which might be subscribed or
exchanged were to remain pledgell to pay the interest and redeem the principal of the stock to be createcl under
this act. The commissioners of the sinking fund were to pay, out of the said fund, the-interest which might become
due on the stock and to purchase the certificates, from time to time, as they purchased other evidences of the public
debt; and so much of the fund as might be necessary was appropriated for the. redemption of the principal, to
continue appropriated until the whole of the stock created was reimbursed. Nothing in the act was to be construed
to abridge or impair the rights of such public creditors as did not choose to Stl,bscribe.
The atte:tnpt to effect an exchange of stocks almost entirely failed, only a very small sum having been subscribed
and exchanged for 5 per cent. stock.

FOUR AND A HALF PER CENT. LOAN OF 1824.
Under the act ofl\fay 24, 1824 (4 Statutes, 33), the sum of $5,000,000 was borrowed to.provide for the awards
of the commissioners under the treaty with Spain of February 22, 1819. This treaty was negotiaterl to settle tho
title.to a portion of Iiouisiana which was.disputed by Spain, and to extend the sovereignty of ·the United State."!
over the province of East Florida. This province, with West Florida, acquired by Spain by conquest from Great
Britain during the war of the Revolution, had been confirmed in her possession under the treaty of 1783.
Commanding the Gulf of Mexico, fringing our southern border, and inhabited by warlike tribes of Indians, its
possession by Spain had long been a source of trouble to the United States .
.A. long series of border tronbles and local insurrections, extending through many years, appear to lmve
convinced the Spanish government that it would be difficult or impossible to hold Florida, while it is i1robable thn.t
the territory was hardly thought worth the expense of holding. The Spanish minister at Washington, in 17HI,
under instructions from home, agreed to and signed a treaty for the cession of East and West Florida, and fixing
the Sabine as the boundary between Lotpsiana and Mexico. In consideration of the cession of the provinces, tho
United States renouncecl certain claims for spoliations on .American commerce and some 'other claims of American
citizens on Spain, and agreed to }lay the same to an amount not exceeding $5 1000,000; also to make satisfaction for
injuries suffered by Spaniards in Florida by the operations of United States troops. This treaty was signed a.t
Washington February 22, 1819, but not ratified by the king of Spain until October 24, 1820.*
By article 11 the five millions indemnity was to be paid either in cash or in 6 per cent. stock, payable out of
the proceeds of sales of public lands in Florida, and a board of commissioners was to be appointed to receive,
examine, and decide on the amount and validity of these claims.
A bill to authorize the creation of new stock to an amount not exceeding $5,000,000, to provide for the u.war<ls
<lf the commissioner!'; under the treaty with Spain, was reported in the Honse of Representatives ,farnmry 8, 1111<1
considered in committee of the whole May 7, 1824. t It was opposed, on the ground tbat the money was not; yot
<lne, t1Je commissioners not having yet adjudicated the claims, and tllat, as it was doubtful if Florida, descdbecl 1L~
being 11 a land of sand-heaps, mosquitoes, frogs, serpents, and alligators", was worth the money, the Unite<l Stiitos
sllould take the option allowed in the treaty, and, instead of borrowing the money, issue t11e 6 per ceut. stook 1
1myal.>Ie out of the proceeds of Florida lands, in which case, if' Florida lands proved worthless, nothing need be pttid.
Those who favored the bill argued that the money was justly due or would soon become due; that, in whatevor
form the stock was issued, it would have to be paid; and that, ff a bi~l was passed to borrow the money, it conlcl be
had at 42- per cent., while if stockwas issued directly to the claimants, it must, by the terms of the treaty, be (i per
cent. stock.
The bill passecl the House by a large majority the same day. Au attem'pt was made to amend it in the Senate
by providing that the stock should be payable, principal and interest, out of the proceeds of the public lands in
Florida, but the Senate rejected the amendment, and the bill 'passed :finally, and was approved May 24, 1824 (4
Statutes, 33). It enacted that, for the purpose of providing funds for paying the awards of the commissioners nnde:t·
the treat.v, the Secretary of the Treasury shoulU cause to be issued ancl sold to the Bank of the United States or
others, at a sum not less than the par value thereof, certificates of United States stock to an amount not exceeding

A bill to ~uthorize the Secretary of the Treasury to exchange certain stocks was first considered in the House
·Of Representatives, in committee of the whole, March 19, 1824. It was opposed as an unnecessary extension of
the loans of the government, throwing upon the i)eople the burden of iuterest for years after the time when it might
be possible to pay the debt. It was said that commerce was increasing and the revenues must increase with it, and
thus the means would be given for paying off the loans as they fell due, and that it was a vain delusion to talk of
saving mone.y by borrowing money and plunging the nation deeper and deeper in debt, unless it was intended to
make the debt perpetual, on the }Jrinciple tbat "a public debt is a public blessing".
By the supporters of the bill it was said, that $63,000,000 of the public debt would become redeemable between
the years 1825 and 1828, and that not the slightest prospect existed of the government beiug able to redeem that
sum as it became due without a resort to new loans, and therefore, as the price of money was low in market, it was
necessary to take advantage of the opportunity of obtaining at least a port.ion of the amount which would be
required, at it low rate of interest; tbat even if the exchange was effected, there would still remain to be paid
$37 ,000,000 in the four years-upwards of $9,000,000 a year-a smn greatly exceecling that portion· <Jf the sinking
fnncl a1)1Jlica1?1e to tho payment of the public debt during those years ; and that therefore it was sound :financial
policy to obtain at least a part of the money, or exchange stock bearing tli low rate of interest for the stocks soon to.
become due, instead of asking at once for a large smn when tbe time for payment had come and the money must
be had.
Tho bill passetl the Honse by a large ma;.iority; passed the Senate apparently without debate; and was approved
J\'fay 26, 182,1 ('.! Stritntm;, 73). It authorized the President to borrow, on the credit of the United States,
on or before April 1, 182G, a, sum not exceeding $5,00o,ooo, at a rate of interest not exceeding 4~ })er cent., and
reimbursable, at the pleasure of the government, at any time after December 31, 1831, the money borrowed to be
applied, together with the money in the treasury, to pay off and discharge such part of the 6 i)er cent. stock of the
yeaT 1812 as might be redeemable ttftei: January 1, 1825.
The Bank of the United States was to be allowed to lend the amount, or any part thereof, ancl the Secretary of
the T,reasury was authorized to raise the money by selling certificates of stock not under par. Nothing contained
in the act was to be construed to impair the rig·hts of such creditors of the U nitecl States as did not choose to subscribe

TREASURY NOTES OF 1837.

In the year 1836 the United States was, for the :first time in the history of the country, 11ractically out of uebt.
The Secretary of the Treasnry, in his report to Congress, dated December 8, 1835,* estimated the amount of the
public debt still outstanding at about $328,582 10, u,nd this remained unpaid solely because payment had not been
Jemanded, ample fnnds to meet it having been deposited with the United States Bank and with the commissioners
of loans. The outstanding debt consisted mainly of unclaimed interest and diviclencls, of claims for services an<I
supplies during the Hevolution, and of old treasury notes, and it is supposed that payment of these had not been
asked for solely because the evidences of the debt had been lost or destroyed. At the same time the estimates of
the receipts and expenditures showed the probabilit.y of a surplus of at least $14,000,000 in the treasury at the
elose of the year 1~36, and this estimate was, as events showed, far below the truth. In this favorable state of the
public finances, Congress adopfod the extraordimtry resolution of depositing tlle surplus over $5,000,000 with the
several States, and under the act of June 23, 1836,-r surplus revenue, amounting to $28,101,644 91 were so deposited.
In 1837, however, the state of the country had changed. The "flush" times of 1835 and 1836 had been
succeeded by extraordinary depression and panic. In l\fay most of the banks suspended specie payments. The
sales of public lands and the duties on the large importations of foreign goods, which had helped to swell the
balance in the treasury to over $42,000,000, had fallen off enormously. Even on the goods that were imported it
was difficult to collect the duties, for the law required them to be paid in specie, .and specie was hard to obta.in.
It had become impossible not ·only to pay the fourth installment of the surplus at the end of 1836 to the several
States, but even to meet the current expenses of the government from the ordinary revenues.
The Secretary of the Treasury therefore suggested that contingent authority be given the President to cause
the issue of treasury notes bearing an interest of 6 per cent. The bill to authorize the issue of not exceeding.
$12,000,000 was introduced in the House of Representatives September 13, and debated at length in both the House
and Senate. It was supported on the ground that the issue of treasury notes was absolutely necessary, there being
already a deficit of $2,000,000, likely, in the condition of the country at that time, to increase, and that so large an
amount a<.ltled to the circulating medium would tend to alleviate the distress prevailing; while it was opposed by
those who thought greater economy in expenditures would relieve the treasury, and by others who denounced it as.
an attempt "to start a treasury bank". The bill passed the House by a small majority, while in the Senate there
were but six votes against it. It was approved October 12, 1837 (5 Statutes, 201). It authorized the Preside!J.t to
cause the issue of treasury notes in such sums as the exigencies of the government might require, not exceeding in:
the whole $10,000,000, of' denominations not l~ss than $50, redeemable one year after date, bearing interest :fi·om their
respective dates for the term of one year at rates to be fixed by the Secretary of the Treasury, but not to exceed &
per cent. They were to be issued, in· payment of debts due by the U~ited States, to such public creditors or other
persons as chose to receive them in payment at their par value; were to be transferable by delivery and assignment
inclorsed on them by the person to whom theJ' had been made payable, and were to be received in payment of all
duties and taxes laid by the United States, of all public lands sold by the said authority, and of all debts due to
the United States, credit to be given for the interest due on the notes u,t the time of payment. The Secretary of
the Treasury was authorized to borrow, not below par, such sums as the Presid~nt might deem expedient, on thecredit of the notes. Under this act the fUll amount authorized, $10,000,000, was issued.
TREJ.ASURY NOTES OF 1838.

The condition of the country and of the public .finances was no more favorable at the close of the year 1837
than at its beginning. There was in the treasury January 1, 1838, an apparent balance of over $34,000,000, but
of this amount the largest portion was unavailable. It consisted of the amount deposited with the several States,.
$28,101,644 91; of money belonging to the government deposited with suspended or insolvent banks i of amounts
duo from merchants on bonds given for duties on imports, difficult or impossible to collect; and of various other
items; aggregating, altogether, so large an amount that the Secretary of the Treasury estimated· the availablebalance at the close of the year at but $1,118,393.:j:
It is probable tllis estimate was too ln;rge, as the President informed Oongress, in May, 1838, that the availablo
means in the treasury were only $216,000. Demands to a large amount were suspended in the departments, waiting.
payment from means yet to be provided by Congress, and the resources on hand could carry the government
but a few days longer.§ The dues to the government being largely paid in the treasury notes of 1837, which the
department was forbidden to reissue, the revenue was practically almost not.hing, and it became absolutely necessary
to provide additional means. A bill to allow the issue of treasury notes was introduced in the House March 23,
passed both House and Senate after a long debate, in which the same objections were urged as those to the bill of
October 12 1837 and was approved May 21, 1838 (5 Statutes, 228). It was, when introduceu, entitled ''A bill
supplement~ry to' the act entitled an act to authorize the issue of treasury notes", and it was stated that no

TREASURY NOTES OF 1839.
The power to issue and reissue treasury notes under the acts of October. 12, 1837, and Mtty 21, 1838, expired
on December 31 1838. The act of March 2, 1830 (5 Statutes, 323), extended this time to the 30th day of Juno

was issued.
TREASURY NOTES OF 1840.

The President of the United States informed Congress, by a special message dated February 17, 1840, that,
although the resources of the treasury for the whole year would probably be equal to the expenditures, yet the
department might, notwithstanding, be unable to meet the claims upon it when they fell due, because the larger
part of the charges upon the treasury, including the payment of pensions and the redemption of. treasury notes 1
fell due in the early part of the year, while the resources on which it might otherwise rely would mostly be
una\·ailable until the last half of the year, and a portion1 bei.ng debts due from banks, might not be punctually paid.
To remedy this inconvenience, the act of March 31, 1840 (5 Statutes, 370), was passed, although strenuousls
opposed as unconstitutional and unnecessary, the House of Representatives being fa session at one time for
twenty.five hours on the bill.*
It renewed the provisions of the act of October 12, 1837, except as to the amount of notes and the time in
which they might be issued, and authorized the issue of treasury notes in lieu of those which had been or might;
be redeemed, but not to exceed, in the amount of notes outstanding at any one time, the sum of $5,000,000, to he
redeemed sooner than one year if the means of the treasury would permit, by giving sixty days' notice of those
notes which the department was ready to redeem, no interest to be allowed thereon after the expiration of the sixty
days; the act t-0 continue in force one year and no longer. Under this act the sum of $7,114,251 31 was issti.ed.

TREASURY NOTES OF 1841.
The Secretary of the Treasury, in his report on the finances dated December 7, 1840, estimated that, at the
close of the year 1841, there would remain in the treasury an available balance of but $824,273, and that even this
small balance might entirely disappear, and an actual deficit of several millions be found, under tho operu,tions of
the compromise tariff act of 1833, which was rapidly lowerfag the amount of customs-dntiQs levied, aided by
fluctuations in the amount of goods imported, which had fallen off during the year 1840 nearly sixty millions of
dollars.
To ward off the danger of this possible deficit the act of February 15, 1841 (5 Sta.tutes, 411), was passed. It·
authorized the President to cause treasury notes to be issued for such sums as the exigencies of the government
might require, but not exceeding the sum of $5,0001000 of this emission, outstanding at mw one time to be reimbursctl
in the last quarters of the year, if the condition of the treasury would permit, tho notes to be issued uncler the limitations
and provisions o: the aet of October 12, 1837, as modified by the act of March 31, 1840; and if, on the 4th du.y of
March next ensumg, the treasury notes outstanding, issued under former laws, added to the amount of.notes issued
m1der this act, should exceed the sum of $5,000,000, then the President was authorized to issue such further sums
as would make the whole amount of notes issued under this act and applicable to payments after March 3 1841 tho
full sum of $5,000,000. Under this act notes to the amount of $71529,062 75 were issued.

TREASURY NOTES OF JANUARY, 1842.
The loan proposed by the act of July 21,.1841 (5 Statutes, 438), owing to the short period which was to elapse
be.fore it became redeemablei does not appear to have met with much favor from those who had money to lend.
Up to December 20, 1841, the amount received, of the $12,000,000 asked for, was only $5,532,726 88, while the
-estimated deficiency on January 1, 1842, was $627,557 90, and the estimated excess of expenditures over re-venue
for the year 1842 was $14,218,570 68. ln this emergency the Secretary recommended an extension of tlle time
-within which the residue of the loan, not yet taken, sllould be redeemable, the reissue of the treasury notes
lleretofore authorizecl by law, and an increase of the duties on certain classes of imports.* .A. "bill to allow the issue
and reissue of treasury notes was introduced in the House January 5, 1842, and met witll much opposition, on the
·old grounds of the unconstitutionality of bills of credit, of the inexpediency of adding to the paper money of the
,country, and on the plea that economy would enable the government to meet its expenses without causing a
·deficit in the treasnry. Its supporters denied that tlle measure was unconstitutional, and admitted that the best
way to provide for the deficiency wonld be by obtaining a loan, but asserted that if a loan bill was passed, the
money could not be obtained in this country and that it would be necessary to send the bonds to Europe for sale,
which would consume much time, while the needs of the treasury "'ere urgent. It finally passed both houses, and
was approved January 31, 1842 (5 Statutes, 469).
The act authorized the President to cause tlle issue of treasury notes for such sums as the exigencies of the
go'\ernment migllt require, the notes when redeemed to be reissued, but not exceeding the sum of $5,ooo,ooo of this
~mission to be outstanding at any one time, the notes to be issued under the p:i;ovisions and limitations contained
in the act of October 12, 1837, except that tlle authority gi'Ven to issue treasury notes was to expire at tlle end of
-0ne year from tlle passage of the act. Under this act the sum of $7,959,904 83 was issued.
LOA.N OF 1842.
This loan was obtained under the act of July 21, 1841, the provisions of which were extended by the act of
April 15, 1842 (5 Statutes, 473). The bill was passed, like that for the issue of the treasury notes of January, 1?42, to
prevent the threatened deficiency in the treasury mentioned in the notes on that act. It was manifest that the
power to keep outstanding $5,000,000 in treasury notes could not make up a deficiency of over· $14,000,ooo, hence
tho necessity for this bill. It was introduced in the House December 23, 1841, and passecl after long debate, in
which the responsibility for the condition of the finances was charged by each party on the other. Tlle act extended
the time limited by the first section of the act of July 21, 1841, to the time of one ;year from the passage of this act,
.aud provided that so much of the loan as was obtained after its passage should he reimbursable as should be agreed
upon at the time of issuing the stock, not to exceed twenty years from the 1st day of Janua~, 1843. The Secretary
.of .tlie Treasury was authorized to dispose of the stock below par, if its par value could not be obtained, but. not
until after the loan bad been duly advertised and proposfils for subscription invited. The President was also
authorized to borrow an additional sum of $5,000,000, if the exigencies of the gove:unment should require tlle same,
under the same provisions and limitations. Under this act stoek was issued amounting to $8,343,886 03 for which
$8,301,468 23 was received in cash.

The act of July 21 1841 authorizing a loan of not exceec\ing $12,000,000, provided, that no stock be sold below

force. It was attempted to amend this, by the act of April 15, 1842, allowing the Secretary of the Treasury, if tho
stock could not be sold at par, to dispose of it at lower prices, and also extending the time for redemption to not·
more than twenty years from January 1, 1843, but it was still found impossible to obtain par for the sto?k. To
prevent its sacrifice a bill was introduced in the House to allow the issue of treasury notes when. the remamder of
the :stock could not he sold below par. It was stated in debate, by the chairman of the committee of ways and
means, who introduced the bill, that the immediate liabilities of the government were $3,875,000, and to meet these
demands not one dollar was available, and that the stock must either "be sacrificed to the Shylocks of the country",
or some other means mnst be given the Secretary of the Treasury to meet these liabilities.* The bill does not
ap1)ear to have met with much opposition. It was approved August 31, 1842 (5 Statutes, 581.)
.
It provided that no stock authorized by the act of July 21, 1841, and by the act amendatory of the same, approvell
.April 15, 1842, should thereafter be sold at less than par; and in case the stock could not be sold at or above par,.
and the exigencies of the public service should require, the Secretary of the Treasury was authorized to issue, in lieu
thereof, treasury notes to the amount of not more than $6,000,000, under the provisions and limitations containecl
in the acts of October 12, 1837, and March 31, 1840. The notes when redeemed might be reissued, or new notes.
issued in their stead, but none were to be issued after April 15, 1843, and the amount outstanding at any one time·
was not to exceed $6,000,000. Under this act notes to the amount of $3,025,554 89 were issued. ·
TREASURY NOTES OF 1843.
These notes were simply issues of new notes in place of such as had been issued under previous acts o:f
Congress, and which had been or might be redeemed at the treasury, or received i~ payment of dues. The
necesshy for their issue was in the fact that the"estimated revenues for the year were but little in excess of the current
expenses. The national debt in March, 1843, was said to be $27,409,338, of which $11,068,977 fell due during the
year and might be presented for payment.t Under these circumstances it became necessary either to obtain 11 new
loan, to increase the taxes, always an unpopular expedient, or to issue new treasury notes, as had been done at each
session for the past six years. The course was adopted of giving authority both to obtain a new loan ancl to issue
treasury notes; though this latter was characterized in debate as a mere temporary expedient to enable the government to get along from day to day and to maintain its credit without repudiation.:j: The act was approved March 3,
1843 (5 Statutes, 614). It provided that when any outstanding treasury notes issued under previous acts of
Congress should, after the passage of this act, be redeemed at any time before July 1, 1844, the Secretary of the
Treasury, shoukl the public service require it, might cause other notes, to the same amount, to be issued in their
stead, under the limitations and provisions of the acts under which the notes were originally issued. It authorized
the pa3·ment of interest on notes issued under this act after maturity, and also on those issued under the act of
August 31, 1842. Under this act notes to the amount of $1,806,950 were issued.
. LOAN OF 1843.
This loan was obtained under the act authorizing the issue of treasury notes, approved March 3, 1843 (5 Statutes,
614), and for the same purpose. The third section of the act authorized the Presideni,, if, in his opinion, it should:
be for the interest of the United States so to do, to cause to be redeemed and canceled such of the notes then
outstanding as they became due, by the issue of stock of the United States, under the limitations and provisions of the
act of .April 15, 1842, except that no commissions were to be aUowed to agents, and the stock should be redeemable
at a llerfod not later than ten years from the issue thereof. Under this act stock to the amount of $7,004,231 35,
was issued, most of which was sold at a small premium.

MEXICAN IXDEIIINITY STOCK.
The peculin,r circumstances which gave rise to the issue of this stock are wortlly of note, as resulting from an
ineffectual attempt to collect the money due American citizens on account of claims against the government of
Mexico. By the terms of the convention of January 30, 18!3, between the United States aml the Mexican republic,
it was provided that on the 30th day of Avril, 184:3, the Mexican government should pay all the interest which might
then be due on the awards in favor of claimants under the convention of the 11th of April, 1839. The principal
.of the said awards, and the interest accruing thereon, to be paid in :five years, in equal installments every three
months, to commence April 30, 1843. These payments were to be made in the city of I\Iexico, to such person as the
United States might authorize to receive them, in gold or silver money.* In accordance with these sti1mlations, an
a.gent was duly appointed for the collection of the several installments as they respectively came due, and the
:Secretary of State was informed, by an official communication from the United States minister at the city of 1Iexico,
·elated September 2,1, 1814, that the installments whfoh fell due on the preceding 30th of April and 30th of July
were paid, to the authorized agent appointed to receive and transmit the same, on the 27th of August of that year,
.and it was so reported to Congress. Upon examination into the facts, however, it was found that no money had
been received, and au investigation was ordered, resulting in the development of a singular transaction on the part
.of the o.gent designated to receive the money from the Mexican authorities. It appears from the correspondence
1·el:1tive to this affair that, for ~he avowed purpose of liquidating these claims, Santa .Aiia, the head of the Mexican
gov-ernment, in May, 1843, decreed the collection of a forced loan, to be distributed in certain proportions through
the various departments of the Mexim1n republic, ancl paid at l)eriods cotresponcling to tllose stipulated by the
convention. This measure, essentially unpopular, was hnperfectlj enforced, while the temptation to a misapplication
-Of the fnntls collected, amidst the difficulties by which Santa Aila was surrounded, was very great. Under these
circumstances the Mexican government was unable to pa.y the illstallment which became due in April, 1844; an(l
in J' n1y of the same year, when another installment beca.me due, the incapacity of the government hall become still
clearer. The arrears then clue and payable amountell to $274,GG4 G7. The applications of the agent of the United
States at the Mexic11n tre11sury for payment of these installments were evaded or disregarded, and he was informed
tha.t there was no money in the treasury applicable thereto. Despairing of obtaining any direct lmyment, he made
.an arrangement with an English firm there, of high sta.nding, then believecl to be successful1y 11rosccuting a claim
of considerable amount, by which they assumed the collection of the said installments, in addition to their own .
.clairu, all(l agreed to pay him the pro1>ortionate share of such collections, gradually, as received by them from the
Mexican government. In pursuance of this arrangement, orders, to the amount of $274,664 G7, on the Yarious
departments, were given in favor of the agent of the United States, by the Mexican goYernment, and a receipt for
the amount given by lliUl as if it hacl been r>aid in money. These orders were handed over by him to tlle English
firm, with whom h.e had made the arrangement for the collection of the installments.+ Nothing was realized from
these orders, but the matter of the indernuity was afterward disposed of by article 13 of the treaty of Guadalu1)e
Hidn,lgo, February 2, 1848, which reads as follows: f
The United States engage, moreover, to 11Ssmne 1md pay to the claimants all the amounts now due them, and those hereafter to
become due by reason of the claims already liquidated an<l decided against the Mexican republic, under the conventions between the two
republics severally conclm1ed on the 11th day of April, lS~G. and on the 30th day of January, 1843; so tlrnt the Mexican :republic shall be
.absolutely exmnpt, for the future, from all expense whatever on account of the said claims.

LOAN OF 1848.
The President of the United States, in his message of December 7, 1847, informed Congress that if the war with
Mexico continued until July 1, 1848, the expenditure woukl probably exceed the revenue provic1ec1 by law about
$16,000,ooo, t while the Secretary of the Treasury, in his report of December 8, estimated that, adding tll;e sum
necessary to be kept in the treasury to meet the wants or' the government and maintain the enlarged operations
at the mints, there would be requirecl $18,729,114 27,·in addition to the probable revenue. A bill to authorize a
loan not to exceed $18,500,000 was introdtwed in the House of' Representatives January 19, 1848. The chairman
of the committee of ways and means informed the House, during the debate, that an error lmd been discovered in
the :financial statement of the Secretary of the Treasury, which clisclosecl the gratifying fact that there was in the
treasury nearly $7,000,000 more than had been stated to the House, in consequence of which it was supposed theloan might be reduced to $12,000,000, but that t.he Secretary of War had since askec1 au appropriation of nearly
$4,000,000 to supply certain deficiencies in that branch of the public service, and it would therefore be necessary
to make the amount $16,000,000, and he moved an amendment making the alteration, avowing at the same time
his utter want of confidence in the estimates of the Secretary of the Treasury, and stating that he believed at
least $25,000,000 would be required.t
The hill passed both houses after an extended debate and was approved March 31, 1848 (9 St.atutes, 217). It
authorized the President to borrow, on the credit of the United States, at any time within one year from the passage
of the act, a sum not exceeding $10,000,000, at an interest of not more than 6 per cent. per annum, payable quarterly
or semi-annually, reimbursable at any time after July 1, 1868. No'certificate of stock was to be issued for a less
amount than $50. The expenses attending the execution of the act were not to exceed $16,000. The Secretary of
the Treasury was authorized to purchase the stock of the loan, at any time before the <late at which it became
reimhnrsable, at the market price, not below pa,r. Under this act stock to the amount of $16,ooo,ooo was issued
ancl solcl at a. small premium.

TEXAS INDEMNITY STOOK.
The state of Texas, when first annexed to the Uuitec1 States, had doubtful claims to an extensive territory. It
was sa.icl, in debate on the subject, that her southeast corner was at the mouth of the Rio Grande, the region of
perpetual flowers, her northeast corner near the South pass in the Rocky mountains, a region of everlasting snows;
that i:;he had a front on the Gulf of Mexico of a thousaucl miles, a front on the Rio Grande, partly disputed, of 2,00(}
miles, an unclispnted ·frontier on the Arkansas of 1,000 miles, rmcl within these boundaries she claimecl an area of
350,000 square miles, or more tluw equal to seven states as large as Pennsylvania.§
'
To somewhat rec1uce this enormous territory, to pay her somewhat shadowy claims to all the public lanc11:l
within these boundaries, and to obtain from her a release of her claims on the United States for liability for herdebts, and to indemnify her for the surrender of her ships, forts, arsenals, custom-houses, munitions of war, and
public buildings, a bill was introl1nced in the Senate Augm;t 5, 1850.
'l'he hill, notwithstanding strenuous opposition, was passed ancl approved September 9, 1850 (9 Statutes, 447) ..
It provided that if the state of Texas should consent that her boundary on the north should commence at a point
at which the meridian of one lnmclrecl degrees west from Greenwich is intersected by the parallel of 36° 30' north
latitude and run thence clue west to the meridian of one hundred ancl three clegrees west from· Greenwich, then
south to the thirty-second degree of north latitude, then on said parallel to the Rio Bravo del Norte, and thence,
with the channel of said river, to the Gulf of Mexico, and should cede to the United States all her claim to the
territory exterior to these boundaries, ancl relinquish all claim on the· United States for liability for her debts, anc1
for compensation for the surrender of her ships, forts, custom-houses, public buildings, &c., then the United States,.
in consideration for the same, should pay her the snrn of $10,000,000 in stock, bearing 5 per cent. interest, payable
semi-annually, redeemable at the end of fourteen years. No more than $5,000,000 of said stock was to be issued
until the creditors of the state holding bonds and other certificates of the stock of Texas; for which duties on
imports were specifically pledged, should :first :file at the treasury releases of all claim against the United States on.
account of said bonds or certificates.

TREASURY NOTES OF 18ii7.
Tllo Secretary of the Treasury, in his annual report of December 8, 1857, informed Congress that although tliere
wa8 a balance on hand at the close of tlie last fiscal year amounting to $17, 710,114 27, yet so great was tbe fa1ling
off in the re,·enues from customs, owing to tlle monetary crisis of that period, that it woukl be absolutely necessary
to pro-ride the treasury with some additional means of meeting the demands upon it. On this point lie says:
Such proYisiou shoultl he mado at tho earliest pmcticahlo period, as o. failuro of sufficient means in the treasury may occur at an early
day. '.!.'ho exigency being regarded as temporary, tho mode of providing for it should bo of a temporary clmracter. It is therefore
recommendetl that authority ho given to this department, by law, to issue treasury notos for an amount not to oxceed $20,000,000, payal.>lo
within a limitell time, and carrying n specitlccl rato of interest, whenever tho hnmcdiato demands of the public scn·ico may call for a
greater amount of money th:1n sl1all l11tppe11 to be iu the treasury, subject to the treasurer's drafts in payment of warrants. Tho fact that
811ch temporary exigency may arise from circumstances beyond tho foresight or control of this department makes some ncleqnato 11rovision
to meet it inclispensable to tho public security.

.A bill for this purpose was introduced in the Senate, ©ll the 17th of December, by the committee on finance,
and on the following day in the House of Representatives by the committee of ways and means, and was at once
the cause of an exciting debate.
Serious charges of mismanagement and extravagance in the administration of the several departments were
made on the one side, while on the other the cause of the recent monetary crisis was charged to the action taken by
the banks in their suspension of specie payments.
Notwithstanding the strong 01>positiou manifested in both llouses, the necessity for.the passage of some measure
for the immediate relief of the treasury was conceded, and the bill :finally passed the Senato on the lDth of December,
by a Yote of 31 yeas to 18 nays. On the 22d it passed the House of Representati1es by a vote of 118 yeas to SG
nays, and, being approvecl by the President on the following daJ', became a law December 23, 1857 (11 Statutes,
257). This act authorized the issue of treasiJry notes for such sum as the exigencies of the public ser-rice migllt
require, not to exceed at any time the amount of $20,0oo,ooo, and of denominations not less tlrnn $100 for any such
note, payable and redeemable at the treasury of the'United States after Hie expiration of one year from the dates
of Sltid notes, with interest thereon, not to exceed six per cent.; the :first issue not to exceed $0,000,000. The resil1ue
to be issued jn whole or in part, after public advertisement of not less than thirty drtys, by exchanging said notes,
at their par value for specie, to tlle bidder or bidders agreeing to make such exchange at the lowest rate of interest,
not exceeding six per cent.; interest to cease at the expiration of sixty days' notice by the Secretary of the Treasury
of readiness to imy and redeem said notes at their maturity. The faith of the United States was solemnly i>ledged
for their redemption, and they were mtide receivable for aH dues to t11e United States. Under this ::wt the amount
(Jf $52,778,900 was issued, incluuing reissues.

LOAN OF 1858.
The prediction made in the debate on the bill to issue the treasury notes of 1857, that the amount asked for
was not enough, and tliat at least $201000,000 more would be· required, was speedily realized. In 1\fay, 1858, the
Secretary of the Treasury informed Congress that, owing to the appropriations having been increased by legislation
nearly $10,000,000 over the estimates, while the revenue from customs had fallen about ~10,000,000 below the
estimates, it would be necessary to provide some means of meeting the deficit.* A bill to authorize a loan not
exceeding $15,000,000 was reported in the House of Representatives May 26, 1858, and this amount was subsE:'quen tly
increased· to $20,000,000.
The chairman of the committee of ways and means, who reported the bill, said that the public debt was then
$~5,157,058 27, all of which would fall dne within ten years, and the date of redemption of the proposed Joan had
therefore been made fifteen years, or five years beyond the period within which tho then existing debt would faU
·due. He estimatetl that, even if authority was given to borrow $15,000,000, there would still be a deficit at tlic
close of the fiscal year of nearly $1,000,000. On account of this probable deficit, and of an increase in tho amount
of appropriations, the Senate raised the amount of the proposed loan to $20 1000,000. The bill was approved ,Tnnc
14, 1858 (11 Statutes, 363). It authorized the President to borrow, on the credit of the United States, at any
time within twelve months after the date of the act, a sum not exceerling $201000,000, reimbursable at any time
after January 1, 1874. The interest was not to exceed 5 per cent. per annum, payable semi-annually, with cou11ons
for the semi-annual interest attached to each certificate of stock. No certificate was to be issued for less than
$1,000, and none was to be sold at less than its par Yalue. Under thi:; act the s:m1 of $20,000,000 was obtained,
the certificates selling at from 2 to 7 i>er cent. premium.

LO.AN OF 1860.

The annual report of the Secretary of the Treasury dated December 22, 1859, contained the following clause:
In the estimated means of the treasury for the present and next fiscal ye11rs, it will be seen th11t no provision is made for the
permanent redemption of any portion of the $20,000,000 of treasury notes. 'l'he authority for reissuing these notes will expire on the
30th of June nex.t1 and it will, therefore, lie necessary for Congress to extend the law for that purpose for another period.

Oongress appears to have differed with the Secretary as to the propriety of reis~uing the notes, as, instead of
giving authority so to do, a bill providing for their redemption by means of a loan was introduced in the House of ·
Representatives .June 21, passed both houses almost without debate, and was approved .June 22, 1860 (12 Statutes,
79) .. It authorized the President to borrow, at any time within twelve months after the passage of the act, a
sum not exceeding $21,000,000, on the credit of the United States, the money to be used only in the redemption of
treasury·notes, and to replace in the treasury any amount of said notes which should have been paicl in for public
<lues.
The stock was to bear interest not exceeding 6 per cent. per annum, and was to be reimbursed within a period
not beyond twenty years nor less than ten y~ars. No certificate was to be issued for less than $1,000. The
Secretary of the Treasury was authorized to cause coupons of semi-annual interest to be attached to the certificates,
and any certificate bearing such coupons attached was to be assignable by delivery. No stock was to be disposecl
-0f at less than its par value.
Undei; this act the sum of $7,022,000 was borrowed, at 5 per cent. interest, the certificates selling at from par
to 1.45 per cent. premium.
It would appear that the failure to realize the whole amount of the loan was caused by the political troubles
that culminated in the war of the Rebellion. Bids were invited for $10,000,000 on September 8, 1860, and the whole
amount offered was speedily taken. It soon became evident, however, that war was inevitable, ancl a commercial
crisis ensued, during which a portion of·the bidders forfeited their deposits, and the loan was withdrawn from the
market after the amount stated above had been realized.
TREASURY NOTES OF 1860.
Authority for the issue of these notes became necessary when it was found that but a portion of the $21,000,000
loan authorized by the act of June 22, 1860, could be obtained, and was recommended by the Secretary of the
Treasury in his annual report of December 4, 1860, as follows :
To meet the remaining outstanding treasury notes and interest thereon, there is yet to be negotiated eleven millions of the stock
authorized by the act of .Tune 22, 1860. The statement just made of the difficulties attending the payment for the stock alrei~dy solcl, in
-0onncction with the fact that capitalists, in the present condition of the country, seem unwilling to invest in Unitecl States stock at par,
renders it almost certain that this remaining eleven millions cannot now be negotiated upon terms acceptable to the government. The
condition of the treasury is such that no serious delay can be indulged. I recommend, therefore, a repeal of so much of t;he act of June
22, 1860, as uuthorizes tho issuing of this additional eleven millions of stock, and that authority be given for the is'suing of treasury notes
to the same amount, to be negotiated at such rates as will command the confidence of the country. To creato that confidence, I recommend
that the public lands be unconditionally pledged for the ulJiimate redemption of all the treasury notes which it may become necessary to
issue. I make 'this recommendation of substituting treasury notes for stock the more readily from the conviction that there should
.always exist in the department power to issue treasury notes for a limited amount, under the direction of the Pxesident, to meet unforeseen
·Contingencies. It is a power which can never be abused, as the amount realized from such source can only be used to meet lawful
demancls upon the treasury.
·

A bill to allow the issue of treasury notes to an amount not exceeding $10,000,000 was introduced in tll.e
House of Representatives December 10. There appears to have been but little opposition to the bill as a whole,
but an effort was made to change some of its features. .A.n ~ttempt was made to pledge the receipts from the
public lands specifically for the redemption of the notes. In the Senate an unsuccessful effort was made to
&uthorize the issue of notes as low as $20, it being urged that notes of the smaller denominations would be more
1eadily taken at par, as they could be paid to laborers and others having small demands against the government.
The Senate r~iected this amendment, but altered the bill so as to allow of the issue of notes of as low a denomination
as $50. The same attempt was made as in the House to pledge the proceeds of the public lands for the specific
redemption of the notes, and objected to on the ground that it would interfere with the homestead bill then under
discussion iJt Oongress.
The bill passed, and was approved December 17, 1860 (12 Statutes, 121). It authorized the President to cause
the issue of treasury notes for such sums as the exigencies of the public service might require, not exceeding at any
time"the amount of $10,000,000, of denominations not less than $50 for each note, redeemable at the treasury after
the expiration of one year from the date of issue, bearing interest until redeemed or called at the rate of G per cent.
per annum; but the Secretary was authorized to issue them, after advertisement, at such rates of interest as might
be offered by the lowest responsible bidders. Under this act treasury notes were issued, redeemable at the.
· expiration of one year from date, bearing interest as follows: $70,200, at 6 per cent.; $5,000, at 7 per cent.; $24,500,
