LOAN OF FEBRUARY, 1861.
So low had the credit of the government fallen at this time that Secretary John A. Dix, 111 a letter to the
chairman of the committee of ways and means of the House of Representatives, dated Jannar! 18, 1861, suggested
to the committee, as an additional financial resource, that the several states be asked: as secu:1ty for the repayment
of any money the government might :find it necessary to borrow, ·to pledge the deposits recervecl by them from the
government under the act for the distribution .of the s~rplus revenu~s in 1836, believing th~t ~ l~an coutractecl on
such a basis of security, superadding to the pllghted faith of the Umted States that of the rnd1v1dual states, could
hardly fail to be acceptable to capitalists.
.
·
The committee reported, on the 1st of February, a bill to authorize a loan of $25,000,ooo, with a statement tlrnt
the Secretary of the Treasury had repprted tliat there would be an actual deficit in the revenue of $21,677,524. In
addition to this, the chairman of the committee stated that the deficiency bill appropriated between two and throo
millions of dollars, making an estimated deficiency of $24,000,000, while the amount in the treasury on the 1st of
January was but $2,233,220. At least $5,000,000 were required to carry on the daily operations; there sllonld,
therefore, be provided, in addition to the revenues, at least $25,000,000.* It was said, in debate on the bill, tlin.t tho
amount was needed to pay off the debts of the retiring administration. The falling off in the revenue was caused
by the political troubles which ended in the war of the Rebellion, and which had ah'eacly diminishecl the imports to
a very large extent.
The bill was objected to on the ground that the Secretary still possessed the power, under the act of June, 1800,
to borrow $14,000,000, and that this amount, at least, should be deducted from the prOJ)Osed loan. To this it was
answered that the balance of the loan of.June 22, 1860, could not be sold upon the terms prescribed in the htw, mul
that if it could, the money was specifically pledged to the redemption of the treasmy notes of 1860.
The bill was approvecl Felmiary 8, 1861 (12 Statutes, 129). It authorized the President to borrow, at any tiurn
before the 1st day of July, 1861, a sum not exceeding $25,000,000, to be used in the payment of current demands
on the treasury, for the redemption of outstanding treaslll'y notes, and to replace in the treasury snch notes as had
been paid in for public dues. The stock was to bear interest not exceeding 6 per cent. per annum, and to be
reimblU'.sable within not less than ten nor more than twenty years. The Secretary of the Treasury was anthorized.
to cause coupons of semi-annual interest to be attached to the bonds, whenever required. No certificate of stoek
was to be issued for less than $1,000. Under this act bonds were issued amounting to $18,415,000, at an aggrogato
discount of $2,019,776 10, or an average rate of $89 03 per one hundred dollars.
TREASURY NOTES OF 1861.
The Secretar,r of the Treasury in his repo~t on the :finances, dated December 22, 1859, had estimated that:
there woultl be a balance in the treasury, June 30, 1801, of only $3,530,196 61, which left no margin for additional
appropriations. If, therefore, the appropriations should exceed the estimates, or Congress should determine withh1
this periocl to provide for the payment of any portion of the public debt, which then amounted .to nearly $00,000,000,
it would be necessary to mak~ adequate provision for such contingencies. The Secretary expressed himself as of
tbe opinion that the idea of increasing the public debt to meet the ordinary expenses of government should not be
entertained for a moment. If additional demands on the treasury were created by legislation, provision must he
made to meet them by such an increase of tariff duties as might be necessary.
A. bill for the repayment of outstanding treasury notes, to authorize a loan, and to regulate and :fix the duties,
on imports was introduced in the Honse March 12, 1860. It was said, in debate on the bill that the idea of not
increasing the public debt to meet current expenses was a very patriotic one, and that it would hardly be suspected
that the administration had practically illustrated the sentiment, by steadily increasing the debt to meet those
expenses during every year that it bad been in power, with the following result:

OREGON WAR DEBT,

....

,,~

··

The settlement of Oregon appears to have commenced in 1830, bht was oon:tined to a few adventmous pioneers,
wh@ made their way across the plains from Missouri through friendly or hostile llidian tri<bes, until the year 1850,
whoo. emigration was stimulated by the i~assage of an act giving 320 acres of land to each settler, and, if married,
the same amount to his wife. In 1851 the gold-washings of the Rogue river valley were discovered, which caui;;ed a
sll!dldcn increase of population from the mining districts of California. Shortly after these miners arrived, the
tmubles with the Indians commenced.
In 1855 hostilities broke out and a general war ensued, which continued for more than a year, after which thcro .
was a short but hollow peace. In 1858 another war began, but was of short duration, the increased wbite
population and the military forces of the general government soon overpowering the Indians.
At the breaking out of the war in 1855 the militia were called out by the governors of Oregon and Washington,
and heavy expenses incurred. After the war closed a demand was made on the general government for the
payment of these troops, and for the expenses of their maintenance in the field.
A commission, appointecl under an act of Congress, was sent out to investigate the subject anc1 audit the clnhm;.
The repm•tof this commission stated the amount due to Oregon at $4,449,949 33, and to Washington $1,481,475 45.* 1
A supplemental report added about $80,000, making the total amount to $6,011,424 78. The committee on military
affairs referred this report ancl the papers to ihe third auditor of the treasury, under a resolution of the House,
with directions to audit the claims, and in so doing to allow the militia the same pay as was given to regular soldiers
of the· same grade in the United States army, ancl for the supplies a reasooable approximation to what was already
authorized by existing laws'.
On the 7th of February, 1860, the third auditor transmitted bis report to the Rouse, showing that the claims,
if settled on this footing, would aggregate $2,714,808 55. Against this reduction the delegates from Oregon and
Washington protested. A bill passed ihe Senate in 1861, appropriating between three and four millions of dollars
for the payment of these claims, but it never reached a vote in the House.
A bill for the payment of the Oregon and Washington war debt was introduced in Fet>ruary, 1861, passed
both hC!luses, with some slight amendments, and was approved March 21 1861 (12 Statutes, 198). It appropriated,
for the pay of volunteers m the war, the sqm of $400,000, the claims to be examined and audited by the third
auditor, and the volunteer~ to be allowed the same pay as United States officers and soldiers serving in Oregon
and Washington. For the payment of claims for services, supplies, transportation, &c., incurred in the maintenance
of the volunteers, $2,400 1000 was appropriated, the claims to be also settled by foe third auditor, and the prices
allowed to be, as nearly as possible, the same as had been paid for supplies to the regular forces of the United
States; horses and other property lost or destroyed in the military service to be settled for on the principles
laid do~n in. previous acps.

The Secretary of the Treasury was auth~rized, if be deemed it expedient, to issue to t~e c.laimants, or their
:representatives, in payment of their audited claims, bonds of the United States of. a denommat10n not less than
:$50, redeemable in twenty years, and bearing interest at the rate of 6 per cent., with coupons attached, payable
..annually or semi-annually, at the discretion of the Secretary.
Under this act bonds to the amount of $1,090,850 were issued.
LOAN OF JULY AND AUGUST, 1861-0LD DEMAND NOTES-SEVEN-THIRTIES OF 1861.
The extra session of Congress which began July 4, 1861, was held under very different circumstances from
those which had accompanied any preceding session. Eleven states had attempted to secede from the Union, and
were in actual rebellion. Seventy.five thousand volunteers had been called into the field to s~1ppress the unlawful
combination, and Congress met in a military camp.
The Secretary of the Treasury, Salmon P. Chase, in his report on the finances, dated July 4, 1861, estimatocl
the expenditures for the :fiscal ;rear 1862 at $318,519 1581 87, and this sum, vast as it seemed, was, as events proved,
more than $240,ooo,ooo less than the actual expenditures.
The President in his message to Congress at the beginning of the extra session, had asked anthority to borrow
.$400,000,000, and 'this amount, added to the whole revenues of the government, was more than one hundrecl
millions short of the actual expenditures.
A bill to meet the expenses of the war, by obtaining a loan of $250,00o,ooo, was introduced in the House July
9, 1861, and considered in committee of the whole the next clay. Debate on the bill was limited to one hour, and
this hour was entirely occupied by one member. At the conclusion of his remarks the bill was passed by yeas 150,
nays 5.
·
The bill passed the Senate without much debate, but with many amendments, which were concurred in by
the House, and was approved July 17, 1861 (12 Statutes, 259.) It authorized the Secretary of the Treasury to
borrow, on the credit of the United States, within twelve months from the passage of the act, a sum not exceeding
$250,ooo,ooo, for which he was authorized to issue coupon or registered bonds, or treasury notes, in such propor·
tions of each as he might deem advisable, the bonds to bear interest not exceeding 7 per cent. per annum, payable
semi-annually, and redeemable, at the pleasure of the United States, aftertwenty years. The treasury notes to be
of any denomination :fixed by the Secretary, not less than $50, payable three years after date, with interest at the
rate of 7-fci per cent. per annum, payable semi-annually.
The Secretary was also authorized to issue treasury notes of a less denomination than $50 and not less than
$10, bearing interest at the rate of 3.65 per cent. per annum, payable in one year from date, and exchangeable at
any time for treasury notes of $50 and upward, bearing interest as specified above ; also, to issue treasury notes
of less tban :fifty and not less than ten dollars, not bearing interest, but payable on demand; but the whole
amount of such notes issued was not to exceed $50,000,000.
The Secretary was further authorized to issue, whenever he should deem it expedient, treasury notes of a.ny of'
the denominations before specified, bearing interest not exceeding 6 per cent., payable at any time not exceeding
twelve.months after date, but the whole amount of notes so issued was not to exceecl $20,000,000. ·
Notes of less than $50, when redeemed, might be reissued or canceled and new notes issued in their stead,
but the aggregate amount of bonds and notes issued under previous sections of the act was never to exceed
$250,000,ooo, and the power to issue or reissue the treasury notes was to cease December 31, 1862.
A bill supplemental to the above act was introduced in the Senate July 22, and passed both houses with but
little debate, though some opposition was made to the section which allowed the Secretary of the Treasury to fix
the denomination of the treasury notes at any sum below $50. The limit was finally fixed at $5, and the bill
passed and was approved August 5, 1861 (12 Statutes, 313).
·
.
It authorized the Secretary to issue a part of the bonds provided for by the act of July 17, 1861, at 6"per cent.
per annum, these bonds to be exchangeable for treasury notes bearing interest at 7-fo per cent., but no such bond
was to be issuecl for less than $500, nor was the whole amount of such bonds to exceecl the whole amount of treasury
notes bearing 71\ per cent. interest issued under the said act.
The Secretary was also authorized to issue treasury notes, not bearing interest, of denominations as low as five
dollars. The act of February 12, 1862 (12 Statutes, 338), authorized the issue of $10 000 000 in demand notes in
addition to the $50,000,000 authorized by the acts of July 17 and August 5, 1861.

. When it is con~idere.d .that. only a year before a loan of $~1,oo0,000 had been asked for, and only about oueth1rd that sum obtamed, it is evident that the danger which menaced the Union had aroused the people and induced

The disasters of the early part of the war were really.a gain instead of a loss as they convinced the North of

FIVE-TWENTIES OF 1862-TEMPORARY LOAN.

LEGAL'.TENDER NOTES.
The Secretary of the Treasury, at ~he beginning of the extra session of Congress, July 4, 1861, had estimated
the t•xpenses during the fiscal year 1862 at $318,519,58187. In his report on the :finances, December O, 1861, owiug
to the unexpected proportions which the war had assumed, and the extraordinary increase in tho army and. mwy
which hail become necessary, he was obliged to revise his :figures. He estimated, at that date, the expenditures
duriug the year at $.543,406,422 06, of which sum $213 1904,427 68 remained to be provided, for which recourse nrnHt
be Juul to loans. The result proved that even the increased figures were more than $20,000,000 too low.
""
A bill to authorize the issue of $100,000 1000 in treasury notes, and to fund the outstanding deb~ aucl. the floating
debt. by the issue of $500,000,000 in bonds, was introduced in the House January 22, 1862. The first section of tlrn
hill, ;s lntroduceu-tliough subsequently amended-provided that there should be printed on tho back of the notes
the following words:*
The within note is a legal tender in payment of all debts, public and private, and is exchangeable for bonds of tho Uuito<l. Stiite!l
bearing' 6 per cent. interest.

This legal-tender clause developed opposition in both houses of Congress, even among supporters of tho
administration. It "Was well said in debate that this was the :first time it had been proposed in the Cougress of
the United States to make anything but gold and silver a legal tender. The question has been raised and disputed
a!l to whether what was ca11ed the ''continental currency", issued during the war of the Revolution by the old
govemment, was or was not legal tender. The facts appear to be that while the Oontineutal Congress ·did not by
any ordinance attempt to gi-ve it that character, they asked the states to do so, and all seem to have complied
exc0pt lthode Island. The Continental Congress or<ly enacted that the man who refused to take the money should
1Je deemed an enemy of his country. George Washington complained, in one of his letters, that he lu:t(l beou ol>liged
to receive the currency at imr, iu.imyment of a debt, when it had so depreciated that its value wi.1s almost nominnl.
The opr1ouents of the clause nuder which the first issue of' foe present legal-tender currency was made, said that
such au issue woulcl lie, in effect, to aid and assist in.en who owed debts to pay them with a depreciated emTe1icy, at
par, at the cost and expense of the creditor, and that it was therefore clearly a proposal t0 interfere with exi1:1ting
contracts; that the government was abont to go before the community and ask for CI'.edit on the plighted public
faith, arnl it was asked on what the public faith rested¥ Evidently on the belief that the government appreciated
the inviolability of contracts. And yet, it was said, the very bill in which it was proposed to appeal for credit for
a -vast sum propo::;etl to destroy this inviolability.
Those who farnred the bill do not seem to have found. any very strenuous arguments in favor of the leg·nJtender clause, per se, but made the plea that it was a uecessity, a war measure, justified as any other step might ba
justified which had become necessary, in order that the nation might live.
The words of Secretary Chase on this subject show how the matter was regarded by the administration :t
The proviHion making Unitell States notes a legal tender has cloubtless been well considerecl by the committee, and their conclusion

needs 110 support from any observation of mine. I think it my duty, however, to say, that in respect to this provision my reflections ha vo
conducted me to the same conclusions they have reached. It is not unknown to them that I have felt, nor do I wish to conceal that J uow
feel, a great aversion to making anything but coin a legal tencler in payment of debts. It has been my anxious wish to a.voicl tho necossil;y
of such legislation. It is, however, at present impossible, in consequence of the large expendiimres entailed by the war ancl thQ suspcmsio;1
of the _banks, to l~roeure sufficient coin for disbursements; and it has, therefore, become indispensably necessary that we should resort
to tlui Illsne of }Jmted Stat~s notes. Th~ making them a legal tender might, however, still be avoided if the willingness manifested by
the people generally, by ~ailroad ~ompames, and by many of the banking institutions, to receive ancl pay them as money in all transactions
were absolutely or iiractically umversal, hut, unfortunately, thero are some persons and some institutions whioh rofnse to recuive an cl
pay. them, anil whose action tends not merely to the unnecessary depreciation of the notes but to establish cliscriminatious in business
agruD.llt ~hose ~ho, in this matter, give a cordial support to the government, auil in favo~ of those who do not. Such discriminations
shou!~· if pos!Ubl?, be prevented; and the provision making the notes a legal tender, in a great measure at least, prevents it by putting
all citizens, in this respect, on the same level, both of rights and duties,
'

hardly worth while for ua to plead any very remarkable degree of ignorance when nobody is competent to instruct us; and yet such ia the

faet. I can state to you, Mr. president, that on one day I Wllll advised very strongly by a leading financial man, at all events to oppose
this legal-tender clause"; he exclaimed agaiinst it with all the bittemess in the :world. On the very same day I recei..-ed a note from a.
friend of his, telling me that we coul:d not get along without it. I shoi\Ved it to him, and he e~pressed his utter surprise. He went home,
and next day telegraphed to me that he had changed his mind and now thought it was absolutely necessary.; and his friend who wrote to
me wrote again that he had changed his, and they were two of .the most eminent financial men in the country.

When two of the leading :fi.11.ancial luminaries cou1d thus find themselves on both sides of so important a.
question within a few hours, it may be doubted whether enough fixed principles in finance have been discovered to
entitle it to be called a science.
It is very evident that the issue of so large an amount of noites, without the legal-tender clause, must have
(}ansed them to depreciate much more than they did, and that with specie payments suspended by all the banks,
and the coin of the country locked up, or in the bands of speculators, the distress must have been much increased by
the fact t,hat creditors could have demanded payment, not i11. the common currency, but in gold and silver, which
eould only have been obtained at exorbitant prices.
Several amendments to the original bill were adopted, including a modification of the legal-tender clause, and
the amount of notes of that character to be issued was increased to $150,000,000.
Tlle bill was approved February 25, 1802 (12 Statutes, 345). It authorized the Secretary of tlie Treasury to issue,
on the credit of the United States, treasury notes to the amount of $150,000 1000, bearing no interest and payable to
bearer, of such denominations as he might deem expedient, not less than five dollars each. Fifty millions of these
notes to be in lieu of the ''demand notes" issued under the act of July 17, 1801, whick latter were to be taken up
as rapidly as practicable, and the n.otes authorizetl by this act substituted therefor.
Tlle amount of the two kinds of notes together was at no time to exceed $150,000 1000. These notes to be
"receivable in payment of all taxes, internal duties, excises, debts, ancl demands of every kind due to the United
States, except duties on imports, ancl of all claims and demamls against the United States, of every kind whatsoever,
except for interest upon bonds and notes, which shall be paicl in coin, and shall also be lawful money and a legal
tender in payment of all debts, public and private, wi:thin the United States, except duties on imports and interest
as aforesaid".
They were to be exchangea,ble in sums of $50, or any multiple thereof, for bonds of the United States, bearing
interest at the rate of 6 per cent., payable semi-annually, the bonds to be redeemable at the l)leasure of the United
States after five years, and payable twenty years after date. The notes were made receivable the same as coin, at
their lmr value, for any lmms that might thereafter be sold or negotiated by t)?.e Secretary of the Treasury, imd
when receivecl for such loans or for public dues might be reissued from time to time as the exigencies of the
.government might require.
The act of July 11, 1862 (12 Statutes, 532), authorized an additional issue of $150 1000,000 in legal-tender notes of
such denominations as the Secretary of the Treasury might deem expedient, but no note was to be for the fractional
part Gf a uolfar, and not more than $35,000,000 of lower denominations th~tn $5. The act of l\Iarch 3, 1803 (12
Statutes, 7H), authorizecl another issue of $150,000,000 in legn1-teuder notes, including the amount authorized. by
resolution of January 17, 1803 (12 Statutes, 822), of such denominations not less than one dollar as the Secretary
might prescribe.
This act ahlo limited the time at which treasury notes might be exchanged for United States bonds to July 1,
1803.

Undar these acts legal-tender notes have been issued amounting in the aggregate to $1,040,559,947. The
difference between the amount authorized and the amount "issued is accounted for. by reissues from time to time, as
authorized by law.
CERTIFICATES OF INDEBTEDNESS.
.A bill to allow the issue of certificates for the amount due on audited and settled accounts was introduced iu
the Senate February 27, passed both houses without debat~, and was approved March 1, 1862 (12 Statutes, 352).
It authorized the Secretary of the Treasury to issue, to any public creditor who might desire to receive the same,
certificates for the whole amount due, or parts thereof, not less than $1,000, in satisfaction of audited and settled
accounts. These certificates were to bear 6 per cent. interest per annum, and were payable one year from date or
earlier at the option of the government. The act of March 17, 1802 (12 Statutes, 370), authorized the issue of these
certificates to such public creditors as might desire to receive them, in payment of disbursing officers' checks drawn
ora the treasurer of the United States. The act of l\farch 31 1803 (12 Statutes, 710), made the interest payable in
lawful money. Under these acts certificates of indebtedness amounting to $561,753,241 05 have been issued.
